# Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
<note>original: 四庫全書 (Siku Quanshu). This was the largest collection of books in Chinese history, commissioned by the Qianlong Emperor of the Qing dynasty in 1772 to categorize and preserve the empire's vast literary output.</note>

## The Masters Section
<note>original: 子部 (Zibu). This is the third of the four traditional divisions of Chinese literature. While the first two sections cover Confucian classics and history, the "Masters" section includes a wide array of works on philosophy, military strategy, medicine, astronomy, and the arts.</note>

<term>Complete Library of the Four Treasuries</term>, <term>Masters Section</term>, Siku Quanshu

<summary>This title page identifies a volume from the Siku Quanshu, the definitive 18th-century imperial encyclopedia of China. This specific volume belongs to the "Masters" category, which houses philosophical, scientific, and technical writings.</summary>
<keywords>Siku Quanshu, Masters Section, Imperial Library, Qing Dynasty, Chinese Bibliography</keywords>

->Volume 10 Table of Contents: Compendium of Materia Medica<-

# By Imperial Decree: Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

## Table of Contents for Volume 10 of the Compendium of Materia Medica

### Category of Metals and Stones, Part Four: Minerals (Lower Section), Thirty-Nine Varieties <note>The "Lower Section" (下) usually refers to the final part of a larger category. In early Chinese medical classification, "lower class" minerals were often those specifically valued for their powerful medicinal properties in treating illness rather than for long-term health cultivation.</note>

Actinolite <gloss>The Divine Farmer's Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 陽起石 (Yangqishi). A silicate mineral. The name literally means "stone that raises the yang," reflecting its traditional use in warming the kidneys and treating exhaustion.</note>

Magnetite <gloss>The Divine Farmer's Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 慈石 (Cishi). Literally "the compassionate stone" or "affectionate stone," so named because it attracts iron like a mother attracting her children. Today known as Loadstone or Magnetite.</note>

Black Stone <gloss>Supplementary Records of Famous Physicians</gloss> <note>original: 玄石 (Xuanshi). A variety of dark magnetite or related mineral. The "Supplementary Records" (Bielu) is a major historical source for medicinal knowledge from the 3rd to 6th centuries.</note>

Hematite <gloss>The Divine Farmer's Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 代赭石 (Daizheshi). Red iron ore. It was historically used in powder form to "anchor" the body's energy and treat conditions like dizziness or coughing up blood.</note>

Limonite <gloss>The Divine Farmer's Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 禹餘糧 (Yuyuliang). Literally "Yu’s Surplus Grain." Legend says that when the Great Yu (the legendary tamer of floods) finished his work, he threw leftover grain into the mountains and it turned into these stone nodules.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the table of contents for the tenth volume of the Compendium of Materia Medica, focusing on the fourth section of minerals and stones. It lists various medicinal minerals like magnetite and hematite, citing their original appearances in classic texts like the Divine Farmer's Materia Medica.</summary>

<keywords>Table of Contents, Compendium of Materia Medica, Metals and Stones, Actinolite, Magnetite, Hematite, Limonite, Divine Farmer's Materia Medica</keywords>

# Volume 10 Table of Contents

Taiyi's Surplus Grain <gloss>The Divine Farmer’s Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 太一餘粮 (Taiyi yuliang). A type of iron-rich mineral geode, likely limonite. In Chinese mythology, Taiyi is the "Great One" or a supreme celestial deity; these stones were poetically seen as the leftover food of the gods.</note>

Yellow Kernel in the Stone <gloss>Tang Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 石中黃子 (Shizhong huangzi). This refers to the soft, yellow ochre-like core found inside certain mineral nodules.</note>

Hollow Azurite <gloss>The Divine Farmer’s Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 空青 (Kongqing). A high-grade variety of azurite (copper carbonate) that is naturally hollow or contains a small amount of liquid.</note>

Layered Azurite <gloss>The Divine Farmer’s Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 曾青 (Zengqing). Literally "cumulative green," referring to azurite that has formed in distinct layers or strata.</note>

Green Azurite <gloss>The Divine Farmer’s Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 綠青 (Lüqing). A term often used for malachite, the green counterpart to the blue azurite minerals.</note>

Flat Azurite <gloss>The Divine Farmer’s Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: 扁青 (Bianqing). A variety of azurite that occurs in flat, plate-like crystalline structures.</note>

White Azurite <gloss>The Divine Farmer’s Materia Medica; Green-Skin Azurite and Blue-Green Stone Azurite are appended</gloss> <note>original: 白青 (Baiqing). Likely a pale or weathered form of copper ore. The appended items (綠膚青, 碧石青) represent further variations in color and texture.</note>

Chalcantite <gloss>The Divine Farmer’s Materia Medica; also known as Bile Alum</gloss> <note>original: 石膽 (Shidan). Literally "Stone Gallbladder." It is copper sulfate (blue vitriol), named for its intense blue color and bitter, "bile-like" properties. The gloss identifies it by its common name, 膽礬 (Danfan).</note>

<summary>This page lists several mineral medicines, specifically focusing on iron-based "grains" and various forms of copper-based minerals like azurite and malachite. These were valued in ancient pharmacy for both their external pigments and internal medicinal properties.</summary>

# Volume Three: Minerals and Stones — Compendium of Materia Medica
## Page Two

### Mica Schist <gloss>Added during the Jiayou era (1056–1063)</gloss> <note>original: "Mengshi." This stone was traditionally ground into a fine powder to treat "stubborn phlegm" and certain psychological disturbances.</note>

<column-break/>

### Posuo Stone <gloss>Added during the Kaibao era (968–976)</gloss> <note>This name likely refers to a mineral bezoar or a specific type of malachite; it was historically used as an antidote to poisons and to treat eye diseases.</note>

<column-break/>

### Gold-star Stone <gloss>Added during the Jiayou era</gloss> <gloss>Attached to the Mineral section</gloss> <note>original: "Jinxingshi." Often identified as Aventurine or quartz with glittering inclusions; used both for its beauty and for its purported "cooling" medicinal properties.</note>

<column-break/>

### Earth-Yellow <gloss>From the Compendium (Gangmu)</gloss> <note>original: "Tuhuang." A type of yellow-colored mineral earth or limonite used in topical applications for sores and detoxification.</note>

<column-break/>

### Arsenic Stone <gloss>Added during the Kaibao era</gloss> <note>original: "Pishi." A general term for arsenic ores like arsenopyrite. Though highly toxic, it was used in controlled, minute amounts to treat chronic malaria and severe skin infections.</note>

<column-break/>

### Snow-Clutching Arsenic Stone <gloss>From the Tang Dynasty Herbal</gloss> <note>original: "Woxue Yushi." A legendary variety of arsenic ore said to be so "hot" in nature that snow would not settle on the ground above its mineral vein.</note>

<column-break/>

### Naturally Occurring Arsenic Stone <gloss>From the Supplementary Records (Mingyi Bielu)</gloss> <note>original: "Tesheng Yushi." Refers to high-quality, distinct crystals of arsenic ore found in isolation rather than embedded in common rock.</note>

<column-break/>

### Arsenopyrite <gloss>From the Classic Herbal (Shennong Bencao Jing)</gloss> <note>original: "Yushi." One of the most ancient minerals in Chinese medicine, recognized since antiquity for its potent, heat-inducing properties and toxicity.</note>

# Catalog of the Minerals and Stones Section: Volume Ten

### Flowered Milk Stone <gloss>Added during the Jiayou era (1056–1063)</gloss> <note>original: "Huarushi." This is a variegated mineral, often identified as ophicalcite or a type of marble with serpentine inclusions. It was highly valued in traditional medicine for its ability to stop bleeding and promote the healing of wounds.</note>

### White Sheep Stone <gloss>From the Illustrated Classics</gloss> <note>original: "Baiyangshi." A white mineral described in historical texts as resembling sheep's fat. It was typically used in topical applications for skin ailments.</note>

### Golden Tooth Stone <gloss>From the Supplementary Records of Famous Physicians</gloss> <note>original: "Jinyashi." Identified as iron pyrite or chalcopyrite (copper pyrites). Because of its metallic, golden appearance, it was associated with "strengthening the bones" and treating "wind-dampness" (joint pain).</note>

### Diamond <gloss>From the Compendium of Materia Medica; Stone Tea is attached</gloss> <note>original: "Jingangshi." While recognized for its extreme hardness, it was rarely consumed; "Stone Tea" (Shiming) refers to a mineral substance, likely a type of weathered rock or lichen, used as a cooling infusion.</note>

### Whetstone of Yue <gloss>From the Supplementary Records; commonly known as a grindstone</gloss> <note>original: "Yuedishi." High-quality abrasive stones sourced from the ancient Yue region (modern Zhejiang). In medicine, the fine slurry produced by grinding these stones was sometimes used to treat sores.</note>

### Ginger Stone <gloss>From the Tang Dynasty Herbal</gloss> <note>original: "Jiangshi." These are calcareous concretions found in loess soil that naturally resemble the gnarled shape of ginger roots. They were often used to treat "heat-toxins" and swellings.</note>

### Barley-Rice Stone <gloss>From the Illustrated Classics</gloss> <note>original: "Maifanshi." Named for its appearance—speckled like barley or rice grains embedded in a bun. Today it is widely used for water filtration, but historically it was applied to treat skin ulcers and abscesses.</note>

### White River Stone <gloss>From the Supplement to Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: "Shuizhong Baishi," literally "White stone from within water." Usually refers to smooth, white quartz pebbles found in stream beds, used for their perceived "cooling" properties in decoctions.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a table of contents for Volume Ten of the Minerals and Stones section, listing eight specific mineral substances and their primary historical bibliographic sources.</summary>
<keywords>Huarushi, Jinyashi, Diamond, Maifan Stone, Ginger Stone, pharmacological bibliography, mineral medicine</keywords>

<meta>Continuing the Table of Contents for the Minerals and Stones section of the Compendium of Materia Medica. This page lists various geological substances and fossils, citing their primary historical sources ranging from the Tang Dynasty (7th century) to the Ming Dynasty (16th century).</meta>

# Compendium of Materia Medica
### Page Three

River Sand <gloss>Supplement</gloss> <note>original: "Hesha." Refers to common river sand. In traditional practice, it was often heated and used as a localized "hot pack" to relieve rheumatic pain or used as a heat-transfer medium for roasting other medicinal ingredients.</note>

<column-break/>

Ladle Sand <gloss>Compendium</gloss> <note>original: "Shaoshangsha." This refers to the fine sediment or grit found adhering to water ladles or dippers. Li Shizhen included it in his Compendium for its specific traditional use in treating sudden physical obstructions or eye irritants.</note>

<column-break/>

Stone Swallows <gloss>Tang Dynasty Herbal</gloss> <note>original: "Shiyan." These are actually fossilized brachiopods. Because their shape resembles a swallow with spread wings, they were named "Stone Swallows." They were often ground into powder to treat urinary issues or eye films.</note>

<column-break/>

Stone Crabs <gloss>Kaibao Era Herbal</gloss> <note>original: "Shixie." These are fossilized crabs, often found along the coast. Historically, they were pulverized and used to treat "heat" in the eyes and as an antidote for various toxins.</note>

<column-break/>

Stone Snakes <gloss>Illustrated Classics</gloss> <note>original: "Shishe." These fossils are typically coiled ammonites or similar prehistoric marine life. Ancient naturalists believed they were petrified snakes and used them to treat skin diseases and neutralize venom.</note>

<column-break/>

Stone Silkworms <gloss>Kaibao Era Herbal</gloss> <note>original: "Shican." These are often the fossilized stems of crinoids or the protective cases of caddisfly larvae. They were primarily used in formulas meant to dissolve kidney stones and treat urinary blockages.</note>

<column-break/>

Stone Turtles <gloss>Compendium</gloss> <note>original: "Shibie." Likely referring to fossilized mollusks or the polyplacophorans (chitons). They were used in traditional formulas to treat "lumps" or hard masses in the abdomen.</note>

<column-break/>

Snake Yellow <gloss>Tang Dynasty Herbal</gloss> <note>original: "Shehuang." These are geologically known as limonite concretions or nodules found in the soil. Their name comes from a folk belief that they were formed from the breath or eggs of snakes. They were used to treat tremors, fright, and bone fractures.</note>

<summary>This page lists several mineral and fossilized substances used in traditional medicine, including fossilized swallows, crabs, and "snake yellow" nodules. It highlights the ancient practice of using paleontological remains (fossils) to treat specific ailments based on their appearance and elemental properties.</summary>
<keywords>Bencao Gangmu, fossils, 石燕 (Stone Swallows), 石蟹 (Stone Crabs), medicinal minerals, paleontology in medicine, traditional Chinese pharmacy</keywords>

->Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections, Volume 10 Table of Contents<-

Thunderbolt Anvil <gloss>Supplement</gloss> <note>original: "Pilizhen." These were actually Neolithic stone tools, such as polished stone axes or adzes, discovered in the soil. Ancient observers believed these objects fell from the sky during thunderstorms. In traditional medicine, they were ground into powder and used to treat extreme fright or to help with difficult labors.</note>

Thunder Ink <gloss>Compendium</gloss> <note>original: "Leimo." A black, mineral-like substance found in the earth following a lightning strike, possibly fossilized resin or charcoal. It was historically used to treat sudden ailments like fainting or intense abdominal pain, believed to carry the "cleansing" power of thunder.</note>

To the right <note>In the original vertical Chinese layout, "to the right" refers to the text already read—in this case, the entries preceding this statement</note> are twenty-five old formulas and ninety-five new ones.

# Volume 10 of the Compendium of Materia Medica

->Written by Li Shizhen of the Ming Dynasty<-

## Metals and Minerals, Part 4 <gloss>Stones (Lower Category), 39 Varieties</gloss>

### Tremolite <gloss>Medium Grade in the Divine Husbandman's Classic of Materia Medica</gloss> <note>original: "Yangqi Shi." This mineral, typically actinolite or tremolite asbestos, was prized in traditional medicine for its "warming" properties. It was famously used to treat conditions associated with "kidney yang deficiency," such as impotence or infertility.</note>

**Explanation of Names** Sheep-Rising Stone <gloss>Miscellaneous Records</gloss> <note>original: "Yangqi Shi." While the pronunciation is identical to Tremolite, this version uses the character for "sheep."</note>, White Stone <gloss>Divine Husbandman's Classic</gloss>, Stone Life <gloss>Shizhen says: It is named for its functional ability to "raise" or revitalize the body's vital energy.</gloss>

**Collected Explanations** <gloss>The *Miscellaneous Records* state: Tremolite is produced in the valleys of Mount Qi and Langya. Some say it is the root of mica <note>Mica was a central substance in Daoist alchemy, believed to grant longevity.</note>. It can be harvested at any time. Wu Pu <note>A 3rd-century physician and student of the famous doctor Hua Tuo.</note> says: It is produced at Mount Tai. Tao Hongjing <note>A prominent 5th-century alchemist and physician who reorganized the materia medica.</note> says: This substance comes from the same source as mica and looks very much like it, though it differs in thickness. What is used today comes from Yizhou, found in the same locations as arsenopyrite. It is slightly yellowish-black. However, regarding the "roots" of arsenopyrite and mica, it is unclear which one is the true source. Its common use is rare, though the scriptures of the Immortals <note>Referring to Daoist texts on alchemy and transcendence.</note> describe consuming it. Su Gong <note>The lead editor of the Tang Dynasty's official materia medica.</note> says: This...</gloss>

<summary>This page introduces Tremolite (Actinolite), a mineral historically used for its revitalizing properties. The text discusses its various names and the geological confusion among early physicians regarding its relationship to mica and arsenopyrite.</summary>

<keywords>Tremolite, Actinolite, Li Shizhen, Bencao Gangmu, Mineral Medicine, Mica, Tao Hongjing, Daoist Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed entry for Tremolite (Yangqi Shi) within the "Stones" section of Volume 10. The previous page established the mineral's name and its general classification. This page focuses on identifying high-quality specimens, correcting historical geographical errors, and describing the unique "warming" properties observed at its primary source in Qizhou.</meta>

# Volume 10

Stones with a white color and a texture resembling a ram’s fleece <gloss>original: *gu*, referring to the thick, fibrous coat of a male sheep</gloss>, which still contain traces of mica and appear moist, are considered of superior quality. Therefore, the *Divine Husbandman's Classic of Materia Medica* gives it the name "White Stone." The current practice of using stones that are as purely black as charcoal is a mistake. Black mica is known as "Cloud Gall" <note>original: *Yundan*</note>; consuming it is harmful to the human body. By extension, black Tremolite must also be of poor and harmful quality.

Currently, Mount Qi is located west of Qizhou <note>Modern-day Jinan, Shandong Province</note>, but it does not produce Tremolite. The stone is actually produced at Mount Lu, which is six or seven miles northwest of Mount Qi. The mention of "Mount Qi" in the *Classic* may be due to the character for "Qi" being a scribal error for "Lu." 

Regarding the regions of Mount Tai and Yizhou, they only produce the black variety. The white variety comes exclusively from Qizhou. Li Xun says: "The yellow variety produced at Mount Tai is of excellent quality. The white variety from Mount He in Xingzhou is also good." 

Su Song says: "Today, it is only produced in Qizhou; it is no longer found in other places. In Qizhou, there is a single earthen hill where the stone is found. The local people call it 'Tremolite Mountain.' That mountain always possesses a warm aura (*qi*). Even in the depth of winter when heavy snow covers the entire region, this mountain alone has no accumulation of white snow. This is because the 'breath' of the stone steams and evaporates the snow. There is only one mining cave on the mountain, which the government keeps strictly sealed. At the beginning of winter, the prefecture drafts laborers and sends officials to oversee the extraction. Over many years, the cave has grown deeper, and the mineral is mixed with other stones, making it very difficult to obtain. Those that are white, bright, and translucent like 'wolf fangs' are considered top grade. Some specimens are clumped together with other stones and are unusable. Each year, after the finest pieces are selected for the imperial tribute, the remainder is sold within the prefecture; otherwise, there would be no way to obtain it. Although there are many sellers, high-quality specimens are truly hard to find. Ancient accounts say this stone is the 'root of mica' and that it still contains mica, though this is no longer seen today."

Ancient prescriptions for longevity and internal cultivation did not typically use this stone, but modern restorative medicines for the "lower body" <note>The "Lower Burner," specifically referring to kidney and reproductive health.</note> find it very useful. 

Shizhen says: "Nowadays, those with 'cloud heads and rain feet' <note>A poetic description of the mineral's fibrous, tapering texture, resembling rain falling from a cloud.</note> that are light and loose like wolf fangs are considered the best. Those that are matted like felt or have sharp, protruding corners are of inferior quality." 

Wang Jianping’s *Methods of the Classics* <note>original: *Dianshu*</note> states: "Those that are yellow-white with a reddish tint and are heavy and thick are the best; they are the roots of mica." The *Gengxin Jade Register* <note>A famous alchemical text concerning the transformation of minerals.</note> says:

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of high-quality Tremolite, noting its "warm" energy which prevents snow from settling on its source mountain. It also provides a detailed account of the government-controlled mining process in Qizhou and clarifies the mineral's relationship to mica.</summary>
<keywords>Tremolite, Yangqi Shi, Qizhou, mineralogy, Kidney Yang, Li Shizhen, mica, mining history, Mount Lu, traditional pharmacology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of mineral-based medicines, specifically focusing on stones believed to possess "Yang" or warming properties for the body.</meta>

# Actinolite

Actinolite <note>The Chinese name "Yangqishi" literally translates to "the stone that raises the Sun," referring to its reputation for treating impotence.</note>: Those produced at Jianjin Mountain in Qizhou are superior. Those with sharp tips resembling arrowheads are potent; those shaped like dog teeth are weak. If placed in deep snow and the snow suddenly melts away, the stone is genuine.

---

### Preparation

**Da Ming** <note>A 10th-century pharmaceutical author.</note> says: For medicinal use, it should be burned and then quenched in water. The variety that becomes a congealed white color is best. 
**Li Shizhen** <note>The primary author of this compendium (1518–1593).</note> says: Generally, it should be heated in a fire until glowing red, then quenched in wine seven times. It is then ground into a fine powder, refined through water levigation <note>A process of separating fine powder from grit by stirring it in water and collecting the suspended particles.</note>, and dried in the sun. 
There is also a method where it is soaked in distilled spirits and placed in a jar with camphor to be sublimated into a powder for use.

---

### Nature and Flavor

Salty, slightly warm, and non-toxic.
<gloss>The Emperor Shennong and the physician Bian Que say: Sour and non-toxic. Tongjun, Lei Gong, and Qi Bo say: Salty and non-toxic. Li Dangzhi says: Slightly cold. Zhen Quan says: Sweet and neutral.</gloss>
<gloss>Xu Zhicai says: Mantis egg-case serves as its "messenger" (enhancer). It is incompatible with Alisma, Cinnamon, Omphalia, and Snake slough. It "fears" (is weakened by) Dodder seed. Avoid sheep blood. Do not include it in medicinal soups (decoctions).</gloss>

---

### Main Indications

Treats uterine bleeding, vaginal discharge, and breaks up blood masses and accumulations <term>Zhengjia: abdominal masses or tumors often associated with stagnant blood or energy</term> in the uterus. It treats chills and fever, abdominal pain, infertility, and impotence, while tonifying deficiencies in the body.
<gloss>From the Divine Farmer’s Classic of Materia Medica:</gloss> It treats coldness in the glans, dampness and itching under the scrotum, removes foul-smelling sweat, and resolves edema. Long-term consumption prevents aging and enables a person to have children.
<gloss>From the Miscellaneous Records of Famous Physicians:</gloss> It tonifies the Kidney Qi <note>In Chinese medicine, "Kidney Qi" is the fundamental energy for growth and reproduction.</note> and treats sperm deficiency, lower back pain, and coldness or dampness in the knees.

<summary>This page describes the mineral Actinolite (Yangqishi), detailing its physical identification, the complex process of quenching it in wine for medicinal use, and its primary role in treating reproductive and "Kidney" related ailments.</summary>
<keywords>Actinolite, Yangqishi, Li Shizhen, Kidney Qi, infertility, impotence, medicinal preparation, mineral medicine, traditional Chinese medicine</keywords>

Minerals Section, Volume 10

...numbness, long-standing coldness of the uterus, and "cold masses" <term>Lengjia; abdominal masses believed to be caused by stagnant cold energy</term> or "winter masses," and regulates irregular menstrual cycles. <gloss>Zhen Quan says: It treats vaginal discharge and epidemic pestilence, relieves "cold Qi," and restores the body from the "Five Exhaustions and Seven Injuries" <note>A traditional categorization of physical and emotional overexertion leading to chronic fatigue.</note>.</gloss> <gloss>Da Ming says: It tonifies deficiencies in the "Gate of Vitality" <note>Mingmen; a vital energy center located between the kidneys, considered the root of the body's fire.</note>.</gloss> <gloss>Wang Haogu says: It disperses various swellings caused by heat.</gloss> <gloss>Li Shizhen says: [This entry continues below].</gloss>

# Analysis of the Properties

<gloss>Kou Zongshi says:</gloss> For men and women experiencing deficiency-cold in the lower body, exhaustion of kidney energy, or a chronically cold womb, this mineral must be refined through water levigation <note>original: "shuifei"; a process of grinding a mineral in water to obtain a pure, microscopic powder while removing grit</note> before use. Generally, mineral-based medicines, whether their nature is cooling or heating, contain innate toxins; their use must be carefully weighed and moderated.

<gloss>Li Shizhen says:</gloss> Actinolite is a medicine that acts upon the "Qi division" of the right kidney and the Gate of Vitality. It is suitable for those suffering from "deficiency-cold" in the "Lower Burner" <note>The lower abdominal region containing the reproductive and excretory organs.</note>. However, it is not a substance meant for long-term consumption.

<gloss>Zhang Zihe says in "Confucian Duties to Their Parents":</gloss> "Throat blockage" <note>original: "houbi"; a painful, acute swelling of the throat</note> is a disease caused by a sudden flare-up of "Minister Fire" <note>Xianghuo; a secondary heat energy in the body that can become pathologically intense</note>. Minister Fire is a form of "Dragon Fire"; it is appropriate to use fire to drive out fire <note>A therapeutic principle of using a hot-natured medicine to treat a "fire" disease, essentially "guiding" the heat out of the body.</note>. A man once suffered from "entwined throat wind" <note>A severe, restrictive swelling of the neck and throat.</note> where the swelling was so great that no medicine—internal or external—could be swallowed. Cooling medicines were instead administered through his nose, which induced over ten bowel movements. Externally, Actinolite was roasted until glowing red and mixed with an equal part of "Hidden Dragon Liver" <note>original: "fulonggan"; medicinal clay taken from the center of an old wood-burning stove</note>, then ground into a fine powder. This was mixed with freshly drawn water and applied to the throat a hundred times a day. After three days, the intensity began to recede and the swelling finally dissipated. This is an example of the "method of harmony" <note>Aligning the treatment with the specific nature of the pathological fire.</note>.

**Deficiency-cold of the Original Energy:** <gloss>Used for uncontrollable seminal emission and spermatorrhea.</gloss>

For chronic diarrhea and loose stools from the large intestine, accompanied by coldness in the hands and feet: Take equal parts calcined and powdered Actinolite and Stalactite powder. Mix with the powder of Aconite <note>original: "fuzi"; the processed lateral root of monkshood, used to warm the body's internal fire</note> that has been boiled in wine. Take fifty pills on an empty stomach with rice water, continuing until the condition is cured. <margin>Formulas for Aiding Life (Jisheng Fang)</margin>

# Magnetite
->Classified as a Middle-Grade Substance in the Divine Husbandman's Classic of Materia Medica<-

### Names and Etymology
**Black Stone** (Xuanshi) <gloss>Divine Husbandman's Classic (Benjing)</gloss> **Dwelling Stone** (Chushi) <gloss>Miscellaneous Records (Bielu)</gloss> **Iron-Attracting Stone** (Xietieshi) <gloss>Augmented Meaning (Yanyi)</gloss> **Needle-Attracting Stone** (Xizhenshi)
<gloss>Chen Cangqi says: Magnetite attracts iron just as a compassionate mother calls to her children; therefore, it is named "Compassionate Stone" [Cishi]. Li Shizhen says: Stones that lack this "compassion" cannot attract iron and are called "Black Stones." However, the *Bielu* lists Black Stone as a separate entry later in the text.</gloss>

### Collected Explanations
<gloss>The Miscellaneous Records (Bielu) says</gloss>: Magnetite is produced in the river valleys of Mount Tai and at Mount Ci. On the northern (shady) side of a mountain where iron is found, magnetite will be produced on the southern (sunny) side. It may be harvested at any time.

<gloss>Tao Hongjing says</gloss>: Nowadays, high-quality specimens are also found in the south. They can suspend iron needles, linking three or four together in the air. These are frequently used by alchemists in the "Yellow and White" arts <note>A traditional term for alchemy, specifically the transmutation of base metals into gold and silver.</note>.

<gloss>Chen Cangqi says</gloss>: It is produced in the northern mountains of Xiongzhou.

<gloss>Su Song says</gloss>: Today it is found in Cizhou, Xuzhou, and in the mountains along the southern coast. Those from Cizhou, sent as annual tribute, are of the highest quality; they can attract dozens of needles to hang in a line. Stones that can lift a knife or iron vessel weighing one or two *jin* <term>approx. 0.5 to 1 kilogram</term> and spin it without it falling are considered especially excellent. They may be harvested at any time. Within these stones, there are naturally occurring holes; those with holes... <margin>Compendium of Materia Medica (Bencao Gangmu)</margin>

# Minerals and Stones, Volume 10

Inside the stone, there is a yellow-red color, and its surface is covered with fine hairs; such specimens are superior in their medicinal efficacy. According to the *Records of Exotic Things of Nanchuan* <note>original: "Nanchuan Yi Wu Zhi"; a 3rd-century geography text</note>, the waters at Qitou in the Rising Sea <note>original: "Zhanghai"; an ancient name for the South China Sea</note> are shallow and abound with Magnetite. When large foreign ships reinforced with iron plates reach this area, they are all unable to pass. Based on this account, Magnetite is particularly abundant in the regions both north and south of the sea.

Duan <note>likely referring to the scholar Duan Chengshi</note> states: When using this substance, do not mistakenly use *Xuanzhongshi* or *Zhongmashi*. These two stones both resemble Magnetite but cannot attract iron. *Zhongmashi* has a red core and a coarse texture; it is merely stone from an iron-ore mountain. Accidental ingestion of these will cause a person to develop malignant sores that cannot be treated. 

True Magnetite is graded by its strength:
*   A piece that can attract one catty <term>approx. 600 grams</term> of iron from all four sides is named **Life-Extending Sand** (Yanniansha).
*   One that attracts eight ounces (liang) <term>approx. 300 grams</term> is named **Continuance-Gathering Stone** (Xucaishi).
*   One that attracts five ounces <term>approx. 187 grams</term> is named **Magnetite** (Cishi). 

The "hairs" on the stone are light purple and the surface of the stone is quite clear. It can attract iron pieces so they link together; thus, it is commonly called **Iron-Joining Stone** (Xietieshi). The "Black Stone" is simply the black variety of Magnetite.

When Magnetite is rubbed against the point of an iron needle, the needle is then able to point south. However, it often deviates slightly to the east rather than pointing directly south. 

**The Method of Construction:**
Take a single strand of new silk thread and use a piece of wax the size of half a mustard seed to attach it to the middle <gloss>the "waist"</gloss> of the needle. Suspend it in a place where there is no wind, and the iron will then always point south. One may also push a needle horizontally through a lamp wick and float it on water; this also points south, though it usually deviates toward the *Bing* position <note>the South-Southeast direction in the traditional Chinese compass</note>. This is because the *Bing* position corresponds to Great Fire, and the elements *Geng* and *Xin* <note>the metal elements of the needle</note> are restrained by it. This is simply the resonance of natural principles.

The Perfected Lord of the Earth Star <note>original: "Tu Su Zhen Jun"; an alchemical authority</note> says: Iron receives the *Qi* <term>vital energy</term> of the Great Sun. At the beginning of its life, stone is produced. After one hundred and fifty years, it matures into Magnetite. After another two hundred years of gestation, it finally becomes Iron.

<summary>This page describes the physical properties and grading of Magnetite, its use in early compasses, and the alchemical belief that minerals evolve over centuries from stone to metal.</summary>
<keywords>Magnetite, Cishi, magnetic declination, compass, South China Sea, alchemical maturation, Yanniansha, Five Elements</keywords>

# Preparation and Processing
<gloss>To calcine and prepare the substance: For every one catty <term>approx. 600 grams</term> used, take one *yi* <term>an ancient measure of weight</term> of Five-Flower Bark <note>Wujiapi; Acanthopanax bark</note> and one *yi* of Burnet <note>Diyu; Sanguisorba officinalis</note>. Take fifteen ounces of cotton. Coarsely chop the two herbal ingredients on a stone and pound them into thirty cakes.</gloss>

<column-break/>

Place the Magnetite into a porcelain jar and add the herbal cakes. Boil this in "East-flowing water" <note>water from a stream flowing eastward, believed in ancient pharmacy to possess purifying qualities</note> for three days and three nights. Remove the stones, wipe them dry, wrap them in cloth, and pound them into fine pieces. Then, stretch the material until it is as fine as dust. Perform water-levigation <term>shui fei; a process of grinding minerals in water to separate the finest powder from grit</term> and grind it again for use. Zongshi <note>Kou Zongshi, a 12th-century pharmacologist</note> states: When using it in medicine, one must burn it in fire, quench it in vinegar, grind it into a powder, and then perform water-levigation; alternatively, boil it in vinegar for three days and nights.

<column-break/>

# Flavor and Nature
**Acrid, cold, and non-toxic.** <gloss>Zhen Quan states: Its nature is warm; to call it cold is an error. Zhicai states: Bupleurum <note>Chaihu</note> serves as its envoy.</gloss> <gloss>Daming states: Sweet, astringent, and balanced. Zangqi says it is cold.</gloss>

<column-break/>

# Compatibilities
<gloss>It neutralizes iron poisoning and can dissolve the "evil" <note>impurities or harmful energies</note> of gold. It dislikes Peony and Mangcao <note>Illicium anisatum</note>, and it fears Yellow Halloysite <note>Huangshizhi</note>.</gloss> <gloss>Dugu Tao states: It can subdue Cinnabar, "nurture" Mercury, and remove the "halos" <note>impurities</note> from copper.</gloss>

## Main Indications: General Numbness and Wind-Dampness

<column-break/>

# Joint and Limb Pain
For pain in the limbs and joints where one cannot hold objects, and for aching sensations. It eliminates "Great Heat" <note>severe internal heat or high fever</note>, irritability, fullness, and deafness.

<column-break/>

# According to the Classic
<note>Refers to the Shennong Bencao Jing, the foundational text of Chinese herbology</note>
It nourishes the Kidneys, strengthens the bone qi <note>vital energy</note>, and augments the essence. It eliminates irritability, eases the joints, and dissolves swellings, abscesses, and "rat-leprosy" <note>scrofula; tuberculous swelling of the lymph nodes in the neck</note>.

<column-break/>

It treats throat pain and pediatric epilepsy. Drinking water in which the refined mineral has been soaked also makes a person fertile <gloss>The *Additional Records* <note>Mingyi Bielu</note> notes it tonifies the male system</gloss>.

# Metals and Stones, Section I — Volume 10

For kidney deficiency, "wind" type deficiencies <note>In traditional medicine, "wind" often refers to conditions causing tremors, stiffness, or sudden onset symptoms.</note>, stiffness in the body, and lack of mobility in the waist, add this substance to the treatment. <gloss>Zhen Quan</gloss> It treats weakness and fragility of the muscles and bones.

It tonifies the "Five Labors and Seven Injuries" <note>A collective term for various forms of physical and emotional exhaustion</note>, clears blurred vision, and removes irritability. For children who accidentally swallow needles or bits of iron, grind this substance into a fine powder; wrap the powder in meat fiber—ensuring the meat is not cut through—and have them swallow it together. <gloss>Da Ming</gloss> It brightens the eyes, improves hearing, and stops bleeding from metal-inflicted wounds <note>original: "jinchuang," referring to cuts from blades or tools</note>. <gloss>Shizhen</gloss>

### Explanations

<gloss>Zongshi says:</gloss> It nourishes the Kidney Qi and fills the essence and marrow. It is used by all those suffering from deafness or blurred vision due to Kidney deficiency. <gloss>Zangqi says:</gloss> Heavy substances can settle "trembling" or fear; Magnetite and iron powder are examples of this class. <gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> 

Firstly, Magnetite corresponds to the element Water; its color is black, and thus it enters the Kidney system. For this reason, it treats all manner of Kidney-related illnesses, opens the ears, and clears the eyes.

I once knew a scholar who frequently suffered from eye disease; his vision gradually grew dark and he developed cataracts. I, Shizhen, used a modified version of Li Dongyuan’s "Notopterygium Wind-Overcoming Decoction" <note>original: "Qianghuo Shengfeng Tang"</note> and supplemented it with Magnetite and Cinnabar Pills <note>original: "Cizhu Wan"</note>. After two months, his vision was restored to its original state. Magnetite enters the Kidneys to settle and nourish the "True Essence," ensuring that the "Spirit Water" <note>The vital fluids associated with sight</note> does not leak outward. Cinnabar enters the Heart to settle and nourish the "Heart Blood," preventing "Evil Fire" from rising to attack the eyes. 

These are supplemented with Medicated Leaven <note>original: "Shenqu," a fermented mass of herbs and grain</note> to digest stagnant energy. Using both raw and processed forms of these ingredients warms and nourishes the generative energy of the Spleen and Stomach. In Daoist terms, this is the "Yellow Dame" <note>A metaphor for the Spleen/Stomach acting as a mediator</note> acting as a matchmaker to unite the "Infant" <note>A metaphor for the refined vital spirit or specific alchemical elements</note>...

<summary>This text describes the medicinal use of Magnetite (Cishi) to treat Kidney deficiency, deafness, and vision loss, including a case study of a scholar cured of cataracts. It explains the pharmacological action through both traditional organ theory and Daoist alchemical metaphors.</summary>

<keywords>Magnetite, Kidney deficiency, Li Shizhen, Cinnabar, traditional ophthalmology, Five Labors and Seven Injuries, Yellow Dame, Medicated Leaven</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Magnetite (Cishi), specifically debating the safety of its internal consumption and listing historical prescriptions for hearing loss and sensory clarity. It follows the previous discussion on the scholar's eye ailment and the "Wind-Overcoming" decoction.</meta>

Those who formulate medicinal prescriptions should investigate the mysteries of Nature’s creation. Only then can one see the subtle meanings behind Master Sun’s <note>Sun Simiao (581–682 AD), the "King of Medicine" of the Tang Dynasty.</note> "Thousand Gold Stone Spirit Leaven." It is said that this formula brightens the eyes so that even at one hundred years old, one can read fine script; yet, the hidden principles of the medicine have not been fully explained. Who can say that ancient formulas cannot treat modern illnesses? 

Dugu Tao <note>A Tang Dynasty alchemist and author of a text on the properties of minerals.</note> claims that Magnetite is a "hard and stubborn" substance, lacking the vital energy <term>Qi|The vital life force or energy that flows through all things</term> required to melt or transform within the body. He argues it should only be used indirectly for its energetic influence and never consumed long-term, as the physical residue would cause great harm. 

However, medicine is meant to treat disease. If a medicine hits the mark and cures the illness, one may stop; even substances like arsenic or sal ammoniac <note>original: "pishuang" (arsenic) and "naoshe" (sal ammoniac/ammonium chloride), both highly toxic if misused</note> can be ingested as medicine. Why then should Magnetite be the only thing forbidden? Once Magnetite is refined into a fine powder, it is no longer a "hard and stubborn" object. It simply depends on whether the practitioner can accurately diagnose the patient’s condition to strike the target. 

The *Ten Thousand Infallible Arts of Huainan* <note>An ancient collection of folk wisdom and magical techniques.</note> states: "If Magnetite is hung in a well, the missing person will return of their own accord." The commentary explains: "Wrap the clothes of the departed person around Magnetite and hang it in a well; the runaway will return on their own." <note>This reflects an ancient belief in the power of magnetic attraction extending to human spirits or intentions.</note>

## Appended Prescriptions <gloss>13 from older sources, 12 newly added</gloss>

### Sudden Deafness and Blockage
Place half a *qian* <note>A traditional weight, approximately 3.75 grams.</note> of roasted "iron-stone" into the affected ear. Place fine iron sand powder into the healthy ear. The passage will naturally open and allow sound through.

### For Kidney-Deficiency Deafness
Take a piece of high-quality Magnetite the size of a bean and some pangolin scales <note>Manis pentadactyla; in traditional medicine, these were believed to "pierce" through blockages due to the animal's burrowing nature.</note> that have been roasted until charred but preserved. Grind these into a fine powder. Wrap the powder in a small piece of new cotton and place it inside the ear. At the same time, hold a piece of raw iron in the mouth. You will soon feel the ear passage open.

### Deafness in the Elderly
<margin>If there is a sound inside the ear like wind and rain</margin>
Take one *jin* <note>Approximately 500 grams.</note> of Magnetite and pound it into a powder. Wash it with water to remove any red residue. Wrap it in cotton. Take one pair of pig kidneys, slice them thinly, and cook together <note>The text implies a medicinal soup or application involving the kidneys to treat the "Kidney-gate" of the ear.</note> to treat ringing that sounds like wind and rain.

# Elderly Depletion and Frailty
For wind-dampness affecting the lower back and limbs.

<column-break/>

For numbness and pain: Take thirty taels <term>tael|A traditional unit of weight, approximately 37 grams</term> of Magnetite and twenty taels of White Quartz. Crush them. Place in a jar with two buckets of water and soak the jar in the open ground <note>Exposing the medicine to the "dew of the earth" was believed to balance its energetic properties.</note>. Every day, use this water to prepare porridge. After one year, your strength and vitality will be vigorous, and your complexion will be like that of a child.

<column-break/>

# Master Laozi’s Formula
->For when the Yang fails to rise<-
<note>A traditional euphemism for erectile dysfunction or lack of vitality.</note>

Take five catties of Magnetite. Grind it and soak it in clear wine for twenty-seven days. Each dose is three parts during the day and one part at night. Also used for blurred vision.

<column-break/>

# Internal Cataracts
->The Magnetite and Cinnabar Pill<-
<note>original: "Cizhu Wan." This remains a famous formula in Chinese medicine for treating both vision and spirit.</note>

This treats the "spirit-water" <term>Spirit-water|The intraocular fluid or "aqueous humor" in traditional Chinese medicine</term> becoming dilated and gradually thinning. Vision becomes blurred as if walking through fog or heavy dew. Eventually, one sees "flowers in the air" <note>Visual floaters or spots.</note> or objects appearing doubled. If left for long, the eyes cannot capture light. It also treats internal cataracts where the spirit-water appears pale green or pale white.

<column-break/>

For the pale white variety: Use two taels of genuine Magnetite, calcined in fire and quenched in vinegar seven times. Use one tael of Cinnabar <note>original: "Zhusha."</note>. Use three taels of Medicated Leaven <term>Shenqu|A fermented herbal mass used to aid digestion and move the effects of the minerals through the body</term> in its raw state, ground into a powder. Use an additional one tael of Medicated Leaven powder to cook into a paste, then add honey to form pills the size of a parasol tree seed <note>A standard measure for pills, roughly 9mm.</note>.

<column-break/>

Each dose is twenty pills, taken on an empty stomach with rice water. After taking the medicine, a sign of its efficacy is if you look down and see nothing, but look up and can faintly see the stars and the moon. This also treats conditions where "Heart-fire overcomes Metal" <note>A Five Elements reference where excessive heat/stress from the Heart suppresses the Lungs (Metal) because the Kidneys (Water) are too weak to restrain the fire.</note> and the depletion of Water.

<column-break/>

For those with chronic illnesses that recur frequently, taking this will ensure the condition never returns.

# Childhood Convulsions and Epilepsy
->Magnetite Refined Water Drink<-
Authored by Ni Weide <note>A famous Yuan Dynasty (1266–1341) physician.</note> in the *Collection for Clarifying the Mysteries of the Primary Mechanism*.

<column-break/>

# Failure of the Womb to Retract
Known as the disease of spasms <note>original: "Xieji." In this context, it refers to the severe, painful contractions associated with uterine prolapse or postpartum complications.</note>. The pain is unbearable. Use the Magnetite Pill: Magnetite soaked in wine and calcined, then ground to a fine powder. Form into pills with rice paste the size of a parasol tree seed. At bedtime, take forty pills with a decoction of Talcum <note>original: "Huashi."</note>.

# Magnetite

**Taste and Nature** Salty, warm, non-toxic.

**Primary Indications** It repairs severe injuries, benefits the "Yang Way" <note>A traditional reference to male sexual vitality and reproductive health.</note>, and curbs excessive urination.

</margin>

It is said: It is used topically to treat the lower back and feet, removes sores and fistulas, encourages the growth of muscle and skin, and helps a person conceive children. It is best prepared by soaking in wine.

### [The Iron-Sucking Stone]
<note>Magnetite is often colloquially called "Iron-sucking stone" (Xidieshi).</note>

> Take three *qian* <term>qian|A traditional unit of weight, approximately 3.75 grams</term> of Magnetite, four *liang* <term>liang|A traditional unit of weight, often called a "tael," approximately 37.5 grams</term> of Honeysuckle vine, eight *liang* of Red Lead <note>original: "Huangdan." An orange-red lead oxide used in traditional plasters.</note>, and one *jin* <term>jin|A traditional unit of weight, often called a "catty," approximately 500-600 grams</term> of sesame oil. ### Boils and Heat Toxins

### Accidental Swallowing of Iron Needles or Metal

> Take a high-quality Magnetite stone (the size of a date pit), drill a hole through it, and thread it with a string. Swallow the stone and then pull it back out; the swallowed metal will be drawn out immediately.

### Bleeding from Metal Wounds
<note>"Metal wounds" (Jinchuang) typically refer to cuts from blades, swords, or arrows.</note>

### Prolapse of the Rectum

<margin>From "Effective Pointing"</margin>

<gloss>The Classic refers to the stone itself and not the "hair." The efficacy and physical form of "hair-magnetite" and this stone are distinct.</gloss>

# Black Stone
->Middle Grade of the *Records of Distinctive Notations*<-

## Explanation of Names
**Black Water Stone** <gloss>Shizhen says: "Black" refers to its color.</gloss>
**Abiding Stone** <gloss>From the *Records of Distinctive Notations*</gloss>

---

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>The *Records of Distinctive Notations* says:</gloss> Black Stone is produced on the sunny side of Mount Tai. On the shady side of the mountain, there is copper. Copper is female, while iron is male. <gloss>Hongjing says:</gloss> <note>Tao Hongjing (456–536 CE) was a renowned alchemist and physician who organized the *Materia Medica*.</note> The *Classic of Materia Medica* lists "Black Stone" as an alternative name for Magnetite. The *Records of Distinctive Notations* also mentions a Black Stone called "Abiding Stone." While their medicinal uses and physical forms are similar, their thermal natures (cold vs. warm) and their interactions with copper and iron differ. Common formulas do not use this mineral, and few people recognize it; I am unsure if it is truly of the same class as Magnetite. <gloss>Gong says:</gloss> <note>Su Gong was the lead editor of the Tang dynasty's *Newly Revised Materia Medica*.</note> This substance is the "liquid" of iron. True Magnetite contains fine pores, and the interior of those pores is a yellowish-red color. When high-quality pieces are first broken open, they can pick up and attract iron. Those that lack pores and are glossy and pure black are "Black Stone." These cannot pick up iron, and their medicinal efficacy is inferior to that of true Magnetite. <gloss>Song says:</gloss> <note>Su Song (1020–1101 CE) was a Song dynasty scholar famous for his work on pharmacopoeia and horology.</note> Today, the Northern tribes <note>Refers to the Liao or Jin territories north of the Song Empire.</note> bring Magnetite as tribute gifts. Most of these pieces are glossy but lack the strength to attract iron. I suspect these are actually this "Black Stone." They are rarely used in medical prescriptions. <gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> <note>Li Shizhen (1518–1593 CE), the author of this compendium.</note> Magnetite is produced on the shady side of mountains where iron is found; Black Stone is produced on the sunny side of mountains where copper is found. Although their appearances are similar, their inner natures are different. Therefore, Black Stone cannot attract iron.

<summary>This page describes "Black Stone" (Xuanshi), a mineral often confused with Magnetite (Cishi). It establishes that while they look alike, Black Stone lacks magnetic properties and is found near copper deposits rather than iron.</summary>
<keywords>Black Stone, Xuanshi, Magnetite, Cishi, Li Shizhen, Tao Hongjing, Mineralogy, Mount Tai, Magnetism</keywords>

Compendium of Materia Medica

**Collected Explanations** <gloss>The *Records of Distinctive Notations* <note>original: *Bielu*</note> states: Hematite is produced in the mountain valleys of the State of Qi. It is a reddish-blue color, shaped like a rooster's comb, and possesses a lustrous sheen. Those pieces that can stain a fingernail without the color fading are of the highest quality. It may be gathered at any time. Hongjing says: <note>Tao Hongjing (456–536 CE) was a famous physician and alchemist who organized early medical texts.</note> This is the red earth found beneath the city gates of Dai County. In the region east of the Yangtze River, it has long been scarce, and its common use has become infrequent; yet, it remains an essential ingredient in Daoist alchemical formulas. Along with Desert Salt and Brine, it is considered a substance required for urgent needs. Gong says: <note>Su Gong was the lead editor of the Tang dynasty's *Newly Revised Materia Medica*.</note> This stone mostly comes from Daizhou. It is said that within the mountains...</gloss>

<column-break/>

**Explanation of Names** **Beard Pellet** <gloss>from the *Classic*</gloss> **Blood Master** <gloss>from the *Records of Distinctive Notations*</gloss> **Native Cinnabar** <gloss>from the *Compendium*</gloss> <gloss>The *Records of Distinctive Notations* says: That which is produced in Dai County is named "Beard Pellet." Shizhen says: <note>Li Shizhen (1518–1593) is the author of this text.</note> "Zhe" <term>赭: an ancient term for reddish-brown earth or ochre</term> refers to the red color. "Dai" refers to the Yanmen region. Today, the common people call it Native Cinnabar or Iron Cinnabar. The book of *Guanzi* <note>An ancient Chinese political and philosophical text named after the statesman Guan Zhong.</note> states: "Where there is ochre on the mountain's surface, there is iron beneath it." The name "Iron Cinnabar" likely arises from this association, rather than just from its physical shape or color.</gloss>

<column-break/>

# Hematite
<gloss>Lower Grade; from the *Classic* <note>original: *Benjing*</note></gloss>

<column-break/>

**Flavor and Nature:** Salty and warm; non-toxic. <gloss>Zhicai says: <note>Li Zhicai was a Northern Qi physician noted for studying herb-to-herb interactions.</note> It is incompatible with pine resin, cypress seeds, sweet flag, and cinnamon.</gloss> **Main Indications:** Used for "fright-epilepsy" <note>A traditional category of seizures or neurological distress triggered by sudden shocks</note> in both adults and children; infertility in women; cold pain in the lower abdomen; deficiency of "essence" <note>vital reproductive fluids</note>; and a feeling of heaviness in the body. Taking this medicine enables a woman to conceive children. <gloss>From the *Records of Distinctive Notations*</gloss>

<summary>This page introduces Hematite (Daizheshi), detailing its origins in Dai County, its relationship to iron deposits, and its various names like "Blood Master." Its medicinal profile focuses on treating seizures, infertility, and abdominal pain through its "warm" and "salty" properties.</summary>
<keywords>Hematite, Daizheshi, Red Ochre, Ancient Pharmacology, Infertility, Epilepsy, Daizhou, Iron Cinnabar, Li Shizhen, Tao Hongjing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed exploration of Hematite (Daizheshi), specifically refuting earlier misconceptions about its origin and detailing the specific geographical sites where the highest quality "Nail-head" ore is mined. It connects the mineral's physical properties—such as its liver-like color and oily sheen—to its practical uses in metallurgy and ritual protection.</meta>

### Stones and Metals, Part 2
**Volume 10**
**Page 10**

What is gathered is not, in fact, the soil from beneath the city gates. <note>The author is refuting an earlier claim by Tao Hongjing that this mineral was simply red earth from city walls.</note> Today, Mount Ting in the Prefecture of Qi produces these red stones. Among them, one finds shades of red, bright crimson, and even blue-green. The red variety resembles a rooster’s comb and possesses a moist, lustrous sheen. The local people gather it specifically to paint palace pillars a cinnabar-red. The purple variety is oily and resembles the ore produced in Dai County; it has been used since ancient times.

Today, in Mingsha County of Jizhou, along the northern banks of the river, workers dig four to five feet into the level ground to find it. These specimens have a smooth red "skin" on the outside, while the interior is purple and resembles a chicken’s liver. These are far superior to the ones produced in the Qi or Dai regions.

**Song says:** <note>Su Song (1020–1101), a scientist and statesman of the Song Dynasty.</note> Today, it is also found in the mountains of the Hedong and Jiangdong regions. Ancient formulas for "Purple Mineral" used for treating childhood ailments often called for Hematite. It was said that if the genuine article could not be found, "left-facing" oyster shells <note>A rare mutation of oyster shell used as a substitute in traditional pharmacy.</note> should be used instead; from this, we know that true Hematite was once difficult to obtain.

Physicians today mostly select large pieces. Those with patterns on the surface resembling floating bubbles or small "nails" are considered the best; these are called **Nail-head Hematite** <term>丁頭代赭 (Dingtou Daizhe): A high-grade variety of hematite characterized by small, protruding circular nodules on the surface, resembling nail heads.</term>.

The *Classic of Northern Mountains* states: "Within the Shaoyang Mountains, there is much beautiful ochre." The *Classic of Western Mountains* states: "The Guan River flows from Mount Shicui; within it is found 'flowing ochre,' which is smeared upon cattle and horses to prevent disease." Guo Pu <note>A famous 4th-century commentator and mystic.</note> noted: "Ochre is red earth." People today still smear it on the horns of oxen, saying it wards off evil influences.

<column-break/>

**Shizhen says:** <note>Li Shizhen, the primary author of this Compendium.</note> Hematite is found in mountains everywhere, but that produced in the northwest is of the highest quality. During the Song Dynasty, Qingzhou sent ten thousand *jin* <note>Approximately 5,000 kilograms.</note> as an annual tribute.

The *Materia Medica of Outer Alchemy* <note>original: *Waidan Bencao*</note> states: Hematite is a "Yang" stone. It grows alongside "Taiyi’s Surplus Grain" <term>太乙餘糧 (Taiyi Yuliang): Limonite or brown iron ore, often found in hollow, nodular forms.</term> within mountain gorges. When ground into powder, it creates a cinnabar-red color that can be used for punctuating books or enhancing the red luster of gold vessels. When the scholar Zhang Hua used "red earth" to wipe a precious sword to increase its sharpness and brilliance, he was referring to this very substance.

<summary>This page details the geographical origins and physical grading of Hematite (Red Ochre), emphasizing the superior "Nail-head" variety. It also documents the mineral's diverse uses, ranging from veterinary medicine and ritual protection to its role as a pigment and a metal-polishing agent.</summary>

<keywords>Hematite, Daizheshi, Nail-head Hematite, Mingsha County, Su Song, Li Shizhen, metallurgy, protective talismans, traditional Chinese pigments</keywords>

# Compendium of Materia Medica
### Page 8

# Preparation
<gloss>Lei Xiao says: Whenever using this mineral, grind it into a fine powder. Use "wax water" <note>Water in which beeswax has been melted or processed.</note> to repeatedly perform "water levigation" <term>water levigation (shui-fei): a refining technique where powder is stirred in water; the finest particles suspended on the surface are collected while heavy impurities sink</term>. Discard the red film that resembles thin clouds on the water's surface. Then, boil the remaining powder in a decoction of fine tea dregs for one full watch <note>Approximately two hours.</note>. Remove it and grind it another ten thousand times. Heat a clean iron cauldron until it is red-hot, add one ounce of white beeswax, and wait for it to melt. Pour in freshly drawn well water to shock it, then boil it again for twenty or thirty cycles. Remove and sun-dry.</note>
Li Shizhen notes: Modern physicians simply calcine the stone until it is red-hot and quench it in vinegar. This is done three or seven times before grinding and refining it through water levigation. This process is chosen because vinegar "tames" the mineral's harshness and acts as a medicinal guide to the blood division of the Liver Channel. The *Records of Mutual Sensitivities* states: If you boil Hematite in a mixture of wine and vinegar and then plunge a red-hot iron nail into the liquid, stirring it vigorously, it will turn into a liquid state.

# Properties: Bitter, Cold, and Non-toxic
<margin>The *Miscellaneous Records* says: It is sweet.</margin>
<margin>Zhen Quan says: It is sweet and balanced.</margin>
<margin>Xu Zhicai says:</margin>
It is antagonized by *Tianxiong*, *Fuzi* (Aconite), and Dried Ginger, which acts as its medicinal messenger.

## Clinical Indications
For "ghost-infusion" <note>A traditional term for wasting diseases or chronic conditions thought to be caused by malevolent spirits.</note>, "thieving winds" <note>Sudden, sharp neurological or rheumatic pains.</note>, and poisoning from venomous insects or sorcery. It kills "essence-beings" and evil ghosts, and expels poisonous or evil energy from the abdomen. It treats "red flooding" <note>Heavy uterine bleeding.</note> and vaginal discharge in women.

# Additional Applications
<margin>The *Miscellaneous Records*</margin>
Treats the "hundred diseases" of vaginal discharge, difficult labor, retained placenta, and miscarriage. It nourishes the blood and *qi* (vital energy), removes heat from the five viscera and the blood vessels, and treats "blood numbness" <note>Numbness caused by poor circulation.</note> and blood stasis. It is used for "fright-qi" entering the abdomen in both adults and children, and for impotence.

# Volume 10

<gloss>The *Miscellaneous Records* states:</gloss> It stabilizes pregnancy, strengthens the Spleen, and stops "rebellion" of the stomach <note>Referring to nausea or upward-moving digestive issues</note>, vomiting of blood, nosebleeds, excessive menstrual bleeding, "intestinal wind" <term>intestinal wind (chang-feng): a traditional term for passing bright red blood in the stool</term>, hemorrhoids, and fistulas.

<column-break/>
evil spirits. <gloss>Daming <note>Refers to the Song dynasty pharmacopoeia *Da Ming Ri Hua Ben Cao*.</note></gloss>

# Clinical Insights
<gloss>Wang Haogu says: Hematite enters the Hand Shaoyin [Heart] and Foot Jueyin [Liver] meridians. When a patient is fearful, their *qi* <note>Vital energy or life force</note> floats upward; because this mineral is heavy, it can settle it. The weight of Hematite is used to suppress "vacuity-rebellion" <note>An upward rush of energy caused by a deficiency in the body's grounding systems</note>. Therefore, Zhang Zhongjing, when treating</gloss>
<column-break/>
cold damage <note>A category of febrile diseases</note> where, after sweating, vomiting, or purging, the area below the heart feels hard and full and belching does not cease, prescribed Inula and Hematite Decoction. This formula uses two ounces of Inula flower, one ounce of Hematite, two ounces of Ginseng, five ounces of fresh Ginger,
<column-break/>
three ounces of Licorice, half a *jin* <note>Approximately 250 grams</note> of Pinellia, and twelve Jujubes. Boil these in one *dou* <note>Approx. 2 liters</note> of water until six *sheng* remain. Remove the dregs and boil again until three *sheng* remain. Take one *sheng* while warm, three times a day. Li Shizhen notes: Hematite is a medicine for the "blood division" of the Liver and
<column-break/>
Pericardium meridians. Therefore, the conditions it treats are primarily diseases of the blood division of these three meridians. Long ago, there was a child who, after suffering from diarrhea, had eyes that turned upward and had not nursed for three days. The eyes were as yellow as gold and the child's life-force was nearly exhausted. A famous
<column-break/>
physician said: "This is Chronic Convulsion <term>man-jing-feng: a serious pediatric condition involving exhaustion, twitching, and often jaundice</term>. It should be treated using Hematite refined through water levigation."
<column-break/>

## Appended Prescriptions <gloss>12 from old sources, 14 newly added</gloss>
<column-break/>
The stone powder, in doses of half a *qian* <note>Approx. 1.5 to 2 grams</note>, should be mixed into a decoction of winter melon seeds and swallowed. The patient was indeed cured.

### Recurrence of Lily Disease
For cases where the illness <term>Lily Disease</term> <note>A condition of physical and mental exhaustion often following a fever, named because Lily bulbs are a primary treatment</note> has returned after the patient has already undergone sweating or purging treatments. Take seven Lily bulbs, break them apart, and soak them in spring water overnight. ### All Abscesses and Boils
Earth Cinnabar <note>original: *tu zhu* (土硃), likely an earthy form of hematite or red ocher used as a pigment and medicine</note>, snail, and hide glue in equal parts. Grind these into a powder. Mix with one cup of high-quality wine. Let the mixture settle until clear; drink the liquid and apply the dregs to the wound. If it becomes dry, reapply. From *Mr. Zhu's Formulas*.

<column-break/>

### Various Red Rashes and Heat-Toxins
Two *qian* <note>approximately 7.5 grams</note> each of Earth Cinnabar and Indigo Naturalis <note>original: *qingdai* (青黛)</note>, and one *qian* each of Talcum and Schizonepeta <note>original: *jingjie* (荊芥)</note>. Grind into a fine powder. For each dose, take one and a half *qian* mixed with honey water. Additionally, apply the mixture externally. From *Direct Guidance* (*Zhi Zhi*).

<column-break/>

### Throat Obstruction with Swelling and Pain
Boil Purple Cinnabar and drink the decocted juice. From *Prescriptions for Universal Relief* (*Pu Ji Fang*).

### Bleeding Gums with Pests
Grind Earth Cinnabar and Schizonepeta together and rub the mixture onto the gums. Continue for three days. From *Prescriptions for Universal Relief*.

<column-break/>

->Prescriptions for Universal Relief<-

### Red Eyes, Swollen and Closed
Two parts Earth Cinnabar and one part Gypsum <note>original: *shigao* (石膏)</note>. Grind into a fine powder. Take two *qian* with freshly drawn water.

Substances of the ocher class have a sweet, balanced, and warm flavor, and are non-toxic. They are primarily used to treat fright and terror, body heat, and "evil qi" <note>original: *xieqi* (邪氣), a term for external pathogenic factors such as wind or cold</note>. They settle the heart. Long-term consumption

<column-break/>

makes a person's eyes bright and their complexion radiant. [Li] Shizhen <note>the primary author of this work</note> states: This is also used in other regions as a substitute for Hematite <note>original: *daizhe* (代赭)</note>; therefore, its efficacy

<column-break/>

is not significantly different.

# Yu's Leftover Grain
<gloss>Listed in the *Classic of the Materia Medica* (*Benjing*) as a top-grade medicine.</gloss> <note>Limonite concretions, often containing loose yellow earth inside</note>

<column-break/>

## Explanation of Names

White Leftover Grain

<gloss>Shizhen says: Inside the stone is a fine powder like flour, hence it is called "Leftover Grain." Common people call it *Taiyi Yu Yu Liang* <note>Taiyi refers to the "Great One" or a celestial deity</note>; see the entry for *Taiyi* below. [Cheng] Dachang <note>A Song dynasty scholar</note> states: In the mountains of Kuaiji</gloss>

<column-break/>

they are produced in great abundance. The locals there say that in ancient times, when the Great Yu <note>A legendary king and hero famous for controlling the Great Flood</note> held a gathering at Kuaiji, these "leftover grains" were left behind for this reason.

## Collection and Explanation

<gloss>The *Additional Records* (*Bie Lu*) <note>A 3rd-century expansion of the earliest Chinese pharmacopeia</note> states: Yu's Leftover Grain is produced in the pools and marshes of the East Sea and on mountain islands. [Tao] Hongjing <note>A famous 5th-century physician and alchemist</note> says: Nowadays it mostly comes from Dongyang. Its shape is like a goose or duck egg; the outer shell is layered</gloss>

<column-break/>

and overlapping. Inside is a fine yellow powder like cattail pollen; those without sand are of superior quality. In recent years, at Mao Mountain, large quantities were found by digging in the earth; they were extremely fine and good, shaped like ox gallstones <note>original: *niuhuang* (牛黃)</note> with many overlapping layers. The best parts are purple and

<column-break/>

delicate like flour. When chewed, they do not feel gritty. Alchemical texts <note>original: *xianjing* (仙經), literally "Scriptures of the Immortals"</note> include them for dietary ingestion. Southerners also give this name to a type of vine found in level marshes; its leaves are like those of Poria <note>original: *fuling* (茯苓), a medicinal fungus</note>, and its root forms lumps with joints, resembling Poria but red in color. The flavor...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mineral-based medicines, specifically focusing on the identification and properties of Yu Yu Liang (Limonite). It references various historical authorities such as Su Song and Li Shizhen to clarify the distinction between mountain-derived and water-derived varieties.</meta>

->Golden Chamber’s Jade Casket, Volume 10<-

...resembling a green mountain ridge, it is called <term>Yu Yu Liang</term> <note>Literally "Yu's Leftover Grain," scientifically known as Limonite or brown hematite</note>. This has some resemblance to the variety found in ponds and marshes, though some suspect that the modern stone form is actually the "Tai Yi" variety. <term>Su Song</term> <note>A famous Song Dynasty scholar and pharmacologist</note> says: Today it is only found in Zezhou and Luozhou. Ancient accounts described it as shaped like a goose egg with an outer shell, but the modern diagrams show it entirely in the form of mountain rock. It does not resemble an egg at all, which differs slightly from the old descriptions. It can be gathered at any time. Zhang Hua’s *Records of Diverse Matters* states: On the islands of the Fu Sea, there is a "jointed grass" whose fruit is eaten like barley; it is called "Natural Grain" and also known as Yu Yu Liang. Legend has it that when <term>Yu the Great</term> <note>The mythical progenitor of the Xia Dynasty, famous for controlling the Great Flood</note> was managing the waters, he threw his leftover food into the river, and it transformed into this medicine. However, the jointed grass and this mineral are different substances sharing the same name. Or perhaps they are the same species as the variety found in ponds and marshes? <term>Li Shizhen</term> <note>The most famous pharmacologist of the Ming Dynasty</note> says: Yu Yu Liang is actually the yellow powder found inside stones. Those found in ponds and marshes are called Yu Yu Liang, while those found in mountain valleys are called Tai Yi. Although the names were originally the same, the actual substances differ. This distinction is quite remote; for details, see the entry under Tai Yi Yu Liang. <term>Tao Hongjing</term> suggests it comes from a vine, and <term>Su Gong</term> suggests it comes from a grass; although...

# Preparation

<term>Tao Hongjing</term> says: For all use, grind it very finely and use the <term>water-levigation</term> <note>A process called "shui fei" where powder is stirred in water to collect only the finest suspended particles</note> method to obtain the essence. Let it settle and ensure there is no sand or soil remaining. <term>Lei Xiao</term> says: See the entry under Tai Yi.

# Nature and Flavor

Sweet, Cold, Non-toxic. The *Supplementary Records* says: Neutral. <term>Zhen Quan</term> says: Salty. <term>Xu Zhicai</term> says: <term>Peony</term> <note>Mudan</note> acts as its "messenger" herb; it subdues the "five metals" and controls the "three yellows" <note>Referring to alchemical processes involving sulfur, realgar, and orpiment</note>.

# Indications

For coughing and qi-reversal, chills and fever, irritability and fullness, and for treating "red and white" bloody stools. It treats <term>Zheng Jia</term> <note>Fixed and movable masses in the abdomen, often interpreted today as tumors or cysts</note> and extreme heat. If refined and taken as an elixir, it prevents hunger.

It also outlines its preparation through water-levigation and its use in treating digestive issues, coughs, and abdominal masses.</summary>

### Chronic Diarrhea
For cases that will not stop, the "Divinely Effective Tai-Yi Pill" is used: Four <term>liang</term> <note>A traditional unit of weight, roughly 37.5 grams</note> of <term>Yu Yu Liang</term> <note>Limonite/Brown Hematite</note>, calcined in fire and quenched in vinegar; one liang of <term>Aconite</term> <note>Wu Tou</note>, soaked in cold water overnight, peeled, and with the "navel" removed, then roasted. Grind these into a powder and form into pills using a vinegar paste.

### Large Intestine Cough

<column-break/>

After taking this, it causes a person to have great physical strength, allowing them to carry heavy loads and travel long distances; the body feels light and does not reach its limit. Many of those medicinal formulas are not recorded here.

<column-break/>

Therefore, it governs all various ailments of the <term>Lower Burner</term> <note>The "Lower Burner" or Xia Jiao refers to the lower abdomen, including the intestines, bladder, and kidneys</note>, both "front and back" <note>Referring to the urinary and digestive tracts</note>. A poem by <term>Li Zhixian</term> says: "Ailments in the Lower Burner are difficult for people to understand; one must use Yu Yu Liang and Red Kaolin." The *Baopuzi* <note>An influential 4th-century Taoist text by Ge Hong</note> says: The "Yu Yu Liang Nine-Day Pill," if taken for three days...

The heavy nature of Yu Yu Liang makes it a medicinal agent for settling and consolidating the body.

->Essential Prescriptions from the Golden Cabinet: Yu Yu Liang, Volume 10<-

...pills the size of a phoenix tree seed. Take five pills with warm water before each meal. From the *Formulas of Sagely Benevolence*.

### Diarrhea from Cold Damage
For diarrhea that will not stop, accompanied by a feeling of "stuffiness and hardness" <note>This refers to *Pi*, a subjective sensation of blockage or pressure in the upper abdomen</note> below the heart, where the illness is located in the **Lower Burner** <note>The "Lower Burner" or *Xia Jiao* refers to the lower abdomen, including the intestines and pelvic organs</note>: <term>Yu Yu Liang Decoction</term> governs this.

<column-break/>

The formula calls for one <term>jin</term> <note>Approximately 500 grams</note> of <term>Red Kaolin</term> <note>Chi Shi Zhi; a medicinal red clay</note> and one *jin* of <term>Yu Yu Liang</term> <note>Limonite/Brown Hematite</note>. Both are quenched in water. Add six <term>sheng</term> <note>A volume measure, roughly 200ml to 1 liter depending on the historical period</note> of water and boil until only one *sheng* remains. Remove the dross and divide into two doses. From *Zhongjing's Essentials of the Treatise on Cold Damage* <note>Referring to Zhang Zhongjing, a foundational figure in Chinese medicine</note>.

### Red and White Discharge

<column-break/>

### Vaginal Discharge
For vaginal discharge, take equal parts of Yu Yu Liang (calcined in fire and quenched in vinegar) and Dried Ginger. For "red" <note>bloody</note> discharge, reduce the amount of Dried Ginger by half. Grind into a fine powder and take two <term>qian-bi</term> <note>An ancient measurement meaning "a spoonful of powder the size of a coin"</note> on an empty stomach. From the *Formulas of Winning Gold*.

### Uterine Bleeding and Spotting

<column-break/>

### Morbid Leucorrhea
When discharges of green, yellow, red, or white cause a person to be unable to conceive: Use Yu Yu Liang (calcined and ground), Red Kaolin (calcined and ground), Oyster shell (ground), Cuttlefish bone, <term>Dragon's Blood Clay</term> <note>Fu Long Gan; the earth from the center of a long-used wood-burning stove</note> (stir-fried), and Cinnamon heart. Mix these in equal parts and grind into a powder. Take one <term>fang-cun-bi</term> <note>A square-inch spoonful</note> with warm wine. While taking this, one must avoid onions and garlic.

### Intestinal Qi Pain
For lower abdominal pain in women:

<column-break/>

Grind Yu Yu Liang into a fine powder. Take two <term>qian</term> <note>Roughly 3.75 grams</note> with rice water twice a day. This is extremely effective.

### Postpartum Agitation and Restlessness
Take one piece of Yu Yu Liang shaped like a "sour dumpling" <note>original: "酸餡" (suan-xian), a traditional stuffed bun</note>. From the *Simple and Effective Formulas for Safeguarding Life*: Bury half of the stone in the ground, and use one

<column-break/>

*jin* of purple charcoal ash to calcine it. Let it sit in damp earth for one night. Break it open and discard the outer stone; take the fine material from the inside and grind it. Wash it with water five to seven times, dry it in the sun, and then grind it "ten thousand times" <note>A figurative expression meaning to grind it into an extremely fine, impalpable powder</note>. Take two *qian* per dose with a licorice decoction.

<column-break/>

Effect is immediate.

### Scars on the Body and Face
Take equal parts of Yu Yu Liang and <term>Pinellia</term> <note>Ban Xia; a medicinal tuber</note> and grind into a powder. Mix with egg yolk and apply to the area. First, rub the scar with a cloth until it becomes red, then apply the ointment. Do not expose the area to wind or sun for thirty days.

<column-break/>

From the *Tested Formulas*.
