# Great One’s Surplus Grain
->Top Grade in the *Classic of Materia Medica*<-

**Explanations of Names:** Stone Brain <gloss>from the *Classic of Materia Medica*</gloss>; Yu’s Lament <gloss>according to Wu Pu</gloss>. Zangqi <note>Chen Zangqi, a Tang Dynasty pharmacologist</note> states: "Great One" (*Taiyi*) refers to the ancestral source of the Way. "Great One" is the teacher of the Way—that is, the Spirit Lord of Rational Transformation, who was the teacher of Yu <note>referring to Yu the Great, the legendary flood-conqueror</note>. Because the teacher habitually consumed this, it was given the name "Great One." The Minister of Works Zhang states it is the "Stone that Returns the Soul." Within it is the "Yellow Seed" <note>a reference to the yellow core of the mineral</note>, which is guarded by ghosts, spirits, and beasts; it cannot be obtained through reckless means. In Kuaiji, there is a place named "Dream-Born Surplus Grain." The local people dig for it and offer goods to purchase it; they must request a specific amount and offer the equivalent value to obtain it. This suggests the substance possesses a divine spirit; is this not the "Great One"?

**Collected Explanations:** <gloss>The *Miscellaneous Records* states:</gloss> Great One’s Surplus Grain is produced in the valleys of Mount Tai. It is harvested in the ninth month. It is found on the peaks of Mount Tai. It has a casing; within the casing is a white substance, and within the white is a yellow substance resembling the yolk of a chicken egg. For those [missing] rice... <note>Original: "米無" — the text here describes the granular, rice-like consistency of the inner mineral</note>

<column-break/>

...for those who have suffered for ten years, the dosage is also reduced.
**Great Wind and Leprous Disorders**
For eyebrows and hair falling out, and numbness throughout the body: Take one <term>jin</term> <note>approximately 500 grams</note> of Yu’s Surplus Grain, one *jin* of White Alum, and one *jin* of Blue Salt. Grind these into a powder. Use egg to seal and secure the mixture <note>creating a "lute" for the crucible</note>, then calcine it using one "scale" <note>a large, specific weight</note> of charcoal fire from the hour of the Dragon <note>7:00–9:00 AM</note> until the hour of the Dog <note>7:00–9:00 PM</note>. Wait for it to cool, then grind it into a fine powder and bury it in the earth for three days. Take it out, and for every <term>liang</term> <note>approximately 31 grams</note> of the powder, add three *liang* of roasted Sesame powder that has been steamed and sun-dried nine times. Take two <term>qian</term> <note>approximately 7.5 grams</note> daily, washed down with <term>Schizonepeta</term> tea. Take two doses per day.
->From the *Formulas of Sagely Benevolence*<-

Compendium of Materia Medica

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mineral medicines, specifically refining the distinction between Great One’s Surplus Grain (Taiyi Yuliang) and Yu’s Surplus Grain (Yu Yuliang), which were introduced on the previous page as substances used for spiritual and physical fortification.</meta>

<page-num>One</page-num>
<header>->Mineral Category, Middle Grade: Great One’s Surplus Grain, Volume 10<-</header>

**Tao Hongjing** <note>original: "弘景"; a famous 5th-century Daoist and physician</note> **states:** The *Materia Medica* <note>the *Bencao*</note> lists two types: Great One’s Surplus Grain and Yu’s Surplus Grain. Their therapeutic properties are identical. However, in the current age, only Yu’s Surplus Grain is found; people no longer recognize the "Great One" variety. The *Secret Instructions for Ascending to Perfection* <note>original: "登真隱訣"; a Daoist manual for spiritual cultivation</note> contains a formula called the **Pills of the Four Garrisons for Longevity**, which notes that "Great One and Yu’s Surplus Grain stabilize the six viscera and calm the five organs." Since it combines the two names, it is difficult to distinguish which is which; today, people generally refer to them collectively as "Great One’s Yu’s Surplus Grain." Someone once gathered "Empty Green" <note>a copper-based mineral like Azurite</note> at a copper mine and found a yellow-red stone in a rock pit that closely resembled today’s Surplus Grain, but its color was excessively red. I suspect this was the "Great One" variety. The locals there called it "Female Yellow" <note>original: "雌黃"; Orpiment</note>; when applied to objects, its color was exactly like "Male Yellow" <note>original: "雄"; Realgar</note>.

**Su Gong** <note>original: "恭"; a Tang Dynasty official and pharmacologist</note> **states:** Great One’s Surplus Grain is actually a single substance, but it is named based on whether it is refined or coarse. Its outer shell is like porcelain, and its shape is irregular—sometimes square, sometimes round. Initially, the substance inside the shell has not yet solidified and remains a yellow liquid called **Stone Mid-Yellow**. After a long period of solidification, it takes on various colors: some are green, some white, some red, and some yellow. After many years, it turns red, and eventually becomes purple. Those that have turned purple and are easily powdered are all called "Great One." All these various colors are broadly referred to as "Yu’s Surplus Grain." Currently, it is not harvested at Mount Tai <note>original: "太山"</note>, but it can be found in the various mountains of Kuaiji, Wangwu, and the prefectures of Ze and Lu. 

**Tao** <note>Hongjing</note> mentioned that a yellow-red stone might be the Great One, but if it lacks an outer shell, it is definitely not the correct substance.

**Lei Xiao** <note>original: "斅"; author of a famous manual on medicinal preparation</note> **states:** Whenever using this, do not mistakenly use "Stone Mid-Yellow" or "Pebble Yellow," as these two stones are very similar in appearance. "Stone Mid-Yellow" is reddish-black on the inside; its taste is mild and slightly astringent. "Pebble Yellow" has a sour taste. If you tap it and find a single solid piece inside, it is unusable. If mistakenly ingested, it causes the intestines to become dry. To identify true Great One’s Surplus Grain, look for something that resembles a stone but is light; when tapped, it shatters easily into a powder. It is composed of many layers, like the leaves of a plant, or like Female Yellow <note>Orpiment</note>. 

**Kou Zong...** <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, continuing with Kou Zongshi on the next page</note>

<meta>catchword: Zong (referring to Kou Zongshi)</meta>

<summary>This page features a scholarly debate on identifying "Great One’s Surplus Grain" (Taiyi Yuliang), distinguishing it from similar minerals by its unique shell-like structure and its internal "Stone Mid-Yellow" core. Experts provide morphological descriptions and warnings against using look-alike minerals that can cause intestinal dryness.</summary>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding the identification of mineral "grains." It transitions from the general descriptions of the previous page to specific geological observations and criticisms of earlier scholars who misidentified the medicinal parts of these stones.</meta>

<page-num>Thirteen</page-num>

<margin>
*Compendium of Materia Medica* <note>original: "本草綱目"; the definitive 16th-century Chinese encyclopedia of medicine by Li Shizhen</note>
</margin>

Where there is "surplus grain," the snow melts earlier than elsewhere. The *Stone Manual of the Cloud Forest* <note>original: "雲林石譜"; a Song Dynasty treatise on stones</note> states: Dingzhou’s Qishe Mountain produces a stone with yellow earth inside it. People refer to this as **Great One’s Surplus Grain**. Its color is a purplish-black, and it is lumpy and rugged, appearing in various sizes of round shapes.

<column-break/>

When used in medicine, the *Jade Booklet of Gengxin* <note>original: "庚辛玉冊"; an alchemical text focused on the properties of minerals and metals</note> notes: Great One’s Yu’s Surplus Grain is a "Yin Stone" <note>a mineral associated with cool, receptive, or earthly properties</note> found in many locations. It consists of many thin layers stacked together and is deep purple in color. The yellow earth found in the center is called **Stone Yellow**. Its nature is extremely hot. In the winter months...

<column-break/>

...this is because they have not yet thoroughly investigated the original text. Kou Zongshi <note>a 12th-century pharmacologist</note> and various medical formulas actually use the stone shell as the medicinal "Yu’s Surplus Grain." They fail to observe the descriptions regarding the yellow, turbid water that exists before the "grain" has fully formed. These shells are coarse and stubborn... <note>meaning they are difficult to process and lack the medicinal essence of the interior</note>

<column-break/>

Since the Jin and Song dynasties, no distinction has been made between the varieties produced in mountains and valleys versus those from ponds and marshes. Consequently, they are all commonly called **Great One’s Yu’s Surplus Grain**. Su Gong <note>a Tang Dynasty official and scholar</note> further argued that the purple-red variety is "Great One" while all other colors are "Yu’s Surplus Grain," but these...

<column-break/>

...the yellow turbid water inside is what constitutes **Yellow in Stone** <note>original: "石中黃"; Shizhonghuang</note>. This explanation was originally quite clear, but later commentators used their own guesswork, which actually made the meaning obscure. If the substance is stone-like, it is "Yellow in Stone"; if the water solidifies into a powder, it is "Surplus Grain"; when it has completely dried and condensed...

<column-break/>

...it is simply the yellow turbid water inside the shell that has not yet finished forming into "Surplus Grain." Based on this, these three substances are actually the same thing in different states. That which grows in ponds and marshes is called **Yu’s Surplus Grain**, while that which grows in mountains and valleys is **Great One’s Surplus Grain**. Their...

<column-break/>

...yellow turbid water. Shizhen <note>the author, Li Shizhen</note> states: According to the *Additional Records* <note>original: "別錄"; the *Mingyi Bielu*, a classic pharmacological text</note>, Yu’s Surplus Grain is produced in the ponds and marshes of the East Sea and on mountain islands. Great One’s Surplus Grain is produced in the valleys of Mount Tai. "Yellow in Stone" is found wherever these "Surplus Grains" are located.

<column-break/>

[Su] Yu <note>likely Su Gong or a related commentator</note> states: For Great One’s Surplus Grain, the shell is what is used. Therefore, when preparing it for medicine, it must be subjected to intense firing and then quenched in vinegar <note>a common method to make hard minerals brittle enough to be ground into powder</note>. "Yellow in Stone" refers to the dry material inside the shell as well as the fine liquid. "Yellow in Stone Water" is the substance before it has matured into "Surplus Grain."

<summary>This page clarifies the relationship between three mineral substances—Great One's Surplus Grain, Yu's Surplus Grain, and Yellow in Stone—explaining that they are different developmental stages or regional varieties of the same mineral (limonite).</summary>
<keywords>Great One's Surplus Grain, Yu's Surplus Grain, Yellow in Stone, Li Shizhen, mineralogy, pharmacology, Kou Zongshi, alchemical processing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Yu’s Surplus Grain, transitioning from the geological debates of the previous page to the medicinal properties, traditional lore, and rigorous preparation methods required for this mineral. It includes insights from Li Shizhen and citations from ancient authorities like the Divine Farmer’s Classic.</meta>

<page-num>One</page-num>

### Invention
<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> **Yu’s Surplus Grain** <note>original: "禹餘糧"; limonite or brown hematite</note>, **Great One’s Surplus Grain**, and the **Yellow Water** found inside these stones are all identical in their medicinal nature, flavor, and function. However, when used in medicine, there are varying grades of refinement. For this reason, practitioners of the dietary arts <note>alchemists and Daoists seeking longevity</note> prefer to use the internal yellow water.

<column-break/>

...provides great satiation, eliminates fatigue, and makes the body feel heavy.
<gloss>From the Miscellaneous Records</gloss> <note>original: "別錄"; the *Bencao Jing Jizhu* or similar supplementary records</note>: It benefits the Spleen and calms the Qi of the internal organs. <gloss>Lei Xiao says:</gloss> It stabilizes the six hollow organs and settles the five solid viscera.
<gloss>Hongjing says:</gloss> <note>referring to Tao Hongjing, a 5th-century polymath</note>

<column-break/>

...bulrush and iron filings.
If the joints are not supple... if taken over a long period, one can endure extreme cold and heat, feel no hunger, experience a lightened body, and fly a thousand miles like a divine immortal.
<gloss>From the Divine Farmer’s Classic</gloss> <note>original: "本經"; the *Shennong Bencao Jing*, the foundational text of Chinese herbology</note>: It treats...

<column-break/>

It is antagonistic to Fritillaria and Sweetflag <note>meaning these substances should not be combined as they counteract each other</note>.
### Main Indications
Coughing with rebellious upward Qi, abdominal masses, blood blockages <note>such as amenorrhea</note>, and vaginal discharge. It also eliminates malevolent Qi from the limbs...

<column-break/>

### Flavor and Nature
Sweet, neutral, and non-toxic.
<gloss>Wu Pu says:</gloss> Shennong, Qibo, and Lord Lei state it is sweet and neutral. Li Dangzhi says it is slightly cold. Bian Que says it is sweet and non-toxic. <gloss>Zhicai says:</gloss> **Eucommia bark** <note>original: "杜仲"</note> acts as its "messenger" <note>an auxiliary herb that guides the main medicine to its target</note>.

<column-break/>

...as a standard. The medicinal scent is naturally fragrant, similar to newly harvested rice. Once it has been pounded, it must be ground further ten thousand times before it is ready for use.

<column-break/>

### Processing
<gloss>Lei Xiao says:</gloss> To prepare this substance, take five *he* <note>approx. 0.5 liters</note> of black beans and five *he* of Siberian Solomon’s Seal <note>original: "黃精"</note>. Boil these in two *dou* <note>approx. 20 liters</note> of water until only five *sheng* remain. Place this liquid in a porcelain pot and add four *liang* <note>approx. 150 grams</note> of the Surplus Grain. Boil it until the liquid is entirely absorbed.

<column-break/>

The exterior of the stone often has gravel stuck to it; wash away the yellow earth. The stone is then hollow and can hold water, serving as a "water dropper" for an inkstone. The *Mirror of Alchemical Formulas* <note>original: "丹方鑑"</note> states: The "Five-Colored Surplus Grain" and the "Yellow inside the Stone" can both be dried and powdered to reveal a golden color.

The Great One’s Essence <note>original: "太一"; a high-grade mineral</note> is ranked first.

# Stone-Inside Yellow Essence
*From the Tang Materia Medica* <note>original: "唐本草"; the first official pharmacopeia in China, commissioned by the Tang court in 659 CE</note>

## Explanation of Names
**Zongshi says:** <note>referring to Kou Zongshi, a Song dynasty medicinal scholar</note> The word “Essence” or “Seed” <note>original: "子"; *zi*</note> should actually be written as “Water.” Since the text describes it as a yellow, turbid liquid, why would it be called a “seed”?

## Collected Explanations
**Su Gong says:** <note>a primary editor of the *Tang Materia Medica*</note> This substance is the unformed yellow, turbid water found within the shells of **Yu’s Surplus Grain**. It is found in the same locations where the "grain" is produced. 

**Su Song says:** <note>a polymath and statesman of the 11th century</note> Today, it is produced in the valleys of Mount Tiaoshan in Hezhong Prefecture. The stone is shaped like the fruit of the chaste tree and is a purple-black color. The interior of the stone skin is yellow; this is what is called **Stone-Inside Yellow**. 

**Ge Hong says in the *Master Who Embraces Simplicity*:** <note>original: "抱朴子"; an essential 4th-century Daoist text on alchemy and longevity</note> This substance exists wherever stones are found, but it is particularly abundant in the mountains of Qinshui. It resides inside large stones. These stones are perpetually moist and never dry. When you crack one open, you will find dozens of layers. The center contains a yellow, flowing liquid, appearing much like an egg inside its shell. One should drink it immediately before it hardens. If you do not, it will gradually solidify into a hard stone, at which point it is no longer fit for consumption. When cracking a stone, a large one may yield as much as a liter <note>original: "升"; *sheng*</note>, while a smaller one may yield only a few deciliters. It can be swallowed all at once. 

**Wang Ji says:** <note>a Ming dynasty physician</note> If the substance inside the stone is dry or has turned into a fine powder, it should be called **Surplus Grain**. It should not be called **Stone-Inside Yellow**. This distinction is clear from the original text’s use of the phrase “unformed Surplus Grain.” 

**Li Shizhen says:** <note>the 16th-century author of the *Compendium of Materia Medica*</note> **Surplus Grain** refers to the fine powder that has already solidified within the stone. **Stone-Inside Yellow** refers to the form that has hardened into a solid stone-like mass.

<margin>
*Compendium of Materia Medica* <note>original: "本草綱目"</note>
</margin>

<margin>The "inner-yellow" fluid refers to a substance that has not yet solidified. This is why Lei Xiao <note>original: "雷斅", a Liu Song dynasty pharmacologist famous for his work on the processing of medicinal substances.</note> said: "Use the 'residual grain' <note>original: "餘粮", referring to the mineral limonite</note> but do not use the liquid 'stone-yellow' found inside"; this is correct.</margin>

**Sweet, Neutral, and Non-toxic.**
**Primary Indications: Taken over a long period, it lightens the body, extends life, and prevents aging.**

# Hollow Green
<note>Identified as hollow nodules of malachite or azurite, highly prized in antiquity for both medicine and alchemy.</note>

->A "Superior Grade" substance from the Divine Husbandman’s Classic<-

### Explanation of Names
**Bayberry Green**
<gloss>Li Shizhen <note>The primary author of this compendium.</note> says: "Hollow" refers to its physical structure, while "Green" describes its color. "Bayberry" refers to its resemblance to the fruit of the bayberry tree.</gloss>

---

### Collected Explanations
<gloss>The *Supplementary Records* <note>original: "別録", referring to the Mingyi Bielu</note> state: Hollow Green is produced in the mountain valleys of Yizhou and on Yuesui Mountain in areas where copper is found. It is formed by the "fumes" or essence of copper. The nodules are hollow inside. They should be gathered in the third month, though they can actually be collected at any time. This substance has the power to transform copper, iron, lead, and tin into gold.</gloss>

<gloss>Tao Hongjing <note>A famous 5th-century alchemist and physician.</note> says: Yuesui used to belong to Yizhou. Today, it is no longer found in the various commanderies of Yizhou, perhaps because it has not been mined for a long time. The specimens currently coming from the copper mines <note>original: "銅官", a place name or general term for official copper mines</note> have the most vivid and deep color. Those from Shixing are inferior. There is also a "Hollow Green Mountain" in the Gaoping commandery of Liangzhou where it is quite abundant. Nowadays, the "Hollow Green" found is often solid and round like iron beads, lacking the hollow center. These are all extracted by carving into the earth and stone. When used in the compounding of elixirs, they are successful in "transmuting" lead into gold. Among all mineral medicines, this is the most precious. It is rarely used in medical prescriptions, and much of it is used to create yellow pigment, which is a great pity.</gloss>

<gloss>Su Gong <note>The lead editor of the Tang Materia Medica.</note> says: Places that produce copper also contain various other green minerals, but the hollow...</gloss>

<summary>This page introduces "Hollow Green" (malachite nodules), detailing its classification as a superior medicine and its legendary alchemical ability to transmute base metals into gold. It includes geographical notes on its sourcing from ancient Chinese copper mines and its physical resemblance to bayberries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of mineral substances, specifically detailing the geographical origins, physical varieties, and historical expert opinions on Hollow Green (Kongqing), a rare form of malachite or azurite prized for its liquid-filled interior.</meta>

<margin>
Compendium of Materia Medica
Volume 15
Hollow Green
</margin>

...Hollow Green is difficult to obtain. Nowadays, it is produced in Weizhou, Lanzhou, Xuanzhou, and Zizhou. The variety from Xuanzhou is considered the best because the pieces are refined and delicate; occasionally, one is found with a hollow center. Those from Weizhou and Lanzhou come in large chunks with a very deep color, but they lack a hollow interior.

<column-break/>

Master Tao <note>original: "陶氏", referring to Tao Hongjing, the 5th-century alchemist</note> describes those that are small, solid, and round like iron beads as "White Green." Daming <note>A Song Dynasty pharmacologist</note> says: Hollow Green varies in size from a chicken egg down to a <term>rosary pea</term> <note>original: "相思子", the seed of *Abrus precatorius*, used as a standard for small measurements</note>. Its green shell is as thick as the skin of a lychee.

<column-break/>

Inside, it contains a juice that is both sour and sweet. Cangqi <note>original: "藏器", referring to Chen Cangqi</note> says: This is the quintessence of copper. The larger specimens are known as "Hollow Mineral-Green," while the next size down is "Hollow Green." Su Song <note>original: "頌", referring to the author of the *Illustrated Materia Medica*</note> says: Nowadays, Raozhou and Xinzhou also occasionally produce it. It is shaped like a bayberry, hence the name "Bayberry Green."

<column-break/>

Those with a hollow interior are extremely hard to come by. Zongshi <note>original: "宗奭", referring to Kou Zongshi</note> says: Emperor Zhenzong <note>of the Song Dynasty</note> once issued an imperial decree to obtain Hollow Green containing water; it took a long time to find any. This Bayberry Green comes from the mountain caves of Xinzhou...

<column-break/>

...and is extremely difficult to harvest. It is highly effective in medical treatments. Some of these also contain water and are used in the same manner as Hollow Green, though they differ in quality. Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen, the compiler of this work</note> says: The *Essential Secrets of the Jade Cave* by Zhang Guo <note>One of the legendary Eight Immortals, associated here with alchemical knowledge</note> states that Hollow Green resembles the...

<column-break/>

...Bayberry Green. It receives the essence of "red gold" <note>copper</note> and the moisture of the "cloudy mists of wood" <note>original: "甲乙陰霧之氣", referring to the Wood element in the Sexagenary cycle</note>. Growing near springs, it absorbs moisture over a long period. When newly extracted from a pit, if you drill into it, there is water inside; after some time, it dries up and becomes like a pearl with glittering golden specks.

<column-break/>

The *Guidebook of the King of Geng-Xin* <note>original: "庚辛王冊", an alchemical text named after the metal elements Geng and Xin</note> says: Hollow Green is a "Yin Stone" <note>A mineral associated with cool, receptive, or internal properties</note>. It is produced in Shangrao. Those that resemble <term>stalactites</term> are considered superior. Large pieces containing a purple hue with a lustrous sheen are the next best. It is produced in Yandao of the Shu region <note>Modern Sichuan</note>, and at Mt. Daizhi. It grows within gold-bearing pits...

<column-break/>

...continuously, hence the green becomes an elixir. Some are as large as a fist, while others are egg-shaped. They are hollow and contain a fluid like oil. It is instantly effective in treating blindness. Those found in copper pits are also excellent and can be used for painting. There are also Bayberry Green and Stone Green.

It cites various historical scholars and alchemical texts to distinguish between true Hollow Green and its related varieties like Bayberry Green.</summary>

# Volume 10
# Minerals

<margin>
The Divine Farmer’s Classic
</margin>
## Lightening the body and prolonging life.
## Treating redness and pain in the eyes, removing cataracts <note>original: "膚翳", referring to opaque films over the eye</note>, stopping excessive tearing, promoting the flow of the urinary tract, and stimulating the production of breast milk.

<column-break/>

## Treats night blindness and deafness; brightens the eyes and benefits the nine orifices <note>The nine openings of the body: eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, and the two lower orifices</note>; clears the blood vessels, nourishes the vital spirit, and benefits the Qi of the Liver. For long-term use.

<column-break/>

## Flavor and Qi: Sweet, sour, cold, and non-toxic.
<margin>
The *Collected Records* says: It is "Greatly Cold."
Master Quan says: It "dreads" <note>is medicinally incompatible with</note> <term>Dodder Seed</term> <note>original: "兎絲子", *Cuscuta chinensis*</note>.
</margin>
## Principal Indications

<column-break/>

These substances are all of a single essence, though their "Qi" <note>vital energy or material character</note> varies in refinement and coarseness. For the purposes of alchemical transformation, Layered Green <note>original: "曾青", *Zengqing*, a stratified azurite</note> is considered superior, followed by Hollow Green, and then Bayberry Green. The *Guide to the Creation* states: Copper receives the energy of "Purple Yang" to produce green; after two hundred years, this green produces Mineral Green <note>original: "石綠", *Shilü*, malachite</note>, and the copper begins to grow within it. Both Layered Green and Hollow Green are considered the most refined forms of Mineral Green—those that have "attained the Way." All of these are referred to as ores. After another two hundred years, they receive the energy of "Green Yang" and transform into <term>Brass-stone</term> <note>original: "鍮石", *Toushi*, likely refers to a natural alloy or smithsonite/pyrite used to make brass</note>.

Observing these various theories, one sees that Hollow Green comes from two sources: gold mines and copper mines. It may be as large as a fist or an egg, or as small as a bean; it can appear in flat pieces or shaped like a bayberry. Despite these differences in size and quality, the variety containing liquid is always considered the best, while the solid variety without liquid is considered inferior. Physicians who apply chemical green <note>original: "銅青", verdigris or copper acetate</note> to copper objects to produce a green crust and scrape it off to pass it off as "Hollow Green" are using a fake; it is merely copper green and not the refined essence of Mineral Green.

<keywords>Hollow Green, Mineral Green, ophthalmology, Chinese alchemy, traditional pharmacology, copper transformation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical profile of Hollow Green (Azurite), moving from its basic properties to specific therapeutic applications and a deep theoretical explanation of why it heals the eyes based on Five Elements theory.</meta>

Its fluid opens the joints, breaks up stubborn internal accumulations, prevents forgetfulness, and elevates one's willpower to the level of the immortals. <gloss>Collected Records</gloss> It treats "wind-headaches," stabilizes the Liver, and allows those with damaged pupils to see objects again. <gloss>Zhen Quan</gloss> One may drill a hole into the stone to extract the inner liquid; using this as eye drops treats many years of "green blindness" <note>likely referring to glaucoma or optic nerve atrophy</note>, internal cataracts, and films over the eye. It nourishes the essence and Qi. The shell of the stone can be ground down to rub away ocular films. <gloss>Daming</gloss> For facial paralysis where the mouth is slanted following a stroke, swallowing a portion the size of a bean is extremely effective. <gloss>Shizhen</gloss> from the formulas of Fan Wang.

### Explanations <note>original: "發明", these sections provide the theoretical rationale for a drug's efficacy</note>

<gloss>Bao Sheng says:</gloss> Hollow Green follows the laws of the Water element; therefore, its color is green-blue and it governs the Liver <note>In Chinese medicine, the color green and the Liver are both associated with the Wood element, which is nourished by Water</note>. <gloss>Song says:</gloss> It is the most essential medicine for treating cataracts and visual obstructions.

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> In the East, the celestial stems *Jia* and *Yi* <note>Wood elements</note> give birth to the Liver and Gallbladder. The purest part of their Qi becomes Liver blood, and its essence becomes Gallbladder bile. This system "opens a portal" through the eyes. Indeed, the refined essence of all five viscera flows into the eyes to form the "Spirit." When Gallbladder bile is sufficient, vision is clear; when the bile decreases, vision becomes dim. 

Copper is also produced from the Qi of "Green Yang." The clear portion of its Qi becomes Mineral Green <note>Malachite</note>, which is analogous to Liver blood. Its refined essence becomes the fluid inside Hollow Green, which is analogous to Gallbladder bile. The reason it serves as a miraculous medicine for the eyes is due to the principle of "categorical resonance" <note>original: "以類相感應", the idea that substances with similar cosmic signatures affect one another</note>.

->For stones that are hollow inside,<-
->bury them in the earth for three to five days,<-
->and they will naturally produce fluid.<-

# Appendix of Formulas
<margin>3 New, 2 Old</margin>

**Blurred and dim vision** where the eyes cannot see clearly. Use a small amount of Hollow Green <note>original: "空青", azurite</note> and apply it to the eyes using fresh dew.
<margin>From: Essential Formulas Worth a Thousand Gold</margin>

---

**Black film covering the pupil.** 
One ounce each of Hollow Green and Arsenopyrite <note>original: "礜石", an arsenic-bearing mineral</note> (both roasted/calcined), one ounce of cowrie shell, and four kernels of *Prinsepia* nut <note>original: "蕤仁", *Prinsepia uniflora*</note>. Grind these into a fine powder and apply to the eye daily.
<margin>From: Imperial Medical Encyclopedia</margin>

---

**Surface films and darkened vision.**
Two mace <note>a "mace" or *qian* is a traditional unit of weight, approx. 3.7 grams</note> of Hollow Green, one ounce of *Prinsepia* nut, and three mace of Borneol camphor <note>original: "片腦", a cooling aromatic resin</note>. Grind finely and apply to the eye daily.
<margin>From: Imperial Medical Encyclopedia</margin>

---

**All manner of eye disorders.**
This includes red eyes, "blue-blindness" <note>likely glaucoma</note>, and both internal and external cataracts or films. The effectiveness of this remedy is proven if the eye feels a cool, refreshing sensation upon application.
<margin>From: Yang’s Formulas; Imperial Medical Encyclopedia</margin>

---

**Layered Green**
Wash it clean. Take 2.5 mace each of the mineral and washed Chinese Goldthread <note>original: "胡黃連", *Picrorhiza kurroa*</note>. Before sunrise and without speaking, gather Sophora buds <note>original: "槐牙", shoots of the scholar tree</note> and place them inside a green bamboo tube. Hang the tube in the "Direction of the Two Virtues of Heaven and Moon" <note>auspicious directions in traditional Chinese cosmology used for timing medical preparations</note>. Once dry—and ensuring the ingredients have not been seen by chickens or dogs—grind into a powder. To 1.5 mace of this powder, add a "character's weight" <note>original: "一字", a small measure approx. 0.5 grams</note> of Dragon’s Brain camphor and store it in a sealed container. At bedtime, rinse the mouth, tilt the head back, and blow a small amount into both nostrils. The condition will be cured immediately; use for two nights.
<margin>From: Imperial Medical Encyclopedia</margin>

---

**Facial paralysis with a crooked mouth**
See the primary entry for treatment instructions.

---

# Layered Green
->Top Grade in the Divine Husbandman's Classic<-

### Explanation of Names
Shizhen says: The word *Zeng* <note>original: "曾"</note> is pronounced like "layer" (*ceng*). This mineral is so named because it grows in distinct layers. Some say it is called Layered Green because its growth progresses from a solid state to a hollow state, and finally to a layered state.

Volume 10
Metal and Stone Section
</margin>
Place the "Green" <note>the Layered Green mineral</note> inside. Using "water flowing to the east" <note>water from a stream flowing eastward, believed in alchemy to possess greater vitality</note>, add two *yi* <note>an ancient unit of weight, roughly 20-24 ounces</note> and simmer it slowly for five days and nights. Do not allow the water or the fire to miss their proper timing. Take the mineral out, wash it with the east-flowing water, and grind it in a mortar until it is as fine as powder. Then it is ready for use.

<column-break/>

# Preparation
Lei Xiao <note>original: "斅"; a famous Liu Song Dynasty pharmacologist</note> says: Whenever using this, do not use pieces that are mixed with common stone or verdigris <note>original: "銅青", the green crust on copper</note>. For every ounce <note>original: "兩", tael</note> of mineral, you require three items: Purple-backed Sky Sunflower <note>original: "紫背天葵", Begonia fimbristipula</note>, Licorice root, and Blue Lingzhi herb <note>original: "青芝草"</note>. Use one *yi* of each, whether they be dry or fresh. Chop them finely and place them in a porcelain pot.

<column-break/>

Hollow Green and Layered Green often emerge from the same sources. Layered Green can be combined to create the Medicines of the Immortals.
<margin>The *Guangji Fang* and the *Records of Mount Heng* state: The mountain has layers...</margin>

<column-break/>

Those that produce a metallic sound when struck are the genuine ones. The *Guide to Creation* <note>original: "Zaohua Zhinan", an alchemical text</note> says: Layered Green is born within copper mines; it is essentially "Stone Green" <note>malachite</note> that has attained the Way <note>matured or reached a higher alchemical state</note>. Its texture possesses the "Correct Color of the East" <note>the symbolic blue-green of the East in five-elements theory</note>. It can be used for refining the Great Elixirs of alchemy and for the transmutation of metals.

<column-break/>

...as one chooses to use it. Li Shizhen <note>the compiler of this Compendium</note> says: In any place where copper has been produced since ancient times, this mineral grows. Its shape resembles the roots of Chinese Goldthread <note>original: "黃連", Coptis chinensis</note> joined together, or like the coiled castings of an earthworm. It has square edges and its color is as deep as Persian indigo. It grows in distinct layers. If you strike it, it rings like...

<column-break/>

Its color and texture are similar. However, Hollow Green is extremely difficult to obtain and very expensive, so the scriptures of the Immortals rarely use it. The methods for using Layered Green to transform metals into gold are the same as those for Hollow Green. Su Gong <note>original: "恭", a Tang Dynasty editor</note> says: Those coming from Weizhou <note>in modern Hebei</note> are best, those from Ezhou <note>in modern Hubei</note> are second. Those from other prefectures are not as good.

<column-break/>

"Positive Green" <note>original: "陽青", Yang Green</note> is the pure essence of copper. Tao Hongjing <note>original: "弘景", the famous 5th-century alchemist</note> says: Old traditions say this is the same as Hollow Green. Their medicinal properties for treating the body are also similar. Today, the mines at Tongguan no longer produce Layered Green; it only comes from Shixing <note>in modern Guangdong</note>. Its shape is lumpy and knotted, like the roots of Chinese Goldthread joined together.

<column-break/>

# Collected Explanations
The *Miscellaneous Records* <note>original: "Bielu"</note> state: Layered Green is produced in the valleys of the Sichuan region <note>original: "蜀"</note> and in Yuexi. It can be gathered at any time. It has the power to transform copper into gold. Jin <note>referring to a historical geographical record</note> says: It is produced in the stone mountains of the Shu Commandery. In those mountains, wherever there is copper, Layered Green is produced...

# Flavor and Nature
Sour, slightly cold, and non-toxic. <gloss>Zhicai <note>Xu Zhicai, a 6th-century physician</note> says: It is suppressed by Dodder seed. Dugu Tao <note>an alchemist</note> says: Layered Green is an ingredient in the "Great Achievement Salve"; it can solidify mercury and control Cinnabar, for it is produced from the "essence of gold" <note>a reference to its mineral purity and alchemical power</note>. It must be boiled in clear wine or vinegar before use. Ge Hong <note>a famous 4th-century Daoist alchemist</note> says: If you rub Layered Green on iron, it turns the color red like copper.</gloss>

# Primary Indications
Eye pain, stopping the flow of tears, and "wind-damp" numbness <note>joint pain or paralysis attributed to environmental factors</note>.

# Benefits the Joints and Opens the Nine Orifices
Breaks down hard abdominal masses and internal accumulations. Long-term consumption makes the body feel light and prevents aging. <gloss>From the *Divine Husbandman's Classic of Materia Medica*</gloss> It nourishes the liver.

# Functions of the Gallbladder; Removes Chills and Fever
Kills "white worms" <note>parasites</note>, treats "head-wind" <note>chronic migraines or neurological symptoms</note> and cold sensations in the brain, stops irritability and thirst, and supplements deficiencies. It causes 

# Yin Energy
to flourish. <gloss>From the *Miscellaneous Records of Famous Physicians*</gloss>

# Elucidations
<gloss>Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen, author of this Compendium</note> says: The logic of using Layered Green to treat the eyes is the same as that of Hollow Green. It was used in the ancient formula "Supreme One Divine Spirit Pill for Warding off Evil." The legendary physician Bian Que treated abdominal accumulations and fluid retention with a "Layered Green Pill"; both of these are found in the *Records of Tested Formulas Past and Present*. As there are many such medicines, I will not record them all here.</gloss>

## Wind-Heat Eye Diseases <gloss>Layered Green Powder: Treats all cases of wind-heat toxic energy attacking the upper body, causing redness or ulceration of the eyes.</gloss>
Symptoms include sensitivity to sunlight, blurred or sandy vision, sticky discharge, tearing, itching, or pain. Take four *liang* <note>a traditional unit of weight, approx. 37 grams</note> of Layered Green, two *liang* of **Vitex seed**, and one *liang* each of **roasted White Ginger** and **Saposhnikovia root** <note>Fangfeng, a root used to "dispel wind" in traditional medicine</note>. Grind these into a fine powder. Snuff a small amount into the nose; the effects are immediate. <gloss>From the *Official Formulas of the Pharmacy Bureau*</gloss>
Take five *fen* <note>approx. 1.8 grams</note> of **Layered Green**, seven and a half *qian* <note>approx. 28 grams</note> of **Realgar**, and two and a half *qian* of **Scutellaria**. Grind into a fine powder and apply it to the affected area. <gloss>From the *Precious Mirror of Health*</gloss>

# Green Verdancy <note>referring to Malachite or Stone Green</note>
<gloss>From the *Classic*: Superior Category</gloss>

## Explanation of Names: Stone Green
<gloss>Called "Great Green" in the *Tang Materia Medica*; also in the *Compendium*.</gloss>

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>The *Miscellaneous Records* say: Green Verdancy grows in the shady caves of mountains; its color is blue-white. Tao Hongjing <note>a 5th-century alchemist and physician</note> says: This is the substance...</gloss>
...used by painters to create the color green. It is found within the same caves as Layered Green, often mixed together. Today’s painters call this "Jade Green," while they call "Hole Green" <note>another copper mineral</note> "Green Verdancy," which is exactly the opposite of the ancient names. Su Gong <note>a Tang dynasty official</note> says: Green Verdancy is the same as "Flat Green." Painters call it **Stone Green**. What they call "Jade Green" is actually "White Green," which is not used in painting. Su Song <note>a Song dynasty scholar</note> says: Older records did not specify which administrative districts produced it, merely stating it grows in shady mountain caves. In the entry for "Hollow Green," it is noted that it grows in the valleys of Yizhou and the mountains of Yuexi in areas where copper is found. This substance likely originates in those same...

<summary>This page details formulas for "Layered Green Powder" used to treat inflammatory eye conditions and begins a new entry on Green Verdancy (Malachite), focusing on the historical confusion over its naming among painters and physicians.</summary>
<keywords>Layered Green, Malachite, Wind-Heat, Eye Disease, Realgar, Saposhnikovia root, Tao Hongjing, Mineral Pigments</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Stone Green" (Malachite) entry from the "Metals and Stones" section of the Compendium of Materia Medica. It transitions from the physical description and geological origins of the mineral to its industrial refining processes and medicinal properties.</meta>

...the shady side of mountains. Today, it is produced in Shaozhou and Xinzhou. Its color is a bluish-white, and it is the substance used by painters to produce green pigment. It often comes in very large chunks, the interior of which features lovely blue and white floral patterns. People in Xinzhou carve these into belt buckles, vessels, and women’s jewelry. For medicinal use, chunks that resemble **Frankincense** <note>referring to the teardrop shape and high-quality resinous appearance</note> are considered best.

<gloss>Zongshi says:</gloss> Those that are a deep, dark green in color are superior. 

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> Stone Green is a "Yin" mineral. It grows within copper mines and represents the "ancestral breath" of copper. When copper receives the "Purple Yang" energy <note>a cosmological term in Daoist alchemy referring to formative, generative solar forces</note>, it produces a green verdancy. Over a long period, this verdancy hardens into stone, which we call **Stone Green** <note>Malachite</note>. Copper grows within it, and it shares the same origin as **Azure Stone** <note>Zengqing; often identified as Azurite</note>. 

Today, people call it "Great Green." Fan Chengda, in his *Treatise on the Southern Flora and Fauna* (*Guihai Zhi*), notes: "Stone Green is the 'sprout' of copper. It is produced in Guijiang, Guangxi, appearing in rocks wherever copper is present." Specimens with a stony texture are called Stone Green. A variety that is brittle and crumbly like loose soil is called **Mud Green**, which is considered the lowest grade. 

The *Collected Statutes of the Ming Dynasty* (*Da Ming Huidian*) states: From [standard batches of] blue-green stone ore, one can wash and refine 61 *liang* and 4 *qian* <note>approx. 2.3 kg</note> of "Pure Green." For "Dark Green," every *jin* <note>approx. 600 grams</note> of ore yields 10 *liang* and 8 *qian* of "Pure Green." By using one *jin* of **Sal Ammoniac** <note>ammonium chloride, used as a flux or reagent in traditional refining</note> in the firing process, one produces 15 *liang* and 5 *qian* of "Sal Ammoniac Green."

## Qi and Flavor
<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> It has a minor level of toxicity.

## Primary Indications
It benefits the Qi, stops chronic diarrhea and dysentery, and treats ailments of the nose. <gloss>From the *Additional Records* (*Bielu*)</gloss> It is very effective at inducing the vomiting of "wind-phlegm" <note>a traditional diagnosis for respiratory or neurological symptoms thought to be caused by internal 'wind' and accumulated fluids</note>.

## Efficacy
<gloss>Zangqi <note>referring to the 8th-century physician Chen Zangqi</note></gloss>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Stone Green (Malachite) from the Compendium of Materia Medica. It transitions from the physical properties and geological origins discussed on page eight to its specific clinical applications, focusing on its use as an emetic to treat phlegm-related neurological disorders and pediatric conditions.</meta>

# Elaboration
<gloss>Song says:</gloss> Physicians today frequently use this substance to induce the vomiting of "wind-phlegm" <note>a type of phlegm in Traditional Chinese Medicine believed to cause sudden neurological symptoms like dizziness or tremors</note>. The method involves selecting pieces of excellent quality and color, grinding and sifting them through a process of water-refining <note>shuifei: a technique of grinding minerals in water to obtain the finest powder</note>, and then grinding them again. If a patient suffers from a wind-disorder characterized by dizziness and chest oppression, take two or three *qian* <note>approximately 8–12 grams</note> and mix it with three or four bean-sized pieces of raw **Borneol** <note>original: "Longnao," literally "Dragon Brain" camphor</note>. Grind these together until even, then mix with fresh mint juice and warm wine for administration. After the patient lies down, saliva will flow from the corners of the mouth in a short while, and the patient will recover. This method does not cause violent vomiting, and its efficacy is faster than other medicines. People today use it frequently with consistent results, which is why I have recorded it here.

<gloss>Zongshi says:</gloss> When used alongside **Sal Ammoniac** <note>original: "Naosha"</note> to induce vomiting, it is indeed effective at clearing saliva, but it can also cause damage to the Heart-energy.

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> If phlegm is in the upper part of the body, it is appropriate to induce vomiting; if it is in the lower part, it should be drained through the bowels. One must also observe the patient’s constitution—whether they are "empty" or "solid," strong or weak—and examine their pulse before administering this. Chu Yushi’s warning regarding the "Golden Tiger" and "Azure Cloud" formulas carries exactly this meaning. The **Golden Tiger Pill** for treating wind-disorders uses large amounts of **Realgar** <note>an arsenic sulfide mineral</note> and other powdered medicines.

# Appendix of Formulas
<gloss>New</gloss> **Acute Convulsions and Coma:** For cases where the patient is unconscious and unresponsive. Use four *liang* <note>approx. 150g</note> of **Stone Green** <note>Malachite</note> and one *qian* <note>approx. 3.7g</note> of **Calomel** <note>original: "Qingfen," a mercurous chloride mineral</note>. Grind into a powder. Mix a "character’s weight" <note>a tiny amount, roughly the weight of a coin's inscription</note> with mint juice and wine. The goal is to induce vomiting.

**Infant Wind-Disorder with Distress and Oppression:** Use the **Azure Cloud Pill** <note>original: "Bixia Dan"</note>. Take ten *liang* of Stone Green, and seventy pieces each of **Aconite** tips and **Prepared Aconite** tips <note>original: "Wutou" and "Fuzi," both species of Aconitum known for their potent, toxic properties if not handled correctly</note>. Grind into a powder and form into pills the size of a Gorgon nut <note>original: "Qianzi," the Euryale ferox seed</note> using a paste. For each dose, dissolve one pill in half a *he* <note>approx. 50ml</note> of wine mixed with mint juice.

# Underarm Body Odor
For cases that have persisted for a long time without improvement, take equal parts of **Stone Green** <note>Malachite</note> and **Dahurian Angelica** <note>original: "Baizhi," a fragrant root used to reduce swelling and pain</note> and grind them into a fine powder. First, wash the affected area with sweet gum-water <note>likely water infused with licorice or a medicinal resin to cleanse the skin</note>, wipe it dry, and then apply the powder. It should heal within one day. <gloss>From the Collection of Mysterious Prescriptions</gloss>

Alternatively: take three *qian* <note>about 11 grams</note> of **Stone Green** and one *qian* <note>about 3.7 grams</note> of **Calomel** <note>original: "Qingfen," a mercurous chloride mineral used topically for skin ailments</note>. Mix these with concentrated vinegar and apply five times to eliminate the condition at its root.
<gloss>From the Collection of Mysterious Prescriptions</gloss>

# Azurite
->Superior Grade of the Divine Husbandman’s Classic<- <note>The "Superior Grade" refers to the highest class of medicines in ancient Chinese pharmacopoeias, believed to be non-toxic and capable of prolonging life</note>

## Names and Definitions
**Stone Blue** <gloss>from the Compendium of Materia Medica</gloss>, **Great Blue** <gloss>Shizhen says: it is called "Flat Blue" because of its shape</gloss>

## Collection and Explanation
<gloss>The Miscellaneous Records say:</gloss> **Azurite** <note>original: "Bianqing," literally "Flat Blue"</note> is produced in the valleys of Zhuya, Wudu, and Zhuti. It may be gathered at any time.

<gloss>Hongjing says:</gloss> The name "Zhuti" is pronounced *shu-chi*; it is a place located within the South Seas. While the "Classics of the Immortals" <note>Taoist alchemical texts</note> mention it, it is rarely used in common folk prescriptions today.

<gloss>Pu says:</gloss> It is produced in the Shu Commandery <note>modern-day Sichuan</note>.

<gloss>Xiao says:</gloss> This is actually the same substance as **Green Malachite**. Those specimens coming from south of Zhuya, or brought by merchant ships from Linyi and Funan <note>ancient kingdoms in Southeast Asia and Vietnam</note>, consist of large chunks about the size of a fist. Their color is deep blue, and they are sometimes hollow inside. The variety from Wuchang comes in smaller pieces but possesses an even finer color. Those from Jianzhou and Zizhou are flat in shape, forming thin plates, and their color is somewhat lighter.

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> Su Gong’s claim that this is the same as Malachite is incorrect. This substance is what we know today as **Stone Blue** <note>Azurite</note>. It is used by painters as a pigment; its brilliant blue-green color does not fade over time. It is commonly called "Great Blue." It is also found in various parts of Chu and Shu <note>modern Hubei and Sichuan</note>. Currently, those who trade in Stone Blue distinguish between several varieties: "Heavenly Blue," "Great Blue," and the "Western Barbarian" or "Huihui" <note>Persian/Islamic</note> varieties.

...Buddha-head Blue, and various other types. Among these, "Return Blue" <note>original: "Huiqing," referring to high-quality cobalt blue imported from the Islamic world or "Hui" regions</note> is especially precious. The varieties recorded in this pharmacopoeia—Flat Blue, Layered Blue, Beryl Blue, and White Blue—are all within this same category.

**Properties:** Sweet, neutral, and non-toxic. <gloss>Wu Pu says: Shennong and Lei Gong say it is slightly cold and non-toxic.</gloss>
**Primary Indications:** Eye pain; improves vision; treats fractures and falls; heals boils, swellings, and metal wounds <note>injuries caused by blades or tools</note> that refuse to close; breaks up internal accumulations <note>such as tumors or cysts</note>; neutralizes toxic vapors; and sharpens the spirit. Taken over a long period, it makes the body feel light and prevents aging.

<gloss>From the Divine Husbandman’s Classic:</gloss> Removes chills and fevers, "wind-dampness" numbness, and the "hundred diseases" of the male genitalia; increases "essence" <note>vitality or reproductive essence</note>.
<gloss>From the Miscellaneous Records:</gloss> Treats internal exhaustion in men and helps them conceive children.
<gloss>Wu Pu says:</gloss> Induces vomiting to clear "wind-phlegm" and treats epilepsy.
<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> It calms the liver.

### Attached Prescriptions <gloss>New</gloss>

**1. For stubborn phlegm that will not dissolve:** 
Take one tael <note>approx. 37 grams</note> of **Stone Blue** <note>Azurite</note> and half a tael of **Stone Green** <note>Malachite</note>. Refine both with water to create a fine powder. Mix with a leaven paste to form pills the size of mung beans. Take nineteen pills per dose with warm water. This will cause the patient to vomit one or two bowls of phlegm without causing harm to their constitution.

# Explanation of Names: Beryl Blue <gloss>Tang Pharmacopoeia: Fish-Eye Blue</gloss>

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>The *Miscellaneous Records* states: White Blue is produced in the mountain valleys of Yuzhang. It can be harvested at any time. It can be smelted into copper to forge swords that ward off the "five weapons" <note>A traditional grouping of weapons: typically the bow, club, spear, halberd, and sword</note>. 
Hongjing <note>Tao Hongjing, 456–536 CE, a distinguished Taoist and physician</note> says: Medical prescriptions do not use this, and it is not sold in markets. It is occasionally required in the *Thirty-Six Methods for Liquid Gold* <note>An ancient alchemical text describing the liquefaction of minerals</note>. The method for forging copper swords is found in the *Arts of the Nine Primal Masters*. 
Gong <note>Su Gong, lead editor of the Tang Dynasty pharmacopoeia</note> says: This is the substance Master Tao referred to as "Empty Blue" <note>A hollow form of azurite</note> which is round like an iron bead, white in color, and not hollow inside. When ground, the color is white with a beryl tint; thus it is called "Beryl Blue." It is not used in painting. When "Empty Blue" is unavailable, this is used as a substitute. It is named "Fish-Eye Blue" because its shape resembles the eye of a fish. Today, the varieties coming from Jianzhou and Zizhou are considered superior. 
Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen, the author</note> says: This belongs to the same category as Stone Blue <note>Azurite</note>. The variety with a deep color is Stone Blue, while the pale variety is Beryl Blue. Today, painters also use it. *Fan Zi Jiran* <note>An ancient text on commerce and natural resources</note> states that White Blue comes from Hongnong and Yuzhang, and the blue-tinted variety from Xingan is the best. The *Ten Thousand Arts of the King of Huainan* <note>A collection of ancient wonders and proto-chemical experiments</note> says: "When White Blue meets iron, it transforms into copper" <note>This likely describes the chemical reaction where copper ions in a solution are displaced by iron, coating the iron in copper</note>.</gloss>

---

## Nature and Flavor: Sweet, Sour, Salty, Neutral, and Non-toxic
<gloss>Wu Pu says: The Divine Husbandman says it is sweet and neutral. Lei Gong says it is salty and non-toxic.</gloss>

## Primary Indications: Brightens the eyes; benefits the nine orifices <note>The eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, urethra, and anus</note>; treats deafness; clears "evil qi" <note>Pathogenic factors or perceived spiritual disturbances</note> from the chest; induces vomiting; destroys various toxins and the "three worms" <note>A traditional term for various parasites or internal agents of disease</note>. Taken over a long period, it facilitates communication with the "spirit-bright" <note>A state of heightened spiritual and mental clarity</note>.

# Stone Gall <gloss>Superior Grade of the *Divine Farmer’s Classic*</gloss> <note>Stone Gall refers to Chalcantite, a hydrated copper sulfate mineral also known as Blue Vitriol</note>

## Explanation of Names

<gloss>Compendium</gloss> Bile Alum <gloss>Divine Farmer’s Classic</gloss> Black Stone <gloss>Divine Farmer’s Classic</gloss> Greenish Stone <gloss>Divine Farmer’s Classic</gloss> Lord Stone <gloss>Master Dang</gloss> Copper Reins <gloss>Wu Pu</gloss> Established-Regulated Stone

> **Shizhen says:** "Gall" <note>original: 膽, Dan</note> refers to its name based on its color and bitter taste. Because it resembles alum, the common people call it "Bile Alum" <note>Dan-fan</note>.

## Collected Explanations

<gloss>The *Miscellaneous Records* states</gloss> Stone Gall is produced in the mountain valleys of Qiangdao in Qinzhou, found among large rocks, or in the Qiangligu Mountains. It should be harvested on the *Gengzi* and *Xinchou* days <note>Specific days in the traditional sexagenary cycle used in alchemical timing</note> of the second lunar month. When formed as a stone, its color is predominantly blue.

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on Stone Gall (Chalcantite/Copper Sulfate) from the "Metals and Stones" section. It transitions from the basic definitions into detailed commentary from historical experts regarding its alchemical properties, geographical origins, and methods for identifying genuine specimens versus common counterfeits.</meta>

<header>Metals and Stones, Middle Grade: Volume Ten</header>

It has white patterns and is easily broken; its appearance resembles Hollow Blue <note>Azurite</note>. It can transform iron into copper and can be combined to form gold and silver.

**Hongjing says:** <note>Tao Hongjing (456–536 CE), a renowned Taoist alchemist and physician</note>
Alchemical scriptures <note>original: 仙經, Xianjing</note> frequently use this, though it is seldom found in common prescriptions. This medicine has recently become scarce; however, people still occasionally gather it. Its color is a greenish-blue, resembling glass, but it features white patterns and is easily broken. It is no longer found in Liangzhou or Xindu. Common people now substitute it with green-colored alum <note>Melanterite/Iron Sulfate</note>, and they are quite unable to distinguish between the two.

**Zhu says:** <note>Likely referring to a commentator cited in the *Tujing Bencao*</note>
This substance is found in places where copper is produced. Its shape resembles Azurite but is interspersed with green. The flavor is extremely sour and bitter. When rubbed onto iron, it turns the iron the color of copper; this is the sign of a genuine specimen. It is produced in the caves of Dongting Valley and Xueji in Yuxiang County, Puzhou. Pieces the size of a chicken egg are considered of the highest quality.

**Tao remarks:**
The variety that resembles glass is actually Green Alum <term>Melanterite; an iron-based sulfate often confused with copper sulfate</term>. Lately, people have been using it as a substitute. They also use vinegar to rub Green Alum to mimic the appearance of Stone Gall—both of these are counterfeits.

**Song says:** <note>Su Song (1020–1101 CE), author of the *Illustrated Materia Medica*</note>
Today, it is found only in Yanshan County, Xinzhou. It grows within copper pits. It is collected and then boiled and refined to be formed. The variety that forms naturally without such processing is particularly precious; both types are a deep jade-green. Physicians in the South use it frequently today.

A further explanation states: The highest grade of Stone Gall comes from Puzhou. The large pieces are the size of a fist, while the smaller ones are like peaches or chestnuts. When struck, they break apart along longitudinal and transverse lines, revealing layered patterns <note>original: 疊文, diewen</note>. The color is blue-green, but after long use <gloss>or exposure</gloss>, it turns green. When broken open, the interior is also blue. 

The next grade comes from the copper pits of Qujiang in Shangrao. These are fine-grained and possess "fired edges" <note>original: 煎稜, jianleng; likely referring to sharp, crystalline facets formed during natural or heat-assisted crystallization</note> that look like the legs of hairpins or grains of rice. 

The *Materia Medica* states that counterfeits are made by rubbing Green Alum with vinegar, but they are entirely different. One should also look out for those made by mixing crude, inferior Stone Gall with Saltpeter <note>Potassium Nitrate</note> and melting them together; these form large blocks with a pale color. They are cloudy and lack any vein-like grain <term>Pulse lines; the internal crystalline structure of the mineral</term>. If struck, they shatter into fragments and lack the characteristic "fired edges" of the genuine mineral. Additionally, there is the "Bed-Supported Stone," which is obtained by scraping the mineral beds where Stone Gall grows.

<summary>This page details the physical properties of Stone Gall (Chalcantite), emphasizing its ability to "turn iron into copper" and its crystalline structure. It provides a guide for distinguishing genuine copper sulfate from iron-based substitutes and describes the various grades based on their regional origins.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of Stone Gall (Chalcantite), focusing on its geographical origins, methods for identifying authentic specimens versus man-made substitutes, and its medicinal properties. It transitions from the physical descriptions of the previous page into expert commentary from Li Shizhen and other historical scholars.</meta>

<header>Compendium of Materia Medica</header>

...When it is being manufactured, it is thrown into saltpeter; it then solidifies and bonds together. **Shizhen says:** Stone Gall <term>Chalcantite; a naturally occurring copper sulfate mineral</term> is found in the mountain caves of Puzhou. Those with a color like a duck’s beak <note>a deep teal or greenish-blue</note> are considered of the highest quality. The common name for it is Blue Vitriol <note>original: 膽礬, Danfan</note>. Those originating from the Qiang regions are lighter in color and are often counterfeits of lower quality. To test it: if it turns into a liquid juice when burned with fire, it is definitely a fake. If you coat iron or copper with it and it turns red upon heating, it is genuine. Furthermore, if you place a small amount into water within a copper vessel and the water does not turn a deep green-blue or change its appearance even after several days, it is genuine. 

The *Essential Secrets of Wang Dong* states: Stone Gall is a "Yang Stone." It is produced in Mount Song and the Zhongtiao Mountains of Puzhou. It is endowed with a heavy, extraordinary lithic energy and is shaped like a zither or lute. Its nature is one of flow and penetration; its essence can enter other stones and transform the five metals, allowing for infinite hardening and transformation. 

In the *Dream Pool Essays* <note>original: 筆談, Bitan; a famous 11th-century scientific work by Shen Kuo</note>, it is recorded that in Qianshan, there is a "Bitter Spring" that flows into a mountain stream. If one draws this water and boils it down, it becomes Blue Vitriol. The iron pots used for this boiling eventually transform into copper as well. <insert>Li Shizhen notes:</insert> This is vitriol produced by boiling and is a processed counterfeit, not true Stone Gall. It must not be used as medicine.

**Flavor and Nature:** Sour, acrid, cold, and toxic.
<gloss>Shen Nong says: Sour and slightly cold. Li Dangzhi says: Greatly cold. Tong Jun says: Acrid and toxic. Bian Que says: Bitter and non-toxic. Da Ming says: Sour, astringent, and non-toxic. Zhen Quan says: Highly toxic. Xu Zhicai says: It is known as 'Water Essence.'</gloss>

## Primary Indications
Brightening the eyes and treating eye pain.

# Expositions

<margin>Shizhen says:</margin> Stone Gall's <term>Chalcantite; blue vitriol</term> energy is cold, and its flavor is sour and acrid. It enters the Lesser Yang Gallbladder Meridian. Its nature is astringent and its movement is upward; it is capable of inducing the vomiting of wind-heat phlegm and saliva, and it disperses "wind-fire" and "ministerial fire" <note>internal heat associated with the liver and kidneys</note>. It can also kill parasites. Therefore, it has miraculous efficacy in treating sores and toxins of the throat, mouth, and teeth. 

Zhou Mi’s *Rustic Words from Eastern Qi* <note>original: 齊東野語, Qidong Yeyu; a collection of historical anecdotes</note> states: A guest passing through Nanpu was once met by an old physician who provided a formula for treating acute throat obstruction <note>quinsy or severe inflammation</note> so severe the patient was near death. The formula uses powdered "Duck-Beak" Blue Vitriol mixed with vinegar and poured down the throat. This causes the patient to vomit several liters of sticky phlegm, after which they are immediately cured. In Linting, the wife of an old soldier suffered from this same condition and had been unable to swallow water or grain for three days. When treated according to this method, she was cured instantly. In every case where it is used, it proves to be a divine formula with immediate results.

Furthermore, Zhou Bida’s *Record of Hidden Virtue* <note>original: 陰德錄, Yinde Lu</note> says: A secret formula for treating parasitic distention <term>Gu Zhang; abdominal bloating often attributed to parasites or toxins</term> and edema involves using Blue Vitriol from Puzhou or Xinzhou—specifically the bright, clear variety that looks like green lapis lazuli or the iridescent feathers on a duck’s back. It is powdered, boiled in vinegar, and taken alongside "sovereign and minister" herbs <note>the primary and supporting ingredients in a traditional formula</note>. This treatment is superior to using iron filings or iron scales. This is because Blue Vitriol is the "essential fluid" of copper; its flavor is acrid and sour, and it enters the Liver and Gallbladder to regulate the Spleen. Wei Qingchen of Ancheng once suffered from swelling...

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the clinical applications of Stone Gall (Chalcanthite) to the introduction of a new mineral entry: Arsenopyrite. The translation maintains the focus on mineral identification and its alchemical/toxic properties as found in historical Chinese pharmacopeias.</meta>

->Section on Minerals and Stones — Arsenopyrite — Volume 10<-

The *Classic* <note>referring to the *Classic of Mountains and Seas* (Shanhaijing), an ancient geographical and mythological text</note> states that on Mount Jutu there is a white stone called **Arsenopyrite** <term>Arsenopyrite (Yushi); an iron arsenic sulfide mineral historically used as a potent toxin and alchemical agent</term>, which can be used to poison rats. Guo Pu <note>a famous Jin Dynasty scholar and commentator</note> noted that while rats die upon eating it, silkworms grow fat from it; the concept of a "rat's hometown" <note>a paradoxical place where things are reversed</note> originates from this.

---

### Collected Explanations

The *Miscellaneous Records* <note>original: 別録, Bielu</note> state that Arsenopyrite is produced in the valleys of Hanzhong and on Mount Shaoshi. It can be harvested at any time. It is sometimes found on Mount Shaoshi or in Weixing. It should be harvested in the twelfth month.
Hongjing <note>Tao Hongjing, a 5th-century polymath and alchemist</note> says: It is found today in the Shu and Han regions. The finest quality comes from the streams of Nanyang in Nankang and the borders of Pengcheng. It is also found in the southern moats of Ruyang County, east of the lakes in Xinning, and in Lingling. There is a "White Arsenopyrite" that has the power to soften gold. If one wraps it in yellow clay and roasts it in a charcoal fire for a full day and night, it will crumble and become usable. Those practicing the "Arts of the Red Chamber" <note>alchemical laboratory practices</note> and the "Arts of Yellow and White" <note>the transmutation of metals into gold and silver</note> use it frequently.
Gong <note>Su Gong, lead author of the Tang Dynasty's official pharmacopeia</note> says: This stone resists fire. Even after long roasting, it merely crumbles but does not lose its hardness. Today, merchants often take "Patterned Stone" <note>original: 理石, Lishi; likely a form of gypsum or calcite</note> and sell it as Arsenopyrite; however, that stone turns to ash when burned. The true source is the Arsenopyrite Valley at the Far Slope west of Wudang in Hanchuan. The variety from Mount Shaoshi has a fine grain but is not as good as that from Hanzhong.
Song <note>Su Song, a Song Dynasty scientist</note> says: It is also found in Liangzhou and Fangzhou today.
Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen, author of the Compendium of Materia Medica</note> says: Detailed information can be found under the entry for "Bull-sized Arsenopyrite."

---

# Nature and Flavor
## Acrid, Greatly Hot, and Toxic

The *Miscellaneous Records* state: Sweet, warm, and intensely hot.
Lei Gong <note>original: 普, Pu; likely referring to the scholar Lei Xiao or records by Wu Pu</note> says: According to Shennong and Qi Bo <note>mythical ancestors of Chinese medicine</note>, it is acrid and toxic. According to Tong Jun and the Yellow Emperor, it is sweet and toxic.
Zhen Quan says: It is sweet and slightly toxic. It uses *Fudan* <note>an alchemical preparation of lead and cinnabar</note> as its catalyst. It is repelled by sheep's blood and should not be included in liquid decoctions.
Lei Xiao <note>original: 才, Cai; likely referring to the pharmacologist Lei Xiao</note> says: It reaches its full potential when exposed to fire. It uses thorn needles <note>likely from the Gleditsia tree</note> as its catalyst. It is repelled by "horse eyes" <unclear>possibly a specific mineral form or plant seed</unclear>, tiger's paw <note>the toxic plant Arisaema</note>, and Asarum. It fears water.

It outlines the mineral's toxic nature and the specific substances that act as catalysts or inhibitors to its potency.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the entry on Arsenopyrite (Yushi), transitioning from its geographical origins and alchemical preparation to its specific medicinal indications and historical commentaries on its extreme toxicity and thermal nature.</meta>

->**Compendium of Materia Medica** — Page 25<-

**Mainly treats:** Chills and fever, scrofula <note>original: 鼠瘻, shulou; cervical lymphadenitis, historically compared to "rat-fistulas"</note>, the erosion of dead flesh, wind-damp numbness, stubborn abdominal masses, and malevolent influences <note>original: 邪氣, xieqi; pathological factors or "evil winds"</note> <gloss>The Classic</gloss>. It removes heat, brightens the eyes, descends energy (*qi*), and clears heat within the diaphragm. It stops "thirst-extinguishing" disease <note>original: 消渴, xiaoke; symptoms corresponding to diabetes</note>, benefits the liver energy, breaks up chronic accumulations and deep-seated cold abdominal pain, and removes nasal polyps. Long-term consumption causes the muscles to contract. If refined by fire for a hundred days, it may be taken in a "one-spatula" dose <term>one-spatula (daogui); an ancient Chinese measurement for medicinal powders, approximately 1/10th of a square-inch spoonful</term>. If taken unrefined, it kills humans and all hundred beasts <gloss>Miscellaneous Records</gloss>. It removes accumulated energy between the chest and diaphragm, and clears long-standing cold-dampness, wind-numbness, scrofula, and itching <gloss>Zhen Quan</gloss>.

## Elucidations

<gloss>Hongjing <note>Tao Hongjing, 5th-century alchemist</note> says: If one takes raw Arsenopyrite and places it in water, it prevents the water from freezing. Based on this, its raw nature is also extremely hot. Zhang Zhongjing <note>the "Sage of Medicine" (150–219 AD)</note> says: Using it raw destroys a person’s heart and liver.</gloss>
<gloss>Gong <note>Su Gong, 7th-century editor of the *Tang Materia Medica*</note> says: This medicine is excellent for attacking diseases of accumulated cold. If other substances are substituted for it, the treatment will be ineffective; it is specifically for this purpose.</gloss>
<gloss>Zongshi <note>Kou Zongshi, Song Dynasty pharmacologist</note> says: In treating long-standing accumulations and abdominal cold from chronic illness, it is indeed effective. However, one must be extremely cautious; its toxicity is such that it must not be tested lightly.</gloss>
<gloss>Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen, author of this work</note> says: The nature and energy of Arsenopyrite are close to Arsenic <term>Arsenic (Pishi); arsenic trioxide, a highly lethal mineral toxin</term>; it belongs to the same category. In ancient prescriptions, the characters for Arsenopyrite (*Yushi*) and Alum (*Fanshi*) were often confused in writing because the two characters look very similar.</gloss>

<summary>This page details the therapeutic applications of Arsenopyrite for cold-related abdominal masses and scrofula, while emphasizing its extreme heat and lethal toxicity if not properly refined. Historical commentators warn of its capacity to destroy internal organs and its chemical similarity to arsenic.</summary>
<keywords>Arsenopyrite, Yushi, scrofula, thermal nature, mineral toxicity, Li Shizhen, Zhang Zhongjing, alchemical refining, ancient measurements</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Arsenopyrite (Yushi), moving from its basic properties into historical anecdotes and a debate on its toxicity versus its misuse by the social elite.</meta>

->**Arsenopyrite — Volume 10**<-

...therefore, it was simply an error. However, [some sources claim] Arsenopyrite is cold in nature and non-toxic, while [others claim] it is hot and toxic; one must not fail to recognize the harm it can cause.

Lu Nongshi <note>Lu Dian, a Song dynasty scholar</note> says: "The potency of Arsenopyrite is ten times that of Stalactite." According to Hong Rongzhai’s <note>Hong Mai, 1123–1202 AD</note> *Random Notes*: Wang Zijing <note>Wang Xianzhi, a famous 4th-century calligrapher</note> mentioned in his *Letter on Quiet Resting* that Arsenopyrite is deeply suspicious. Generally, those who enjoyed "the Powder" <note>original: 散, san; referring to the "Cold Food Powder," a toxic alchemical drug popular among the elite</note> would frequently develop carbuncles. This is because "the Powder" refers to Cold Food Powder <term>Hanshisan; a potent, heat-inducing drug used by ancient scholars that often led to arsenic poisoning</term>. The ancients consumed it often. It contained Arsenopyrite, which is hot and toxic. 

Therefore, when Liu Biao <note>a warlord of the late Han dynasty</note> was in Jingzhou, he climbed Mount Zhang with Wang Can. They spotted a ridge where no plants or grasses grew. Wang Can said: "This must be an ancient tomb. The person buried here must have consumed raw Arsenopyrite during their life. The heat does not escape outward, and thus the vegetation is scorched and destroyed." Liu Biao had the site excavated, and indeed, Arsenopyrite filled the grave. Furthermore, the Luo River does not freeze today because there is Arsenopyrite beneath it; people call this the "Warm Luo." If one takes this stone and places it in a water jar, the water will not freeze. It is also said that when cranes hatch their eggs, they take this stone and place it in the nest to assist with the warmth. Such is its nature.

How then could it be consumed lightly? My elder brother, Lord Wen'an <note>Hong Kuo, the brother of the author Hong Mai, whom Li Shizhen is quoting here</note>, was stationed in Jinling. During the autumn heat, he lost his appetite. The physician Tang Sanyi saved him by administering Arsenopyrite pills. Afterward, his eating and drinking improved daily, so he took the medicine with even greater intent. After ten months, the toxin manifested. He bled from the nose more than a *dou* <term>dou; an ancient peck, roughly 10 liters, indicating a massive hemorrhage</term>. From then on, the bleeding occurred frequently without stopping, eventually leading to the exhaustion of his vital essence and death.

[Li] Shizhen privately suggests: Lord Wen'an’s illness was not necessarily the eruption of Arsenopyrite toxin alone. It was also because he relied on his robust appetite to indulge in rich, heavy flavors and sexual overindulgence without restraint, leading to the exhaustion of his essence and death. If one suffers from a loss of appetite and takes the stone, once the appetite returns and the illness is gone, the medicine should be stopped. Yet, if one continues to take it without ceasing, relying on the medicine to act recklessly—is that truly the fault of the medicine?

<summary>This page discusses the extreme heat and toxicity of Arsenopyrite through historical anecdotes, including its presence in the notorious "Cold Food Powder" and its legendary ability to prevent water from freezing. It concludes with a debate on whether the mineral's dangers stem from its inherent nature or from human overindulgence and medicinal misuse.</summary>

# Singularly-formed Arsenopyrite
<gloss>From the *Records of Famous Physicians*; Lower Grade</gloss>

<column-break/>

## Explanation of Names
Arsenopyrite
Arsenopyrite <gloss>From the *Records of Famous Physicians*</gloss>; also called "Rat Poison."
<gloss>Su Gong says:</gloss> "Singularly-formed" Arsenopyrite <note>original: 特生礜石, Tesheng Yushi</note> refers to the variety from Liangzhou. It is red with hints of blue.

<column-break/>

The people of Hanzhong also use it to poison rats; however, it is not used in medicinal prescriptions.
<gloss>Kou Zongshi says:</gloss> Arsenopyrite and "Singularly-formed" Arsenopyrite are essentially the same substance. The difference lies only in whether the stone grows independently or not. Those that are not attached to other rocks are called "singularly-formed." Today, this variety is exceedingly rare.
<gloss>Li Shizhen says:</gloss> Arsenopyrite comes in two varieties: gray

<column-break/>

and white. The gray variety is most often found growing independently; therefore, it is also called "Gray Arsenopyrite." In the *Records of Famous Physicians*, the entry for "Gray Stone" is actually a redundant entry for the same substance. Their therapeutic powers are identical, so I have combined them here into a single entry.

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>The *Records of Famous Physicians* says:</gloss> Singularly-formed Arsenopyrite, also known as Gray Arsenopyrite, is produced in the Western Regions. It may be gathered at any time.
<gloss>Tao Hongjing says:</gloss> According to old legends, this stone is found in magpie nests.

<column-break/>

Because magpies frequently enter cold water, they are said to take these stones into their nests to warm their eggs <note>this refers to the belief that the stone’s intense "heat" nature aided incubation</note>. Such stones are no longer obtainable today. Now, we only have the variety from Hanzhong. Its exterior is purplish-red, while its interior is as white as frost. In the center, there is a white shape resembling...
