...those shaped like teeth are of the best quality. It is also produced in Fangling County, Xincheng Commandery, in Jingzhou. Those that are pale white or reddish are considered high quality. They should first be wrapped in yellow clay and roasted <note>calcined</note> for a day; alternatively, they can be placed inside a cauldron and roasted. Today, various alchemical texts like the *Jade Casket* do not use the term "Singularly-formed," referring to it only as White Arsenopyrite.

<gloss>Su Gong says:</gloss> "Those described by Tao Hongjing as having tooth-like 'phantom shapes' are indeed the ones found today in the Cheng Stream of Madao, north of Liangzhou. Their lumps are smaller than White Arsenopyrite, but the grains of their texture are several times larger—about the size of small beans. The grains of White Arsenopyrite, by contrast, are as fine as millet. Today, in Fangling, Hanchuan, Junzhou, and Jingzhou, it is found in the same locations as White Arsenopyrite. Those that are blue-green in color are this variety."

<gloss>Kou Zongshi says:</gloss> "The *Records of Diverse Matters* <note>original: *Bowu Zhi*, a 3rd-century collection of wonders and geography</note> states that when storks hatch their eggs, they place Arsenopyrite in the nest to provide warmth. Alchemists consider stones obtained from such nests to be the genuine article. Since Tao Hongjing used this lore to annotate 'Singularly-formed' Arsenopyrite, it is clear that these two stones are the same substance. However, I have repeatedly searched stork nests and found no such stones. Furthermore, how could Arsenopyrite be found everywhere [that storks are]? If one argues that storks take this stone because they enter cold water, then storks, magpies, and their kind—all of which feed in water—would naturally reproduce and thrive without it. This is merely the talk of common folk who have never investigated the facts or exhausted the logic of the matter."

<gloss>Li Shizhen says:</gloss> "There are several kinds of Arsenopyrite: White, Gray, Purple, Red-skinned, Peach-blossom, Golden-speckled, Silver-speckled, and 'Singularly-formed' Arsenopyrite. They are all the same substance, merely named differently based on their shape and color. Their nature is universally 'hot' and toxic; they can all be used to poison rats or to 'control' <note>stabilize or transmute</note> mercury. 

Only the gray and white varieties are used in medicine. Whenever Arsenopyrite is found in the mountains, vegetation will not grow there, and frost and snow will not accumulate. If it is found in water, the water will not freeze, or there may be hot springs nearby. This is because its Qi <note>vital energy or internal heat</note> is..."

Li Shizhen concludes that all varieties are essentially the same toxic, "hot" mineral used for rat poison and alchemy.</summary>

->**Imperially Commissioned *Complete Library in the Four Sections***<-
->***Compendium of Materia Medica*, Volume 9, Minerals and Stones**<-

...its heat is readily apparent. The *Gengxin Jade Casket* <note>An alchemical manual from the early Ming Dynasty attributed to Prince Zhu Quan</note> states that it is also known as the "Stone of the Sun." It is found in the mud within mountain valleys and waters. It resembles alum and is of the best quality when it possesses a patterned texture that can be cut crosswise through the middle. It is used to subdue <note>alchemically stabilize</note> processed cinnabar and mercury. Its appearance is quite similar to calcite, but the genuine article is distinguished by the fact that when placed in water, the water will not freeze. Those specimens found within gold mines are specifically called "Vibrating Snow Arsenopyrite."

**Properties:** Sweet and warm in nature; poisonous. <gloss>Zhicai says: It is best when refined by fire; it fears water.</gloss> **Indications:** It brightens the eyes and benefits the ears. It treats extreme internal cold within the abdomen, breaks up hard masses or obstructions <note>often referring to tumors or internal swellings</note>, and heals scrofulous sores <note>tuberculous cervical lymphadenitis, historically called "rat leprosies"</note>. It is used to kill various insects and fierce beasts. Long-term consumption is said to extend life. <gloss>*Supplementary Records*</gloss>. Blue-green stone <note>a variety of Arsenopyrite</note> primarily treats chills and fever, directs energy <term>Qi</term> downward, treats eroding sores, and kills birds and beasts. <gloss>*Supplementary Records*</gloss>.

**Exposition:** <gloss>Shizhen says: The *Supplementary Records* state that long-term consumption of Arsenopyrite causes the tendons to waste away, yet also claims that the "Singularly-formed" variety allows one to live longer. Alchemical texts also claim that Arsenopyrite can be turned into a liquid that subdues mercury and can be refined into elixirs of immortality. These are all the fallacious claims of alchemists <term>Fangshi</term>. Following such advice is no different from eating arsenic in hopes of seeking eternal life.</gloss>

# Collected Explanations

Su Gong <note>A Tang Dynasty official and lead editor of the *Revised Materia Medica*</note> states: "Grasping-Snow" [Arsenopyrite] <gloss>It is obtained from between rocks, appearing as a fine powder like flour. It is yellowish-white, and the locals call it "Grasping-Snow."</gloss> This Arsenopyrite originates from Songli Mountain in Xuzhou. It is found by digging more than ten feet into the earth within decomposed layers.

Arsenopyrite is also known as "Chemical Transformation Stone" or "Stone Brain." It is said that consuming it leads to immortality. Li Shizhen states: I have carefully examined Dugu Tao’s *Mirror of the Alchemical Laboratory* <note>A 10th-century manual on "external alchemy" or proto-chemistry</note>, which says that "Stone Brain" is also called "Stone Essence." It originates from the marshes of Qutan. During the peak of winter, a "marrow" <note>likely a mineral efflorescence or precipitate</note> grows upon the rocks and can be harvested. One *fen* <note>approximately 0.4 grams</note> of this substance can solidify ten *liang* <note>approximately 370 grams</note> of mercury.

Furthermore, the *Sacred Book of the Nangong Conglou* states: "Stone Liquid" is the fatty essence of cinnabar alum. This stone is found at Qutan in Xiangyang, occurring either in the mountains or in the water. Its color is white, and its texture is coarse yet sticky like glutinous rice. During the winter months, a fatty liquid emerges. This "essence" appears at dawn but retreats when it sees the sun. One must use a copper knife to scrape it off before sunrise and place it in a vessel. It is then roasted until it is glowing red, after which the juice is extracted and formed into pills. 

Wherever this liquid touches, it turns the color of iron. A single *zhu* <note>a tiny unit, roughly 0.6 grams</note> of this liquid can control four *liang* of mercury; if heated in a vessel, the mercury dries instantly. However, this liquid is rarely found, as it is a substance guarded by divine providence. When harvesting it, one must offer a sacrifice of a white chicken and clear wine. 

While this stone is found in both Mount Hua and Mount Song, only the specimens from Qutan produce this fatty liquid. Additionally, Xiong Taigu’s *Ji-Yue Collection* also mentions that ten *liang* of "Cinnabar Mountain Alum" can dry ten *liang* of mercury. This represents the pinnacle of the human investigation of things <term>Gewu</term> <note>A Neo-Confucian concept of studying the physical world to understand universal principles</note> and the uncovering of the secrets of Heaven and Earth. 

The references in Master Yu’s book suggest that "Grasping-Snow Arsenopyrite" is actually a liquid form of the stone, rather than the "Stone Brain" found in the earth. What Su Gong described is indeed "Stone Brain." His account matches the *Supplementary Records* and the annotations of Tao Hongjing regarding Stone Brain; therefore, it should not have been re-categorized under this entry. Furthermore, according to various books, it is sometimes written as...

The character for *Yushi* <note>Arsenopyrite</note> is sometimes written as 礜 and sometimes as 礜; it is unclear which is correct. In ancient books, these two characters are frequently confused. Based on logic, it seems it should be the version with the "yu" radical <note>original: 礜</note>, as this mineral is toxic, whereas Alum <term>Fanshi</term> is non-toxic.

**Flavor and Nature:** Sweet, warm, and non-toxic. <note>This "non-toxic" classification reflects older alchemical traditions that Li Shizhen is quoting, though he elsewhere warns of its dangers.</note> **Main Indications:** Chronic cold-natured accumulations and masses. It makes the body light and extends life. However, eating too much causes a person to...

**Cooked** <gloss>From the *Tang Materia Medica*</gloss> **Treats "Great Wind" sores** <note>A historical term often referring to leprosy or severe dermatological conditions</note> <gloss>Added by Li Shizhen</gloss>.

# Arsenic Stone <gloss>From the *Kaibao Materia Medica* of the Song Dynasty</gloss>

## **Explanation of Names**
"Xin Stone" <gloss>From the *Compendium of Materia Medica*</gloss>. People say the raw form is called "Arsenic Yellow" <term>Pihuang</term> and the refined form is called "Arsenic Frost" <term>Pishuang</term>. <gloss>Li Shizhen states:</gloss> The nature of Arsenic <term>Pi</term> is as fierce as the *Pi* <note>A legendary fierce beast, often compared to a leopard or tiger</note>, hence the name. Because it is exclusively produced in Xinzhou, people call it "Xin Stone." Furthermore, the name "Human Speech" <term>Renyan</term> is used as a cryptic way to hide the word "Xin" <note>The Chinese character for "Trust" or "Xin" (信) is composed of the radicals for "Person" (人) and "Speech" (言).</note>.

## **Collected Explanations**
<gloss>Su Song states:</gloss> The original records did not specify the districts or counties where Arsenic Frost is produced. Today, it is found near copper mines, but the variety from Xinzhou is superior. Some pieces are extremely large, with a color like goose-egg yellow. They are clear and transparent. True large pieces of this quality are difficult to obtain even in their place of origin; people compete to prize them, valuing them at no less than a thousand pieces of gold. Ancient formulas for longevity also recorded its use, but one must obtain this specific variety for it to be effective...

The "meat" <note>the inner mineral body</note> may have pale white veins. It is said to be "not quite stone, yet not quite earth." Ground with wine, it is used to treat stagnant qi <term>jiqi</term> <note>a traditional concept referring to blocked energy or physical masses in the abdomen</note>. However, once it comes into contact with fire, it becomes toxic and must not be consumed lightly. The method of extraction involves placing the raw arsenic over a fire...

<column-break/>

Kou Zongshi <note>original: 宗奭; a famous Song Dynasty physician known for his critical approach to materia medica</note> says: Nowadays, it is taken from the pits and wells of Xinzhou. These pits are usually kept under lock and key. Inside the pit is a turbid green water; one must first pump the water out completely before descending to chisel out the raw arsenic. This is called "Arsenic Yellow" <term>Pihuang</term>; its color is like an ox-bezoar <note>original: 牛[黃]; a precious yellow gallstone found in cattle</note>.

<column-break/>

All the plants and trees near [the mine] wither and die. It is also mixed with rice to poison rats; if a cat or dog eats a rat killed this way, they will also die. Its toxicity far exceeds that of Wild Kudzu <term>Yege</term> <note>original: 野葛; refers to *Gelsemium elegans*, one of the most toxic plants in ancient Chinese records</note>. The variety produced in Mount Heng is somewhat inferior in potency to the variety from Xinzhou.

<column-break/>

Those who consume the refined "Arsenic Frost" <note>the sublimated white powder obtained through heating</note> will surely suffer violent vomiting and diarrhea. While some may luckily find relief, the number of people harmed by it is extremely high; one cannot be too cautious. When first burning the frost, workers must stand more than ten *zhang* <note>about 30 meters</note> upwind; those who stand downwind...

<column-break/>

The medicine is primarily used by alchemists who refine minerals. Recently, many people use it to treat malaria <term>nüe</term>, based on the logic that malaria is caused by summer heat exhaustion, and the raw form of this mineral can resolve "heat toxins." However, contemporary folk doctors do not investigate the underlying principles and immediately...

<column-break/>

...the stone is burned and the smoke flies upward to become "White Frost." This substance consists of fragments and needle-like splinters. Those pieces that have been more heavily processed by fire form large blocks that are slightly yellow—this is the so-called "goose-egg yellow" and "transparent" variety. Ancient formulas did not include this substance at all.

<column-break/>

The variety that is not mixed with ordinary stone is redder than Realgar <term>Xionghuang</term>. When ground with cold water, it resolves heat toxins, but if it comes near fire, it kills people immediately. This is the variety said to be "worth no less than the price of gold." Nowadays, market traders take the variety mixed with sand from the mountains...

<column-break/>

...and claim it can be used in medicinal doses, but taking it causes no small amount of harm. The government seals and prohibits access to them very strictly. The raw...

Cover the vessel so that the smoke rises and adheres to the container, condensing into hanging clusters like milk-white stalactites. Those with pointed tips are considered superior for medicinal use; short, flat ones are secondary. Large blocks are considered low grade, and pieces shaped like thin eyebrows are the lowest quality. ### Preparation and Processing

Lei Xiao says: Whenever using this substance, place it in a small porcelain jar. Add Purple-backed Heavenly Sunflower <term>zibei tiankui</term> <note>original: 紫背天葵; *Begonia fimbristipula*</note> and Cursed Buttercup <term>shilongrui</term> <note>original: 石龍芮; *Ranunculus sceleratus*</note>. Calcine it <note>heat to high temperatures without melting</note> from the hour of the Snake until the hour of the Monkey <note>roughly 9:00 AM to 5:00 PM</note>. Then, soak it in licorice water from the hour of the Monkey until the hour of the Rat <note>5:00 PM to midnight</note>. Take it out and wipe it dry, return it to the jar and calcine it again. Separately, grind it thirty thousand times. Li Shizhen says: Herbalists all say that raw arsenic is mild, but once it encounters fire, the toxicity becomes extreme. Yet Master Lei’s method uses fire-calcining. Most of what is used today is "fly-refined" <note>sublimated into a fine powder</note> because people desire quick results and disregard the poison. Would it not be better to use it in its raw state?

### Nature and Flavor

**Bitter, sour, warm, and toxic.**

Li Shizhen says: It is pungent, sour, extremely hot, and highly toxic. Da Ming says: It is "feared" by <note>neutralized by</note> mung beans and cold water. If used in medicine, boiling it with sour ingredients can neutralize the poison.

The True Lord of Tusu <note>a legendary Daoist alchemist</note> says: Arsenic Stone can be processed with herbs to produce "golden flowers" or turned into a liquid. It can be used to "fix" mercury <note>an alchemical process to make mercury solid</note>. It is prepared using green salt, "crane-crest" grass, saltpeter, garlic, water smartweed, *Changshan* <note>*Dichroa febrifuga*</note>, motherwort, broom cypress, water-willow, and sweet flag.

## Main Indications
Yellow Arsenic <term>pihuang</term> treats malaria and kidney *qi*. Carrying it on the body repels fleas and lice. <gloss>Da</gloss> Ming says: Grinding it with cold water and swallowing it relieves heat-toxins and treats phlegm congestion. <gloss>Chen</gloss> Cheng says: Grinding and swallowing it treats abdominal masses <term>piji</term> <note>original: 癖積; hard lumps or obstructions in the abdomen</note> and accumulated *qi*. <gloss>Zong</gloss>shi says: It eliminates wheezing, chronic dysentery, and necrotic flesh.

</margin>

Those with a "deficient" stomach <note>referring to a weak digestive system or low vitality in the gut</note> must strictly avoid this substance. **[Li] Shizhen** states: Arsenic is a drug of extreme heat and extreme toxicity. Among its forms, the toxicity of Arsenic Frost <note>original: 砒霜; refined white arsenic</note> is especially violent. If rats or sparrows eat even a tiny amount, they die instantly; if cats or dogs eat those rats or sparrows, they too will perish. For a human, a dose of about one *qian* <term>qian</term> <note>original: 錢; a traditional unit of weight, approximately 3.7 grams</note> is fatal. Even the lethal power of Heartbreak Grass <note>original: 鉤吻; *Gelsemium elegans*</note> or Aconite poison <note>original: 射罔</note> does not exceed this.

Why then did the authors of the Song Dynasty *Materia Medica* <note>referring to earlier pharmacopeias like the *Kaibao Bencao*</note> not speak more forcefully about its toxicity? This is because it is a variety of the ancient "Arsenic Stone" <note>original: 礜石 *yushi*; arsenopyrite</note>. If this substance meets with grain wine or distilled spirits, it will cause the stomach and intestines to rot and decay, killing a person in an instant. In such a case, even mung beans or cold water will find it difficult to provide an antidote. 

Today, those who collect bottled wine often use arsenic smoke to fumigate the bottles so the wine does not spoil. How can they be so heartless and cruel? Those who drink such wine and secretly suffer the poison usually end up blaming the wine itself rather than the arsenic. 

This substance must not be included in boiled decoctions or medicinal drinks; it should only be used in the form of pills or pellets. For chronic malaria or "whooping" asthma <note>original: 齁喘 *houchuan*; likely referring to severe wheezing or respiratory distress</note>, using this drug truly has the power to "hijack" the illness and produce immediate results. However, it must be swallowed with cold water. One must not eat or drink anything hot afterward. After taking it, one should lie still for a day or a night; if this is done, the patient will not vomit. If even a small amount of something is used to "trigger" it <note>such as warm food</note>, vomiting will occur immediately. 

Its nature—intense, dry, and refined—is identical to the flames of distilled spirits. It acts by "hijacking" the cold vapors and damp-phlegm in the body, causing the pent-up congestion to suddenly break open. We can see its fierce nature in how modern pyrotechnicians use a small amount of it to make their firecrackers produce a much louder blast. 

This medicine is only suitable for those "dwellers of the mountains and fields" who eat a simple diet of coarse greens <note>original: 藜藿 *lihuo*</note>. For those who indulge in wine and "rich fat and fine grains" <note>original: 膏粱 *gaoliang*; the wealthy who eat a heavy, greasy diet</note>, it is not appropriate. In such cases, the disease will return because the patient cannot restrain their appetite. Finally, for head sores or any sores where blood is present, this must never be used; if the poison enters the meridians <note>the body's energy channels</note>, it will certainly kill. 

The *Collection of Rare Formulas* by **Li Lou** <note>a Ming dynasty physician</note> tells of a woman who suffered from "heart pain" <note>likely epigastric or chest pain</note> for several years without cure. A doctor used "human word" <note>original: 人言 *renyan*; a common euphemism for arsenic/arsenic frost</note>...

<summary>Li Shizhen warns of the extreme lethality of Arsenic Frost, particularly its fatal interaction with alcohol, while detailing strict protocols for its limited use in treating severe asthma and malaria in patients with robust constitutions.</summary>
<keywords>Arsenic Frost, Li Shizhen, toxicity, malaria, asthma, medical ethics, contraindications, Li Lou</keywords>

## Stroke with Phlegm Congestion
<note>This describes a state where the patient is paralyzed and unconscious due to fluid buildup in the chest or throat.</note>
Where the four limbs cannot be moved and the patient is dazed and confused as if drunk: take a piece of Arsenic Frost <note>original: 砒霜; white arsenic</note> the size of a soy bean and grind it. Mix it with freshly drawn water and swallow; follow this immediately with hot water. Once the patient vomits profusely, they will recover. If they do not vomit, administer another dose.
->*Formulas of Imperial Benevolence* <note>original: *Sheng Hui Fang*; a 10th-century medical encyclopedia</note><-

## Cold and Heat Malaria
->*Sun Zhenren’s Ancestral Secret Proven Formulas* <note>Sun Zhenren refers to the "King of Medicine," Sun Simiao</note><-
Take two *liang* <term>liang</term> <note>original: 兩; approximately 37 grams</note> of refined arsenic and grind separately into a powder. Take an iron ladle and spread a layer of stone powder <note>likely lime or talc used as a buffer</note> at the bottom. Place the arsenic on top, then cover it with another thick layer of stone powder. Seal the ladle with vinegar paste and ten layers of silk strips. Fire it using one *jin* <term>jin</term> <note>original: 斤; approximately 500 grams</note> of charcoal. When the paper strips turn black, remove it and let it cool. Scrape off the arsenic powder from the lid and grind it finely. Mix with millet to form pills the size of mung beans. Use Cinnabar <note>original: 辰砂; mercuric sulfide</note> as a coating. 

For each dose, use three or four pills (one pill for children). Take on the morning of a "Gui" day <note>a day ending in 'Gui' in the sexagenary cycle, considered auspicious for certain treatments</note> with a clear tea infusion. The patient must not eat hot food for the entire day. If a man is suffering, a woman should place the medicine in his mouth; if a woman is suffering, a man should place it in her mouth. 
->*Formulas for Universal Benefit* <note>original: *Ben Shi Fang*</note><-

Take one *qian* <term>qian</term> <note>original: 錢; approximately 3.7 grams</note> of "Human Speech" <note>original: 人言; a common euphemism for arsenic, as the character for arsenic (砒) looks like "human" (人) and "speech" (言) combined</note> and one *liang* of mung bean powder. Grind them together. Form pills the size of mung beans using "rootless well water" <note>water caught before it hits the ground or the bottom of the well, believed to be purer</note>. Use Red Lead <term>huangdan</term> <note>original: 黃丹; lead tetroxide</note> as a coating and dry in the shade. On the day the malaria is expected to strike, take five to seven pills with cold water at the fifth watch <note>between 3:00 AM and 5:00 AM</note>.
->*Precious Mirror for Defending Health* <note>original: *Wei Sheng Bao Jian*</note><-

### One Snip of Gold
Use "Human Speech" (Arsenic), vinegar-boiled Sulfur, and mung beans in equal parts. Grind into a powder. Use a piece of red silk to wrap a portion about the size of a bean, tying it securely with colored silk thread. For each dose, snip off one "pill" and swallow with freshly drawn water on an empty stomach.

# Main indications: Withering tumors, warts, hemorrhoids, breast abscesses, fistulas, scrofula, and all types of sores with malignant flesh <note>Shizhen</note>

<column-break/>

## Golden Star Stone <note>Song Dynasty Jiayou era; includes Silver Star Stone as an appendix</note>

<column-break/>

**Collected Explanations** Song <note>referring to Su Song, a Song Dynasty scholar and pharmacologist</note> says: Golden Star Stone and Silver Star Stone both come from Haozhou and Bingzhou. They are collected regardless of the season. The bodies of these two stones are similar. Zongshi <note>referring to Kou Zongshi, author of the *Bencao Yanyi*</note> says: These two stones treat leprosy <term>great wind disease</term> <note>original: 大風疾; a historical term for leprosy or severe, disfiguring skin conditions</note>. There is a specific

<column-break/>

method for their use: they must be calcined <note>heated to a high temperature to make them brittle and chemically active</note> before use. Golden Star Stone grows within a grey-green stone; both the inside and outside have golden flakes like wheat bran. Another variety is deep blue, hard and moist, with golden spots like stars. The Silver Star Stone has silver flakes like wheat bran.

<column-break/>

The varieties with large bran-like flakes are not used in medicine; craftsmen grind them to make vessels or women's hair ornaments. Shizhen says: There are several types of Golden Star stones. The two stones mentioned by Su Song are also found in Mount Wudang. Some say

<column-break/>

Golden Star Stone comes from Jiaodong and Silver Star Stone from Yanmen; they are likely types of Chlorite Schist <term>Mengshi</term> <note>original: 礞石; a micaceous mineral often used in Chinese medicine to "strike down" phlegm</note>. Regarding the two stones Kou Zongshi mentioned for treating leprosy, an examination of the "Great Wind" section of the *Sage's Prescriptions* <note>original: 聖惠方; referring to the *Taiping Shenghui Fang* compiled in 992 CE</note> shows they are recorded as Golden Star

<column-break/>

Chlorite Schist and Silver Star Chlorite Schist; thus, they seem to be varieties of Chlorite Schist. The *Source of Alchemical Formulas* <note>original: 丹方鑑源; *Danfang Jianyuan*, an alchemical text</note> also records the names of these two stones in the chapter on Chlorite Schist, though they seem different from those described by Su Song. Furthermore, Golden Star and Silver Star stones are non-toxic

<column-break/>

and primarily treat heat-phlegm and blood diseases. In contrast, Chlorite Schist is toxic and primarily treats "wind-madness" <note>likely referring to epilepsy or severe psychiatric episodes</note>. Based on this, there are two distinct types each of Golden Star and Silver Star stones used in medicine. Additionally, the stones used for inkstones in Qinzhou also feature Golden Star and Silver Star patterns.

Bozhou <note>a historical region in modern-day Zunyi, Guizhou</note> also produces Golden Star Stone, all varieties of which can be fashioned into inkstones. ### Appended Prescriptions <gloss>Two new recipes</gloss>

**Vomiting and Coughing Blood** <gloss>For cases of lung damage: use Golden Star Stone, Silver Star Stone, Selenite <term>Xuanjingshi</term>, Asbestos <term>Buhuimu</term> <note>literally "wood that does not turn to ash"</note>, Actinolite <term>Yangqishi</term>, and Mica <term>Yunmushi</term> in equal portions. Use a single crucible and layer the bottom with one or two inches of winter water-buffalo dung. Lay a layer of the medicinal ingredients, then another layer of dung, repeating this process. Cover the top with ash and seal the vessel securely with salt-enriched mud. Use a full scale's weight of charcoal to calcine <note>heat to high temperatures to refine or purify</note> the vessel for one full day and night. Afterward, bury it in the earth for one night. Remove the mineral lumps, clear away any remaining ash, and grind the minerals into a fine powder. For every ounce <term>tael</term> of powder, add half a mace <term>qian</term> each of Borneol <term>Longnao</term> and Musk <term>Shexiang</term>, and two and a half mace of Donkey-hide gelatin <term>Ejiao</term> (fried). For each dose, take one mace of the mixture with glutinous rice water.</gloss>

## Sores from "Great Wind" and Parasites
In cases where there are "five-colored worms" <note>This refers to severe skin conditions like leprosy, which traditional Chinese medicine often attributed to "worms" or pathogens that consumed the flesh</note>. Take the following nine stones:
Limonite <term>Jinyuliangshi</term>, Talc <term>Huashi</term>, Actinolite <term>Yangqishi</term>, Magnetite <term>Cishi</term>, Calcite <term>Ningshuishi</term>, Litharge <term>Mituoseng</term>, Pyrite <term>Zirantong</term>, and "Dragon Saliva Stone" <term>Longxianshi</term>. Take equal portions of each. Grind them into a fine powder and place them into a ceramic jar. Seal the jar tightly with a mixture of salt and clay. Calcine <note>Heat at a high temperature without melting</note> the jar using ten catties <note>approx. 6kg</note> of charcoal. After cooling, grind the contents into a fine powder. Mix with vinegar paste to form pills the size of a mung bean. Each dose consists of fifteen pills, taken with wine infused with White-flowered Snake <note>Agkistrodon, a venomous viper used to "track down" wind-related illnesses in the nerves and skin</note>. Take three times a day until cured. From the *Taiping Prescriptions of Divine Benevolence*.

### Appended Minerals and Stones

It consists of gold dust found within stones and is reddish-brown in color.

### Whirling Stone [Posuo Shi]

**[Synonyms]** Stroked Stone [Mosuo Shi]

Minerals and Stones, Part 2, Volume 10

## Main Indications
**Detoxifies all medicinal poisons, and treats miasmic vapors, pestilence, epidemic fevers, chest oppression, and headaches.**

<margin>Baokai <note>Likely a reference to a specific source or person, possibly the Buddhist monk Baokai who was known for his medical knowledge</note></margin>

<column-break/>

## Flavor and Nature
**The flavor is sweet and bland; its nature is cold and non-toxic.**

<column-break/>

## Collected Explanations
Zhi <note>Referring to the scholar Su Song in the *Illustrated Materia Medica*</note> says: Whirling Stone <term>Posuoshi</term> <note>Also known as "Whirling Stone," this mineral was often imported from the South Seas and Central Asia; it was highly valued as a universal antidote</note> is produced in the South Seas. Foreign merchants collect it. The stone is green in color without any spots, but it features "golden stars" <note>likely inclusions of pyrite</note>. The highest quality is that which, when ground, produces a milky-white juice. There is also "Bean-Spotted Stone" <term>Doubanshi</term>; although it can also detoxify, its medicinal power is inferior. Furthermore, there is "Or-Green" <term>Elü</term> <note>A type of green mineral, possibly a variety of malachite</note>, which has a patterned texture and can turn iron into the color of copper when rubbed against it. Many people use this as a substitute, but it is not the genuine article. The method for testing its authenticity is to grind it with water and apply a drop to the hot blood of a rooster's comb; if the blood immediately thins and turns into water, it is genuine.

Zongshi <note>Referring to Kou Zongshi in the *Materia Medica Expanded*</note> says: Those stones that are a pale "mineral green" color with subtle golden stars interspersed within are the best. "Bean-Spotted Stone" is similar to this stone, but it has black spots and lacks the golden stars.

Song <note>Referring again to Su Song</note> says: Foreigners value this stone especially highly. They set it in gold to make finger rings, which they wear constantly. Whenever they are about to eat, or after they have finished eating, they will suck on the stone several times to protect themselves against any potential poison. Nowadays, if someone manages to obtain a piece even the size of a thumbnail, it is worth a hundred pieces of gold.

Shizhen <note>Referring to Li Shizhen, the primary author of the *Compendium of Materia Medica*</note> says: The *Wang Ce* by Yu Xin <note>A famous poet and official of the Northern and Southern Dynasties</note> states that the Whirling Stone <term>Mosuoshi</term> <note>An alternate name for the same mineral</note> is a "Yang stone." It is produced in Srivijaya <note>An ancient maritime empire based in Sumatra, Indonesia</note>. There are "Five-Colored Mountains" in the sea that rise up steeply; these stones possess a glowing flame. The water below the mountains rushes by as fast as an arrow. People passing beneath them in ships strike the cliffs with knives and axes to take the stones. When burned, they emit a sulfurous odor. Those that are shaped like the teeth of a yellow dragon and are heavy and hard are considered the finest quality.

<summary>This page describes the origins, characteristics, and testing methods for Whirling Stone (Posuoshi/Mosuoshi), a highly prized mineral used as a universal antidote against poisons and miasma.</summary>
<keywords>Whirling Stone (Posuoshi), Srivijaya (Sanfoqi), detoxification, golden stars, testing authenticity, Li Shizhen, Su Song</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Minerals and Stones" section of the *Compendium of Materia Medica*. While the previous page concluded the entry on Whirling Stone (Posuoshi), this page begins the entry for Chlorite Schist (Mengshi), a mineral primarily used for resolving stubborn phlegm and digestive accumulations.</meta>

# Chlorite Schist
<note>original: "礞石" (Mengshi). This is typically identified as a type of chlorite schist or mica schist.</note>
First recorded in the Jiayou Era of the Song Dynasty <note>1056–1063 AD</note>

<column-break/>

<margin>Shizhen says: Its color appears shimmering and undulating, hence the name <note>The character "Meng" (礞) suggests a misty or shimmering quality</note></margin>
## Explanation of Names: Green Chlorite Schist

<column-break/>

## Collected Explanations
Shizhen says: Chlorite Schist is often found in the various mountains north of the Yangtze River; that which comes from Mount Xu is considered the highest quality. There are two varieties, green and white, with the green being superior. It is firm, fine-grained, and blue-black in color. When broken open, it contains white star-like spots. After being calcined <note>heated to high temperatures</note>, these stars turn golden-yellow, resembling "gold bran" <note>likely pyrite or mica inclusions</note>. Those without these star-like spots are not used in medicine. In Tongcheng County, there is a mountain that produces it, and craftsmen there use the stone to carve various vessels and objects.

<column-break/>

## Preparation
Shizhen says: Use one large crucible. Take four taels <note>approximately 150 grams</note> of Chlorite Schist and break it into pieces. Add four taels of nitre <term>Xiaoshi</term> <note>Saltpeter or potassium nitrate, used here as a flux to chemically alter the stone during heating</note> and mix evenly. Surround the crucible with fifteen catties <note>about 9 kilograms</note> of charcoal and calcine the mixture until the nitre is completely consumed and the color of the stone has turned gold. Remove it, grind it into a fine powder, and use **water levigation** <term>Shuifei</term> <note>A refining technique where the powder is stirred in water; the finest suspended particles are collected while the heavy, "toxic" grit sinks and is discarded</note> to remove impurities. Dry in the sun before use.

<column-break/>

## Flavor and Nature
**Sweet and salty in flavor; neutral in nature; non-toxic.**

<column-break/>

## Main Indications
Treats food stagnation that will not dissolve, accumulations lingering in the viscera and bowels, and long-standing abdominal masses <term>Zhengkuai</term> <note>Hard tumors or masses in the abdomen often attributed to stagnant blood or phlegm</note> that have not healed for a long time. Also treats childhood food stagnation...

<summary>This page introduces Chlorite Schist (Mengshi), detailing its physical characteristics, its sourcing from Mount Xu, and the specific chemical process of calcining it with saltpeter to prepare it for medicinal use.</summary>
<keywords>Chlorite Schist, Mengshi, Li Shizhen, Calcination, Nitre, Saltpeter, Water Levigation, Indigestion, Abdominal Masses</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Green Chlorite Schist (Qing Mengshi). While the previous page detailed its physical properties and calcination process with nitre, this page moves into its clinical "Development" (Faming), featuring commentary by the famous Ming dynasty herbalist Li Shizhen and other historical physicians regarding its potency and the dangers of its misuse.</meta>

# Essential Prescriptions from the Golden Chamber, Volume 10
<note>original: "金匱藥略" (Jingui Yaolüe). Note that while the header identifies this as the Golden Chamber, the content actually consists of commentary from Li Shizhen’s *Compendium of Materia Medica*.</note>
Page 11

# For emaciated women with chronic food accumulations causing piercing pain in the heart and abdomen: take Croton seed, Sal ammoniac, and Rhubarb

# and Sparganium root; made into pills, it is excellent.
<margin>Jiayou Era</margin> To treat accumulated phlegm, fright-epilepsy, coughing, and wheezing. <margin>Shizhen</margin>

## Development
<margin>Shizhen says:</margin> The flavor of Green Chlorite Schist is neutral and salty. Its nature is to move downward; it is "Yin" and "Sinking" in character. It is a medicine for the Jueyin [Liver] meridian. When the "Wind-Wood" energy of the Liver meridian is excessive, it overcomes the Spleen Earth, and the bodily energy (Qi) fails to circulate.

### The Medicine's Heavy Sinking Nature
This transforms stagnant accumulations and dissolves phlegm that congests the upper and middle "burners" <term>Triple Burner</term> <note>The body's three functional divisions</note>, which can manifest as various wind-heat diseases. Therefore, this medicine's heavy, sinking nature is appropriate. When prepared with nitre <term>Xiaoshi</term> <note>Saltpeter</note>, its nature becomes dredging and swift.

### A Sacred Medicine for Expulsion
It causes the Wood energy to level and the Qi to descend, allowing accumulated phlegm to pass through easily, which naturally resolves all associated symptoms. Tang Heng's *Baojian for Infants* states that Chlorite Schist is a sacred medicine for treating fright and facilitating the expulsion of phlegm.

### Cautions on Long-term Use: Yang
If phlegm is like water on a surface and one sprinkles it with the powdered stone, the phlegm immediately follows the water downward; from this, its sinking nature can be known. However, it should only be used in emergencies. 

### On Deficiency and Excess
For those with weak Qi or a deficient Spleen, it should not be taken long-term. Yang Shiying noted that while its function is useful for phlegm, its nature is not favored by the stomach. For conditions like chronic convulsions [in children], it should be supplemented with Aucklandia root <term>Muxiang</term>.

### Until Nightfall
However, Wang Yinjun claimed that since phlegm is the root of a hundred diseases, the "Vaporizing Phlegm Pills" <term>Guntan Wan</term> can treat all diseases regardless of whether they are of a "Deficiency" or "Excess," or "Cold" or "Heat" nature. How could such a principle be sound? Zhu Danxi told of an old man who suddenly went blind—a case of extreme "Deficiency." A physician gave him medicine containing Chlorite Schist, and he died by nightfall. Alas! This was the fault of a "blind" physician who "weakened the weak" <term>Xu-xu zhi guo</term> <note>A medical error where a depleting treatment is given to an already exhausted patient</note>. How can Chlorite Schist be blamed for killing a man?

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal properties of Green Chlorite Schist, emphasizing its heavy, "sinking" nature used to treat stubborn phlegm and liver-related ailments. It includes a stern warning from Li Shizhen against using this potent mineral for patients with "Deficiency" conditions, citing a fatal medical error as a cautionary tale.</summary>
<keywords>Green Chlorite Schist, Phlegm, Li Shizhen, Vaporizing Phlegm Pills, Spleen Deficiency, Medical Ethics, Pediatric Fright</keywords>

## New Additional Prescriptions
<note>These are practical clinical formulas added to the entry to demonstrate the mineral's application.</note>

### Phlegm-Rolling Pill
<note>original: "滾痰丸" (Guntan Wan). One of the most famous formulas in Chinese medicine for "rolling away" stubborn, old phlegm that causes mania or chronic illness.</note>
This formula provides a general treatment for all diseases caused by phlegm. However, it must not be taken by those suffering from watery diarrhea or by pregnant women. 

**Green Chlorite Schist** and **Nitre** <term>Xiaoshi</term> <note>Saltpeter</note>, two ounces of each. Calcine these together, then grind and refine the powder through levigation <note>a process of grinding a moist powder to extreme fineness</note>. Once sun-dried, take one ounce of this prepared powder. Add **Rhubarb** (eight ounces, steamed in wine), **Baikal Skullcap** <term>Huangqin</term> <note>Scutellaria baicalensis</note> (eight ounces, washed in wine), and **Agarwood** (five mace <term>Qian</term>). Grind these into a fine powder and form into pills with water, roughly the size of a parasol seed. For regular use, take ten to twenty pills. If you wish to induce a bowel movement to purge the phlegm, take one to two hundred pills, washed down with warm water.

<gloss>Yin Jun's Treatise on Cultivating Life</gloss>
Wang Yi

### All Accumulation Diseases
**Golden Treasure Divine Pill** <note>original: "金寶神丹" (Jinbao Shendan). The name implies a formula of high value and efficacy.</note> 
This pill treats all conditions of cold-deficiency, long-standing accumulations, chronic diarrhea, persistent dysentery, abdominal masses, piercing pain in the heart and abdomen, and uterine bleeding or spotting in women. 

Take half a catty <term>Jin</term> <note>Approximately 250–300 grams in the historical context</note> of **Green Chlorite Schist** and grind it into a fine powder. Take two ounces of **Nitre** powder. Layer them inside a crucible—lining both the bottom and the top—and pack the contents firmly. Calcine the crucible using twenty catties of charcoal. Once removed, add two ounces of **Red Bole** <term>Chishizhi</term> <note>A medicinal clay, often Red Halloysite, used to stop leakage and diarrhea</note> powder. Add a drop of water and form into pills the size of a charcoal piece. Once dry, return them to the crucible and calcine until red-hot. For each dose, take one to three pills on an empty stomach with a small amount of warm water to help them down. For chronic diarrhea or dysentery, the dose may be increased to five pills.

### Acute and Chronic Convulsions
**Life-Saving Powder** <note>original: "奪命散" (Duoming San). A "Life-Saving" or "Fate-Snatching" powder used in emergencies.</note>
This formula treats acute and chronic convulsions <note>commonly referred to as "fright wind" in children</note> where phlegm and saliva obstruct the throat. This is from *Master Yang's Family Collection of Prescriptions*. When a life hangs in the balance, taking this will cause the "wind-phlegm" to drop down and be expelled. It is considered a "sacred medicine" for treating convulsions and purging phlegm. 

Take one ounce of **True Chlorite Schist** and one ounce of **Nitre**. Calcine them together and grind into a fine powder. Each dose is half a mace or one mace.

<margin>Compendium of Materia Medica</margin>

# Flower Nipple Stone <note>original: "花乳石" (Hua Ru Shi); scientifically identified as Ophicalcite, a form of marble containing serpentine</note> <gloss>Song Dynasty, Jiayou Era</gloss>

## Explanation of Names
<gloss>Zongshi says:</gloss> Within the yellow stone, there are pale white spots; the name "Flower Nipple Stone" is derived from this appearance. In the *Illustrated Classic* <term>Tujing</term>, it is called "Flower Leaf Stone" <gloss>this refers to its yellow color</gloss>.

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>Yuxi says:</gloss> Flower Nipple Stone is produced in various prefectures of Shaanxi and Huazhou. Its color is a true yellow. It is found in square blocks of various sizes, some of which are extremely large. The people of Shaanxi carve them into vessels. It is collected at no specific time.

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> Its shape is irregular. It is said to come from Lvxiang in Shanzhou. Its substance is extremely hard and heavy, with a color like whetstone jade. The *Records* <term>Ce</term> state: Flower Nipple Stone is a "Yin" stone <note>meaning it has cooling or grounding properties</note>. It is produced in the mountain valleys of Daizhou. It comes in five colors and can be used in place of **Cinnabar** <term>Dansha</term> in pill formulas. It is also found in Wenshan and Pengxian in the Sichuan region.

## Preparation
<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> Whenever it is used in pills or powders, it must be placed in a jar and sealed tightly <note>a process called "luting" where the vessel is sealed with clay</note>. It is then fired (calcined) at a high heat to remove its "great toxins." Finally, it is ground into a fine powder, refined through levigation <note>grinding with water to separate the finest particles</note>, and dried in the sun before use.

<summary>This page concludes the pediatric applications of Green Chlorite Schist and introduces Flower Nipple Stone (Ophicalcite), detailing its physical appearance, geographical origins in Shaanxi and Sichuan, and the intensive heat-processing required for its medicinal use.</summary>
<keywords>Flower Nipple Stone, Ophicalcite, Acute Fright, Slow Fright, Li Shizhen, calcination, levigation, Shaanxi, pediatric medicine</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for Flower Nipple Stone (Ophicalcite) from the previous page, moving from its physical description and origin to its medicinal properties, primary uses in trauma and obstetrics, and an analytical commentary by Li Shizhen regarding its unique pharmaceutical mechanisms.</meta>

### Nature and Flavor
**Sour, astringent, neutral, and non-toxic.**

### Primary Indications
It is primarily used for **incised wounds** <note>original: "金瘡" (jinchuang); wounds caused by metal blades or tools</note> that are bleeding. Scrape the stone into a fine powder and apply it to the wound; it will close immediately and will not fester or form pus. It also treats **postpartum fainting** <note>dizziness or loss of consciousness after childbirth, often due to blood loss or stagnation</note> and the retention of **stale blood** <note>original: "惡血" (exue); refers to pathogenic, non-circulating blood or retained lochia</note>.

<gloss>Jiayou Era</gloss>
It treats all forms of blood loss, traumatic injuries, internal hemorrhaging, and **corneal opacities** <note>original: "目翳" (muyi); cloudy growths or scarring on the eye</note>.

### Elaboration
<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss>
<gloss>Song says:</gloss> Flower Nipple Stone was not included in ancient formulas. In recent times, it is combined with **Sulfur**, calcined together <note>heated to high temperatures in a crucible</note>, and ground into powder to be applied to incised wounds; its effects are miraculous. If someone is suddenly injured by a blade and there is no time to prepare it by calcining, simply scraping the raw stone into a powder and applying it is also effective.

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> In older texts, the nature and flavor of Flower Nipple Stone were not recorded; I have now tested it. Its nature is neutral, and its flavor is astringent and sour. It is a medicinal substance that acts upon the **blood division of the Absolute Yin [Liver] Meridian** <note>original: "厥陰經血分" (Jueyin jing xuefen); in traditional theory, the Liver stores blood and its meridian governs its flow</note>. Its power is specialized in stopping bleeding, as it has the ability to **transform stagnant blood into water** <note>this refers to the stone's ability to dissolve congealed blood or clots so they can be reabsorbed or expelled</note>. Because it is sour, it has an astringent, collecting effect.

Furthermore, it can induce the delivery of a **stillborn fetus** and the expulsion of the **placenta** <note>original: "胞衣" (baoyi)</note>, and remove stale blood. Once the stale blood is transformed and cleared, there is no longer any obstruction to the fetus or the placenta. This follows the principle mentioned by **Li Dongyuan** <note>a famous 13th-century physician</note>: "When the placenta fails to emerge, astringent agents can be used to drive it down." For this reason, **Red Halloysite** <term>Chishizhi</term> <note>a medicinal clay</note> is also able to expel the placenta and fetus, sharing the same therapeutic logic as this stone.

**Ge Kejiu** <note>a renowned specialist in "consumptive" diseases and bleeding disorders</note>, when treating patients vomiting huge quantities of blood, used **Flower Nipple Stone Powder**. The *Formulary of the Imperial Grace Pharmacy* <term>Heji Ju Fang</term> also contains a "Flower Medicine Stone Powder" for treating various blood disorders, traumatic injuries, blade wounds, and complications of childbirth. Both sources state that it can "transform blood into water," proving that the power of this stone is truly extraordinary and far exceeds that of common herbs.

# Essential Prescriptions from the Golden Cabinet, Volume 10

## Appendix Formulas

### Five Flower Nipple Stone Powder
This formula treats severe internal collapse and damage where blood is spat or sprayed out in massive quantities <note>original: "斗升" (dou sheng); literally "pecks and liters," indicating a life-threatening volume of blood loss</note>. Use this to treat it.
Preparation: Use one cup of **boy’s urine** <note>original: "童子小便" (tongzi xiaobian); the urine of a prepubescent boy, traditionally used as a medicinal vehicle to guide medicine to the blood level and clear heat</note>. For a male patient, add half a portion of **wine**; for a female patient, add half a portion of **vinegar**. Decoct the mixture and take it warm after a meal, mixing in **three qian** <note>a traditional unit of weight, approximately 3.75 grams</note> of the powder. In severe cases, use **five qian**. This medicine has the power to transform **stagnant blood** <note>original: "瘀血" (yuxue); clotted or non-circulating blood</note> into "yellow water." Afterward, use **Ginseng-Only Decoction** <note>Du Shen Tang; a potent tonic used to rescue the "Qi" after massive blood loss</note> to supplement the body.
—Ge Kejiu <note>A famous physician of the Yuan Dynasty known for his expertise in treating "consumptive" diseases and hemorrhages</note>

### Flower Nipple Stone Powder
<gloss>Divine Book of Ten Remedies</gloss>
This treats all wounds from metal blades or arrowheads, as well as damage from strikes or falls, and dog bites. Even if the victim is near death, immediately sprinkle the medicine on the wounded area; the blood will turn into yellow water. Once more is sprinkled on, the patient will revive and the pain will cease. If there is internal damage and blood has entered the **Zang-Fu organs** <note>the vital internal organs</note>, decoct boy’s urine with a little wine and take **one qian** of the powder mixed in while hot; the effect is immediate.

If a domestic animal gores the patient and the **intestines protrude** but are not severed, quickly tuck them back in and sew the wound with thread made from **mulberry root-bark** <note>original: "桑白皮" (sangbaipi); the inner bark of the mulberry tree was historically used as a biodegradable surgical suture</note>. Sprinkle the medicine on top; the bleeding will stop and the patient will survive.

For women after childbirth, if the **lochia** <note>original: "血不盡" (xue bu jin); postpartum discharge</note> is not fully discharged, or if there is **postpartum fainting** <note>original: "血運" (xueyun); a crisis caused by sudden blood loss or stagnation</note>, "evil blood" attacking the heart, a dead fetus remaining in the womb, or a retained placenta—even if the patient appears dead but the pit of the heart remains warm—quickly administer **one qian** of the powder mixed with boy’s urine. This will bring down the "foul matter," which will look like **pig's liver**. This treatment ensures the woman will never suffer from **blood-wind** <note>original: "血風" (xuefeng); a broad category of postpartum disorders often involving fever or spasms</note> or blood-related Qi disorders for the rest of her life. If there is blood trapped above the **diaphragm**, it will turn into yellow water and be vomited out immediately or passed through the urine. Its effects are miraculous.

Ingredients: **Sulfur**, four liang; **Flower Nipple Stone**, one liang. Combine and grind into a coarse powder. Mix evenly and seal it inside a vessel with **luting clay** <note>original: "膠泥固濟" (jiaoni guji); a process where a crucible is sealed with clay to prevent air from entering during high-heat processing (calcination)</note>. Dry it in the sun.

Place it in a single earthen jar and seal the mouth with mud. Roast it until dry, then place the jar on a brick positioned to the **West** <note>In traditional Chinese alchemy, the West is associated with the element of Metal and the color white, corresponding to the properties of this stone</note>. On the brick, inscribe the characters for the **Eight Trigrams** and **Five Elements**. Use one *秤* (cheng) <note>a traditional weight, approximately 15 lbs</note> of charcoal to surround the jar. Starting between the hours of *si* and *wu* <note>9:00 AM to 1:00 PM, the peak of Yang energy in the day</note>, ignite the fire from below. Allow it to burn until the charcoal is consumed and the jar has cooled completely. Remove the contents and grind them into a fine powder.

<insert>The formula for bleeding from metal wounds can be found above.</insert> Primarily used to treat long-standing **cloudy vision** <note>障翳 (zhang yi): a general term for cataracts or corneal opacities that veil the sight</note>. Store in a bottle for use. 
—*Official Formularies of the Bureau of People's Welfare Pharmacies* <note>Héjì Júfāng: The standard pharmaceutical manual of the Song Dynasty</note>

# White Sheep Stone
<margin>Illustrated Classic of the Song Dynasty</margin>

### Collected Explanations

> Su Song <note>the primary compiler of the *Illustrated Classic of Materia Medica*</note> states: This stone is produced on White Sheep Mountain in Yanzhou <note>modern-day Shandong Province</note>. It is collected in the middle of spring by digging into the earth. Those that are white and translucent like jade are considered of the best quality. There is also a **Black Sheep Stone** produced to the west of Mount Guan in Yanzhou. It is also gathered in mid-spring by digging. For the black variety, those with distinct, wall-like facets and a brilliant luster are considered superior.

### Properties and Flavor

Bland. In its **raw state**, it is cooling; when **prepared with heat**, it is warming. It is non-toxic.

->Golden Tooth Stone, Volume 10<-

Primarily used to treat drug poisoning, similar in effect to **Black Sheep Stone**. <gloss>Su Song <note>Su Song (1020–1101 AD) was a renowned Song dynasty polymath and mineralogist</note></gloss>

# Golden Tooth Stone
<gloss>From the *Additional Records of Famous Physicians* (Mingyi Bielu), Lower Grade <note>In ancient Chinese herbals, "Lower Grade" refers to substances primarily used for treating illness rather than for daily health or longevity, often because they possess potent or toxic properties</note></gloss>

## Explanation of Names
**Yellow Tooth Stone** <gloss>Shizhen says: The name is pictographic, based on its appearance <note>original: "Xiangxing," referring to the mineral's crystalline, tooth-like structure</note></gloss>

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>The *Additional Records* state:</gloss> **Golden Tooth Stone** is produced in the **Shu Commandery** <note>Modern-day Sichuan province</note>. Those that resemble the color of gold are of superior quality.

<gloss>Hongjing says:</gloss> It is currently produced in **Shuhan** <note>Tao Hongjing (456–536 AD) was a key figure in organizing the Materia Medica</note>. It resembles coarse golden teeth, as large as a piece from the game of **Go**, and is square in shape. There is also **"Copper Tooth,"** which looks similar, but its exterior is black and the interior color is slightly pale; this variety is not used in medicine.

<gloss>Gong says:</gloss> When Golden Tooth Stone leaves its place of origin and sits in the soil or water for a long time, it turns black. One should not mistakenly call these **"Copper Tooth."** These are found at the **Golden Tooth Rapids** in **Hanzhong**, where they are struck out from between two layers of rock; these remain golden inside. Those that fall into the water when the riverbanks collapse eventually turn black. They are found near the stream valleys of the **Southern Mountains**. The specimens from **Maozhou** and **Weizhou** are even better than those from Hanzhong.

<gloss>Song says:</gloss> Nowadays, it is also found in **Yongzhou** <note>An ancient province encompassing parts of modern Shaanxi and Gansu</note>.

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> Cui Fang's *Materia Medica* <note>A Song dynasty work on alchemy and minerals</note> says that **Golden Tooth Stone** is a **Yang stone**. It grows in the mountains of **Sichuan** and **Shaanxi**. It resembles a dense chestnut in shape. Those with golden-colored spots are considered wonderful. The **Classic of Sagely Benefaction** (*Shengji Jing*) contains a major formula for treating **leprous disorders** <note>癘風 (lifeng): historical term for leprosy or severe, disfiguring skin diseases</note> that uses both Golden Tooth Stone and **Silver Tooth Stone**. I suspect "Silver Tooth" is simply the white-colored variety of Golden Tooth Stone. As medical books do not mention it elsewhere, I will leave the matter open for now.

<term>Golden Tooth Stone (Jinyashi): Likely Iron Pyrite, prized for its metallic luster</term>
<term>Drug poisoning (Yaodu): Toxic reactions from incorrectly prepared or overdosed medicines</term>
<term>Hanzhong: A historical region in Shaanxi vital for mineral trade</term>
<term>Yang stone: In traditional theory, a mineral possessing "active" or "heating" properties</term>

<summary>This page introduces Golden Tooth Stone (Pyrite), identifying it as an antidote for poisoning and documenting historical debates regarding its color, quality, and geographical sources in Sichuan and Shaanxi.</summary>

# Preparation
<gloss>Daming says:</gloss> To use as medicine, it should be roasted until red-hot, and the coarse outer layers should be removed before use. <note>Roasting or calcining minerals often makes them more brittle for grinding and is thought to temper their "harsh" qualities.</note>

# Nature and Flavor
**Salty and neutral, non-toxic.**
<gloss>Daming says:</gloss> **Sweet and neutral.**

# Primary Indications
Treats "ghost-infusion" <term>Guizhu: a historical category of infectious wasting diseases believed to be caused by malevolent spirits or pathogens that "infuse" the body</term>, poisonous insect bites, and various types of contagious infusions.

<gloss>The *Additional Records of Famous Physicians* (Bielu) state:</gloss> It treats all conditions of cold-wind qi, including muscle and bone contractions, rigidity, and paralysis of the waist and legs. It should be roasted, soaked in wine, and then consumed.

<gloss>Zhen Quan says:</gloss> It warms the waist and knees, tonifies the **Water Organ** <note>The Kidneys</note>, and treats palpitations and childhood epilepsy.

# Daming

# Invention and Insight
<gloss>Hongjing says:</gloss> **Golden Tooth Stone** is primarily used in medicinal wines, powders, and the "Five-Infusion Pills." It is rarely seen in other types of prescriptions.

<gloss>Su Song says:</gloss> Ge Hong’s *Handbook of Prescriptions for Emergencies* <note>A 4th-century manual for emergency treatments</note> describes "Great" and "Small" Golden Tooth Wines for treating wind-poison and sudden collapse. The method involves soaking the mineral to extract its essence for drinking. 

Sun Simiao’s *Essential Formulas Worth a Thousand Gold* <note>A landmark 7th-century medical text</note> also utilizes "Great" and "Small" Golden Tooth Powders to treat wind-poison, ghost-infusion, southern "miasma" <note>Zhangqi: a term for malaria-like illnesses prevalent in the humid southern regions of ancient China</note>, and "corpse-transmission" <note>Chuanshi: a historical term for consumptive diseases like tuberculosis that seemed to pass from one family member to another</note>. 

The **Small Golden Tooth Wine** is specifically indicated for "wind-infusion" and a hundred types of illnesses involving deficiency-exhaustion, damp-cold, **convulsions and spasms** <note>original: "chizong"</note>, numbness, and the inability to walk. Because it has proven very effective for modern people, the method is recorded as follows: **Golden Tooth Stone**, **Asarum**, **Mangcao**, **Saposhnikovia**, **Kochia Fruit**, and **Rehmannia**... <note>These herbs are typically used to dispel wind, clear dampness, and nourish the blood and kidneys.</note>

<summary>This page details the preparation of Golden Tooth Stone and its primary use in treating "wind-poison" and wasting diseases. It cites famous historical physicians like Sun Simiao and Ge Hong, emphasizing the stone's role in medicinal wines for paralysis and chronic exhaustion.</summary>

<keywords>Golden Tooth Stone, ghost-infusion, wind-poison, medicinal wine, Sun Simiao, Tao Hongjing, paralysis, wasting disease, TCM pharmacology</keywords>

**Golden Tooth Stone Wine for Chronic Convulsions, Volume 10**

**Aconite** <term>Fuzi: the processed side-root of Aconitum carmichaelii, used to restore yang energy</term>, **Wormwood** <term>Yinchen: Artemisia capillaris</term>, **Teasel Root** <term>Xuduan</term>, **Sichuan Pepper**, and **Sambucus Root** <note>original: "Shuodiaogen"</note>: four ounces each. 
**Pubescent Angelica** <term>Duhuo</term>: one pound. 

For these twelve substances <note>The text mentions twelve items, but only six are listed on this specific page; the remainder likely appeared on the previous leaf</note>, the **Golden Tooth Stone** is to be ground into a powder and placed in a separate fine silk bag. The remaining ingredients should be thinly sliced and placed together in a large bag. Soak them in four *dou* <note>approximately 8 liters</note> of clear wine. Seal the mouth of the vessel with clay and let it sit for four nights. 
Once the wine is ready, drink two small cups warm, twice a day, until results are achieved.

# Diamond
<gloss>Mugang</gloss> <note>Literally "The Essential Guide," an alternate name for this mineral in classic catalogs</note>

## Explanation of Names
**The Vajra-Drill** <note>original: "Jingangzhuan"</note>
<gloss>Shizhen says: Its grit can be used to drill into jade or repair porcelain, which is why it is called a "drill."</gloss> <note>Li Shizhen (1518–1593) was the author of the *Compendium of Materia Medica*</note>

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>Shizhen says: Diamonds come from the various kingdoms of India and the Western Regions.</gloss>

<gloss>Ge Hong says in his *Baopuzi* <note>A 4th-century text on alchemy and immortality</note>: The kingdom of Funan <note>an ancient state in mainland Southeast Asia</note> produces diamonds which grow on rocks at the bottom of the water. They resemble stalactites in shape and have a body like purple quartz. They can be used to carve jade. Divers submerge to retrieve them. Even if struck with an iron hammer, they will not be damaged; however, if struck with a horn from a Saiga antelope, they will shatter and dissolve like water.</gloss>

<gloss>The *Danfang Jianyuan* <note>An alchemical text regarding the origins of minerals</note> says: "Purple-backed lead" is the substance capable of crushing a diamond drill.</gloss>

<gloss>Zhou Mi says in his *Qi Dong Ye Yu* <note>"Random Notes from the Eastern Suburbs," a 13th-century miscellany</note>: When jade workers carve jade, they must use "Xing River Sand." Among their engraving tools is the diamond drill, which is shaped like a rat's dropping and is blue-black in color, appearing like stone or iron. Legend says it originates from the high mountain peaks of the Uighur lands in the Western Regions. Eagles carry them and eat them, and the stones pass through their digestive tracts, to be deposited in their droppings among the sandy banks of the rivers in northern China, where they are occasionally found. However, no one truly knows what kind of substance it is.</gloss>

<summary>This page concludes a complex medicinal wine recipe for chronic ailments and begins the entry on diamonds, focusing on their legendary hardness and the folklore surrounding their discovery via eagles and antelope horns.</summary>
<keywords>Diamond, Golden Tooth Stone, medicinal wine, alchemy, Li Shizhen, Ge Hong, Funan, mineralogy, folk medicine</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Diamonds (Jingang), specifically focusing on their physical properties, historical accounts from the West, and metaphorical significance in Buddhism. It then transitions to a new entry for Whetstone (Duanshi), beginning with its nomenclature and geographical origins.</meta>

Records state that the Roman Empire <note>original: "Daqin," a term used in ancient China to refer to the Roman Empire or the Near East</note> produces diamonds, also known as "Jade-Piercing Knives." The large ones are about a foot long, while the small ones are the size of rice or millet grains. When set into a ring, they can be used to carve jade. From this, we can see that diamonds can grow to a substantial size; the "Buddha’s teeth" possessed by monks are actually these stones.

To distinguish the true stone from the false, one should heat it until it glows red and then quench it in vinegar. If it remains unchanged and does not crumble or shatter, it is genuine. If the tool feels dull, forge it red-hot and let it cool; it will then become sharp again. Therefore, in the West <note>referring to the regions of India and Central Asia</note>, the diamond is used as a metaphor for the **Buddha-nature** <note>the inherent potential for enlightenment that cannot be destroyed</note>, while the antelope horn is a metaphor for **worldly afflictions** <note>the "kleshas" or mental impurities that cloud the mind</note.

The *Records of the Ten Continents* <note>a semi-mythological geography text</note> note that among the drifting sands of the Western Sea, there is the **Kunwu Stone**. When refined into a sword, it appears like iron but shines like crystal; it cuts through jade as if it were mud. This is also a type of large diamond. Furthermore, there are beasts such as the **Tapir** <term>Mo: a mythological or actual tapir-like creature believed to consume metal</term> and the **Iron-Gnawer** <note>a legendary rabbit-like creature</note> that are able to eat iron. Their excrement can be forged into weapons that cut through jade; for details, see the entry for the Tapir in the Zoology section.

## Main Indications

->When ground in water and applied topically, it treats scalds and burns. Worn as hairpins or rings, it wards off evil spirits and poisonous vapors.<- <gloss>Shizhen</gloss>

# Whetstone
<gloss>Pronounced: Jin</gloss>
<gloss>From the Compendium</gloss>

### Explanation of Names

**Ironstone** <note>original: "Tieshi"</note>

### Collected Explanations

<gloss>Shizhen says:</gloss> According to the *Classic of Mountains and Seas* (Eastern Section), the Gaoshi Mountains and the Fuli Mountains both abound with **Ironstone**. Guo Pu <note>276–324 AD, a famous scholar and commentator</note> noted that these stones can be used as whetstones to sharpen iron. They are also mentioned in the "Treatise on Different Methods and Appropriate Prescriptions" in the *Basic Questions* <term>Suwen: the foundational text of Traditional Chinese Medicine</term>.

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of diamonds and minerals into the medical history of Stone Needles (Bianshi). Volume Ten

It is said that the eastern regions <note>referring to the coastal areas of ancient China</note> are lands of fish and salt. Because the people there live by the seashore and near the water, they frequently suffer from **boils and ulcers** <term>yongyang: a general term for various inflammatory skin lesions and abscesses</term>. The proper treatment for these conditions is the **stone needle** <term>bianshi: ancient stone tools used for lancing and acupuncture, predating metal needles</term>. Therefore, the stone needle originally came from the East. Wang Bing <note>a Tang dynasty scholar and commentator on medical classics</note> noted that stone needles are like jade and can be used to make acupuncture tools. In ancient times, stones were used as needles; in later ages, metal needles replaced stones. Today, people also use porcelain needles to treat diseases, which is a legacy of the original stone needle concept. However, in recent times, no one recognizes the specific stones used; perhaps they are the same as **stone arrowheads**?

## Appendix: Stone Arrowheads
<gloss>Shizhen says: Stone arrowheads come from the Sushen Kingdom. The people there use withered wood for the shafts and green</gloss>
stone for the tips. They apply poison to the tips; if it strikes a person, they die instantly. These stones are found in the mountains. According to the "Tribute of Yu" <note>a chapter in the *Book of Documents* detailing ancient Chinese geography</note>, the provinces of Jingzhou and Liangzhou both sent these as tribute. This might be that very stone. Furthermore, in the southern region of Tengzhou, they use a green stone to make knives and swords that are as sharp as steel; women there use the stone to make rings and ear ornaments. In the Kingdom of Liuli <note>literally "Glass/Crystal Kingdom," likely referring to a region in Southeast Asia</note>, the people clear their fields using stone knives over a foot long. All of these are of <gloss>this same category.</gloss>

### Main Indications
Used to puncture and treat the "hundred diseases," as well as boils and swellings.

## Whetstone of Yue

The commentary states that the *di* is a fine-grained stone.</gloss>

## Whetstone
<term>Lishi: A generic term for stones used for sharpening tools, utilized here for their mineral properties.</term>

**Main Indications:** Breaking up stagnant, long-standing blood; treating "stone strangury" <term>shilin: a traditional diagnosis for urinary stones or painful urination</term>; and removing abdominal masses. It is used to drive away "hidden ghostly entities" and evil vapors <note>in ancient medicine, "evil vapors" or "ghosts" often referred to infectious diseases or sudden, unexplained illnesses</note>. If heated until red-hot and quenched in wine for drinking, it is said to treat "leaping" ailments <note>likely referring to sudden muscle spasms or tremors</note> and vaginal discharge, though the exact reason for its effectiveness is not yet understood <gloss>Zangqi</gloss>.

# Ginger Stone <gloss>from the Tang Materia Medica</gloss>

## Explanation of Names
Gravelly Stone
<gloss>Li Shizhen says:</gloss> Ginger stone is named for its physical shape <note>the mineral nodules resemble the irregular, branched form of ginger root</note>. It is also written as "Gravelly Stone." Shao Bowen <note>a philosopher of the Northern Song dynasty</note> stated: Heaven has its extreme harshness and Earth has its extreme depths; when the category of stones encounters these forces, they form <meta>catchword: gravelly</meta> gravelly stones. In common writing, the characters for "gravelly" are also written as 礓礰 <note>original: jiānglì</note>.

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>Su Gong says:</gloss> Ginger stone is found everywhere, occurring between soil and rocks. Its shape resembles ginger. There are five varieties; those that are white and crumbly rather than hard and stony are of superior quality. Those from Qicheng, Licheng, and the eastern part of the city <note>locations in modern-day Shandong province</note> are considered excellent. They may be collected at any <meta>catchword: time</meta> time. <gloss>Kou Zongshi says:</gloss> They are found everywhere. It is essential that they have not been exposed to the sun; those gathered fresh that are slightly white are the best. <meta>catchword: see</meta>

## Nature and Flavor
Salty, cold, and non-toxic.

## Main Indications
<margin>Tang Materia Medica</margin>
Treats "heat-type pea sores" <note>a historical term for smallpox or similar eruptive pustules accompanied by fever</note>, "nail-like" boils <term>dingdu: deep-rooted, hard, and painful furuncles or carbuncles resembling the head of a nail</term>, and other toxic swellings.

## Appendix of Prescriptions
<gloss>2 from older sources, 3 newly added</gloss>

**Nail-like boils with swelling and pain**: Grind white ginger stone into a powder and mix with egg whites. Apply the mixture to the area; replace with fresh mixture as soon as it dries. The "nail" <note>the hard central core of the boil</note> will emerge on its own. This is miraculously effective. From *Master Cui's Prescriptions*.

**Breast abscess with large swelling**: When the swelling is as large as a bowl and painful. Use the same prescription as above. From *Secret Essentials from the Imperial Library*.

**Postpartum bloating and qi-blockage**: For feelings of fullness and choking sensations. Take ginger stone and Red Ochre <term>daizheshi: a mineral used in traditional medicine to direct "rebellious qi" downward and stop bleeding</term> in equal parts. Grind into a powder and mix with a vinegar paste <unclear>...</unclear>

# Maifan Stone
<gloss>From the Song Dynasty Illustrated Classic</gloss>
<note>Literally "Barley-rice Stone," so named because the mineral's speckled appearance resembles grains of cooked barley mixed with rice.</note>

## Explanation of Names
<gloss>Li Shizhen says:</gloss> The name is a description of its physical appearance.

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>Li Shizhen says:</gloss> Li Xun <note>a scholar of the Five Dynasties period</note> states that Maifan Stone is found in mountain streams everywhere. These stones vary in size; some are as large as a fist, others like a goose egg, a small saucer, or a flat cake. Generally, they look like a clump of "barley-rice" <note>maifan</note> squeezed together by hand, with granular spots resembling beans or rice grains. Their color is yellowish-white. One simply needs to search among the coarse granitic rocks <note>original: mashì, "hemp-stones"</note> in the stream beds for stones with this appearance to identify them. Ancient formulas claim that stones previously used as millstones are superior, but this is an error. This specific stone is too brittle to be used to make millstones. If this stone is unavailable, one may substitute it with stones taken from near the grinding teeth of an old flour mill, as they are thought to have absorbed the "essence of wheat" <note>maixing</note> through use; however, this is merely a substitute.

## Nature and Flavor
**Sweet, warm, and non-toxic.**

<summary>This page concludes the entry on Ginger Stone with a treatment for systemic edema involving black cow's urine, and introduces Maifan Stone (Barley-rice Stone), detailing its physical characteristics and correcting a common misconception about its use in millstones.</summary>

<keywords>Ginger Stone, Maifan Stone, edema, Li Shizhen, traditional pharmacology, mineral medicine, Barley-rice stone</keywords>
