<meta>This page continues the entry on Maifan Stone (Barley-rice Stone) from the previous page, specifically focusing on its primary clinical application for severe skin infections and the famous historical anecdote regarding its secret formula.</meta>

<page-num>Volume 10</page-num>
<sig>Shi</sig>

# Treating All Types of Boils, Carbuncles, and Back Abscesses
<note>癰疽 (yōngjū) and 發背 (fābèi) refer to severe, deep-seated skin infections and necrotic ulcers, often on the back, which were frequently fatal in pre-modern times.</note>

<margin>Li Shizhen</margin>

**Inventions and Praises:** Su Song <note>a renowned Song Dynasty scholar and scientist</note> states: Generally, most mineral-type substances are effective for treating large boils and carbuncles. A formula known as the "Barley-rice Stone Ointment" has been passed down through the generations and is exceptionally effective for treating abscesses on the back. It was originally a secret formula belonging to Lü Zihua, a hermit of Mount Zhongyue. Pei Hui, an official of the Ministry of Works, was the one who gave the formula its name. Later, Yong, the Governor of Henan, attempted to obtain the formula by subjecting Lü to severe torture. However, Lü preferred to have his neck severed and guarded the secret until his death, refusing to pass it on. 

To prepare it, take this stone and break it into pieces the size of go-stones <note>small, circular stones used in the game of Weiqi or "Go"</note>. Heat them in a charcoal fire until they are glowing red, then submerge them in rice vinegar. Repeat this process ten times. Grind the stones into a powder and sift it finely. Place the powder in a milk mortar <note>a smooth mortar used for fine grinding</note> and have several people take turns grinding it for five to seven days. It must be as fine and smooth as flour. Use four taels <note>roughly 150 grams</note> of this powder. 

Take one set of deer antlers, specifically those still attached to the skull bone; shed antlers are not fit for use. Cut them into sections of two to three inches. Burn them in a charcoal fire until the smoke ceases, then grind into a fine powder. Use two taels of this. 

Take two taels of raw *Bailian* <term>Ampelopsis: a root used in traditional medicine to reduce swelling and heat</term> ground into a powder. 

Use three-year-old rice vinegar and pour it into a vessel made of silver or stone. Boil the vinegar until it reaches the "fish-eye" stage <note>魚目沸: a traditional culinary term describing the second stage of boiling, where medium-sized bubbles resembling fish eyes appear</note>. Gradually stir the medicinal powders into the vinegar, stirring constantly with bamboo chopsticks. Cook for one to two hours until the mixture reaches the proper consistency. Pour it into a basin and wait for it to cool. Cover it with paper to store it, ensuring no dust enters. 

When applying, use a goose feather to brush the ointment onto the swollen area. Cover the surrounding red areas with the ointment, but leave a hole the size of a coin in the center to allow for ventilation and the release of pressure <note>literally "to leak the qi"</note>. If pus has not yet formed, the swelling will dissolve internally. If a "head" has already formed, the abscess will shrink. If it has already burst, the pus will be discharged like rushing water. Even if the illness has persisted so long that the flesh is rotten and tendons or bones are visible, apply the ointment onto fine cloth and patch the wound. Change the dressing as it dries. Day by day, the mouth of the sore will close and heal, provided the internal channels are not obstructed.

<summary>This page describes the "Barley-rice Stone Ointment," a legendary secret formula for treating life-threatening abscesses and carbuncles. It details a rigorous preparation process involving repeated calcination, fine grinding, and blending with deer antler and Ampelopsis root.</summary>
<keywords>Maifan Stone, Barley-rice Stone, carbuncles, back abscesses, Lü Zihua, deer antler, Ampelopsis, traditional pharmacology, Song Dynasty medicine</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page concludes the lengthy clinical instructions for the application of Barley-rice Stone (Maifan Stone) ointment for severe skin infections before beginning a new entry for "White Stones from Water" (Shuizhong Baishi).</meta>

[Page 42]
[Compendium of Materia Medica]

...then none will fail to heal. For those sores that have already ulcerated, first use a decoction of pig's trotters <note>a traditional wash used to cleanse wounds and promote tissue growth</note> to wash away pus and blood. Use old silk to pat the area dry, then apply the medicine. It is strictly forbidden to touch the tender new flesh with the hands. Furthermore, one must not allow the breath from the mouth, or wind, or the "scent of meat," or the presence of menstruating or pregnant women to be near the medicine while it is being prepared or applied <note>These represent traditional ritual and hygiene taboos intended to prevent "contamination" or "wind-evil" from entering a deep wound</note>. At the beginning of treatment, wash and change the dressing once a day. After ten days, change it once every two days. This medicine must be ground until extremely fine to be effective; if it is not fine, applying it will cause extreme pain.

This formula was already recorded in Sun the Perfected Man's <note>Sun Simiao, the famous 7th-century 'King of Medicine'</note> *Monthly Ordinances for the Thousand Golden Formulas*, but it was not as detailed as the description here. Furthermore, Ma Siming of the Ministry of Justice once treated a back abscess for Yang Daoyan. He took a yellow stone the size of a goose egg, heated it in a fierce fire until glowing red, and plunged it into thick vinegar. Some debris naturally fell off into the vinegar. He repeated the heating and quenching until the stone was exhausted. He then took the debris, dried it in the sun, pounded and sifted it into an extremely fine powder, and applied it mixed with vinegar. The ailment was cured immediately. Liu Yuxi, in his *Verified Prescriptions*, calls this the "Stone Calcining Method" <note>original: 鍊石法 (liànshífǎ)</note>; he claims it is invariably effective for all types of sores and swellings.

<column-break/>

# White Stones from Water
*Supplement to the Materia Medica*

<column-break/>

### Collected Explanations

<gloss>The small ones</gloss> Shizhen says: These stones are found in the streams and gullies of every region. The large ones are the size of chicken eggs, and the small ones are like the tips of fingers. They come in both black and white colors. For medicinal purposes, use the small white ones.

<column-break/>

## Primary Indications: For distension and fullness caused by consuming too much raw fish slices <note>original: 魚鱠 (yúkuài); ancient Chinese sashimi or carpaccio, which was a common source of parasitic infections</note>, leading to the formation of masses <term>瘕 (jiǎ) | abdominal masses or parasitic clusters</term>, pain, and a feeling of oppression, resulting in the body becoming gradually thin and weak. Take several dozen pieces...

<summary>This page concludes the instructions for treating deep-seated abscesses with stone-based ointments and begins a new entry on "White Stones from Water," specifically noting their use in treating indigestion or parasites caused by raw fish.</summary>

<keywords>skin infections, pig's trotter soup, Sun Simiao, Liu Yuxi, Stone Calcining Method, White Stones from Water, raw fish consumption, abdominal masses</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "White Stones from Water" (Shuizhong Baishi), specifically concluding the instructions for treating abdominal masses and skin conditions before transitioning into the "Exposition" section which discusses the Daoist alchemical practice of "boiling stones" for sustenance.</meta>

Volume 10

->Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries<-

Heat the stone until it is glowing red and plunge it into five liters of water. Repeat this seven times. Drink the liquid while hot. After doing this three to five times, the patient should purge and expel the abdominal masses <term>jia: hard internal masses often associated with stagnation or parasites</term>. Additionally, heating the stone and quenching it in water, then adding three *he* <note>a *he* is a traditional volume measurement, roughly 100 milliliters</note> of salt, can be used to wash "wind-leprosy" <note>a historical term for severe, scaly skin diseases</note> and hives. <insert>Zangqi: Referring to Chen Zangqi, the Tang dynasty pharmacologist</insert> To treat a sudden swelling on the back that is as large as a platter and of unknown origin: take one or two basins of these stones, heat them until hot, plunge them into water, and wash the area frequently. It will be cured immediately. <gloss>Su Song: Referring to the Song dynasty scientist and author of the *Illustrated Pharmacopoeia*</gloss>

---

### Clinical Application

<gloss>Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen (1518–1593), the primary author of this Compendium</note> says: The ancients had a method of "boiling stones" which was considered an excellent practice; it used this very stone. The method is as follows:</gloss>

Use the juice of wild onions or the roots of Great Burnet <note>*Sanguisorba officinalis*, a plant used in traditional medicine to stop bleeding and cool the blood</note> to boil the stones. They will become soft and cooked, like taro. This dish is called "Stone Soup." The *Baopuzi* <note>The "Master Who Embraces Simplicity," a classic 4th-century Daoist text on alchemy and immortality by Ge Hong</note> states: Dong Wei, a Daoist practitioner from Luoyang, had a formula for "Avoiding Grains" <term>Bigu: a Daoist dietary practice of abstaining from grain-based foods to purify the body and seek immortality</term>. He used about ten types of ingredients, such as Silere seeds and licorice, to make a medicinal powder. First, one takes three square-inch spoonfuls of the powder, then swallows twelve stone pebbles the size of sparrow eggs. One can then go a full hundred days without eating, yet one's physical strength and complexion will remain as they were before. If one wishes to return to a normal diet, drink a decoction of mallow to pass the stones from the body. There are also substances known as "Red Dragon Blood" and "Green Dragon Ointment" that can be applied to boil stones. Furthermore, there is an "Attracting Stone Powder"; adding just one square-inch spoonful allows one to boil a *dou* <note>a peck, or about 10 liters</note> of white stones until they are instantly cooked and as soft as taro, making them fit for consumption.

<summary>This page describes the medicinal use of heated stones to treat abdominal masses and skin swellings, and details the Daoist alchemical tradition of "boiling stones" to create a soft, edible substance for practitioners undergoing long-term fasts.</summary>
<keywords>Stone Soup, Bigu (abstaining from grains), Baopuzi, Ge Hong, Li Shizhen, medicinal washing, alchemical diet, white stones</keywords>

# River Sand <gloss>From "Collected Additions" <note>referring to the *Bencao Shiyi* (Supplement to the Materia Medica) by Chen Zangqi</note></gloss>

**Explanation of Names** <gloss>Sand is simply small stones; the Chinese character for "sand" (砂) is a compound of the characters for "small" (少) and "stone" (石).</gloss>

**Primary Indications:** For stone strangury <term>stone strangury: a condition involving painful urination caused by the presence of urinary stones or calculi</term>, take three *sheng* <note>a *sheng* is a traditional volume unit, roughly one liter</note> of fine sand. Stir-fry it until hot, then drench it with three *sheng* of wine. Drink one *he* <note>a *he* is approximately 100 milliliters</note> of the resulting liquid twice a day. It is also used to treat "intestinal-twisting sand pain" <term>intestinal-twisting sand pain: a historical term for severe, acute abdominal colic or cramping</term>. Stir-fry the sand until it is glowing red, quench it in cold water, and wait for it to settle until clear. Drink one or two *he* of the liquid. <gloss>Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen (1518–1593), the primary author</note></gloss> For wind-dampness <note>a category of ailments involving joint pain and stiffness</note>, stubborn numbness and loss of sensation, contraction of the muscles and bones, paralysis caused by "cold wind," and cases where the blood circulation is obstructed: during the sixth lunar month <note>the peak of summer</note>, take river sand and expose it to the intense sun until it is extremely hot. The patient should lie or sit buried within the sand. As the sand cools, replace it with fresh, hot sand. The heat should penetrate the body thoroughly to induce sweating. Following this, use medications appropriate to the specific ailment. It is critical to avoid exposure to wind, cold, or physical exhaustion. <gloss>Zangqi <note>Chen Zangqi, a Tang dynasty pharmacologist</note></gloss>

**Appendix of Prescriptions** <gloss>New</gloss> For someone who has drowned: Stir-fry white sand until hot and cover the person's body and face with it, leaving only the "seven apertures" <note>the eyes, ears, nostrils, and mouth</note> exposed. If the sand becomes cold or damp, replace it immediately. <gloss>From the *Essential Prescriptions Worth a Thousand Gold* <note>a famous 7th-century medical text by Sun Simiao</note></gloss>

<summary>This page describes the medicinal uses of river sand, including heat therapy for joint pain and paralysis, a wine-based infusion for urinary stones, and a specialized method of using heated sand to resuscitate drowning victims.</summary>
<keywords>River Sand, stone strangury, intestinal colic, heat therapy, wind-dampness, resuscitation, Li Shizhen, Chen Zangqi, Sun Simiao</keywords>

Volume 10

## Residue from the Ladle <gloss>From the *Compendium of Materia Medica*</gloss>

### Collected Explanations
<gloss>Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen (1518–1593), the author of this compendium</note> says: This refers to the ladles used in Northern China for washing rice. Whether they are made of wood or gourd, both types are suitable for use.</gloss>

**Primary Indications:** For "wind-millet" rashes on the face <note>likely referring to acne or small, hard papules</note>, which may appear greenish or yellow, or manifest as subtle, rough, and painful spots. It is also used for people suffering from sores on the lips: scrape one or two grains of the "sand" <note>the dried starchy residue or mineral buildup</note> from the rim of the household's own ladle and apply it; the condition will resolve immediately. Additionally, for women suffering from "blown breasts" <term>blown breasts: a historical term for acute mastitis or blocked milk ducts, often accompanied by painful swelling</term>, take seven grains of the residue and wash them down with warm wine. Further, use a bristle from a rice-cleaning broom to clear the nipple pore. The underlying logic for these treatments remains unexplained. <gloss>Shizhen</gloss>

---

## Stone Swallows <gloss>From the *Tang Materia Medica*</gloss>

### Collected Explanations
<gloss>Li Ji <note>A Tang dynasty scholar-official</note> says: Stone swallows are produced in Lingling <note>a region in modern-day Hunan province</note>. Su Gong <note>The lead editor of the Tang Materia Medica</note> says: Ten *li* <note>approximately 5 kilometers</note> northwest of Qiyang County in Yongzhou, there is an earthen mound. By digging more than ten feet deep, they can be retrieved. In shape, they resemble a blood clam <note>a type of ribbed ark clam</note> but are smaller and very hard.</gloss>

<summary>This page describes the medicinal use of starchy residue from rice ladles for skin conditions and mastitis, and introduces "Stone Swallows"—fossilized brachiopods found in the earth of Hunan province.</summary>
<keywords>Residue from the Ladle, Stone Swallows, mastitis, skin rashes, fossils, Li Shizhen, Tang Materia Medica, Lingling</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for "Stone Swallows" from the "Stones" section of Volume 10. The previous page ended with Su Gong’s description of the origins of these stones in Qiyang County.</meta>

...is preposterous. <gloss>Song <note>Su Song (1020–1101), a renowned scientist and statesman of the Song Dynasty</note> says: They are found on the sandy banks of the river in Qiyang County. Some say they are born in caves. They are dense and heavy, just like stone. Local lore claims that during thunderstorms, they emerge from stone caves and fly about with the rain. Those that are clear and as hard as stone are considered of the best quality. They can be harvested at any time.</gloss> <gloss>Zongshi <note>Kou Zongshi, a 12th-century physician and herbalist</note> says: Stone swallows are shaped like clams or mussels. Their color is like earth, and they are as hard and heavy as stone. Since they have no wings, how could they possibly fly out? Such talk borders on the preposterous.</gloss> <gloss>Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen, the primary author</note> says: There are two types of "Stone Swallows." One is a true stone, belonging to the mineral category <note>these are actually brachiopod fossils</note>. It is shaped like a swallow and has patterns. The large, round ones are considered male, while the small, elongated ones are female. The other type is the "stone swallow" found in stalactite caves, which resembles a bat. It feeds on "stone milk" <note>liquid mineral deposits from stalactites</note> and is capable of flight; this is a species of bird. See the "Birds" section for details. The avian stone swallow feeds on "stone milk," and consuming it provides tonic benefits similar to those of stalactites. Therefore, medical manuals often use them in medicines to boost *yang* <note>vital masculine energy</note>. Common people, unaware of the distinction, frequently use the northern stones <note>the fossils</note> in recipes for boosting *yang* and even record this in printed medical books. This is a mistake.</gloss>

### Flavor and Nature
Sweet, cooling, and non-toxic.

### Primary Indications
For treating strangury <term>strangury: a condition characterized by painful, frequent, and drop-by-drop urination, often associated with stones or infection</term>, boil them and drink the decoction. For women suffering from difficult labor, have them hold one stone in each hand; the effect is immediate.

Also treats cataracts and opacities of the eyes, all types of urinary dripping, chronic "thirst-extinguishing" disease <term>thirst-extinguishing disease: a historical term corresponding to symptoms of diabetes, such as excessive thirst and weight loss</term>, frequent diarrhea of the viscera, and "intestinal wind" <note>a term for bloody stools or rectal bleeding, often attributed to "wind" invading the intestines</note>.

<summary>This page clarifies the distinction between fossilized "Stone Swallows" (brachiopod fossils) and bat-like "Stone Swallows" found in caves, correcting common medicinal misuses. It lists the mineral version's uses for urinary issues, difficult labor, and eye conditions.</summary>
<keywords>Stone Swallows, Shiyan, fossils, Li Shizhen, strangury, difficult labor, thirst-extinguishing disease, Materia Medica</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for "Stone Swallows" from the "Metals and Stones" section of Volume 10. The previous page discussed the physical characteristics and origins of these fossils, as well as the common confusion between the mineral fossil and a species of cave-dwelling bird (a bat) that shares the same name.</meta>

# Metals and Stones Section, Volume 10

For hemorrhoids and fistulas <note>abnormal connections or passages between organs</note> that have not healed for many years, a sallow or sickly yellow complexion, and a lack of appetite; also for women with turbid menstruation or persistent red and white vaginal discharge: grind the stone daily to produce juice and drink it. One stone should be used over the course of three days; let this be the standard. Alternatively, the stone may be ground into a powder and refined through water levigation <note>a process of grinding a substance in water to separate the finest particles from grit</note>. Take a daily dose of half a *qian* to one *qian* <note>approximately 1.8 to 3.75 grams</note> mixed with rice water. If taken for one month, these various ailments will be completely pacified. <gloss>Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen (1518–1593), the primary author and compiler of this work</note></gloss>

---

## Explanations
<gloss>Shizhen says: The nature of the Stone Swallow <note>referring to the fossilized brachiopod</note> is cool. It is a substance that clears the "orifices" <note>body openings</note> and promotes the movement of "damp-heat" <note>a concept in traditional medicine involving inflammation, infection, or fluid buildup</note>. When the scholars of the Song Dynasty revised the *Bencao* [Materia Medica], they mistakenly included the "Stone Swallow" bird—which feeds on "stone milk" <note>liquid mineral deposits from stalactites</note>—under this entry for the mineral Stone Swallow. Consequently, popular lore has erroneously passed down the idea that this stone can assist the *yang* <note>vital masculine energy or warmth</note>. They do not realize that its properties are actually the exact opposite.</gloss>

---

## Appended Prescriptions
<gloss>Three from old sources, seven newly added.</gloss>

### Urinary Difficulty due to Cold Damage
<gloss>For abdominal distention and fullness: Grind Stone Swallows into a powder. Take half a *qian* <note>approx. 1.8g</note> as a dose, mixed with a decoction of scallion whites. This is found in the *Sheng Hui Fang* <note>The *Taiping Shenghui Fang* or "Imperial Benevolence Formulary," a 10th-century medical encyclopedia</note>.</gloss>

### Painful Urinary Dribbling
<gloss>Take seven Stone Swallows and crush them to the size of broomcorn millet grains. Take three *liang* <note>approx. 112 grams</note> of fresh white root-bark of the mulberry tree, chop it finely, and mix the ingredients evenly. Divide the mixture into seven doses. For each dose, use one cup of water and decoct until seven-tenths of the liquid remains.</gloss>

<gloss>From *Simplified Brief for Aiding the Masses*</gloss>

Grind them into a fine powder. For each dose, take one *qian* <note>about 3.75 grams</note> mixed into a decoction of scallion whites.

<gloss>From *Sagely Benevolent Prescriptions*</gloss>

<gloss>From *Spiritual Garden Prescriptions*</gloss>
**Red and white vaginal discharge**: For cases that have persisted for years without stopping, grind one Stone Swallow in water and drink. It has an immediate effect.

<gloss>From *Master Xu’s Family Prescriptions*</gloss>
**Wheezing and vomiting of milk in infants**: For a cough that remains uncured for a long time, grind Stone Swallows into a powder. Mix a small amount with honey and apply it to the infant’s lips three to five times a day.

<gloss>From *The Precious Mirror of Health*</gloss>
**To strengthen teeth and stop pain**: Take three pairs of Stone Swallows. Calcine them in fire <note>heated until red-hot</note> and quench them in vinegar seven times. Add one *liang* <note>approx. 37 grams</note> each of sea salt and frankincense, and half a *liang* of *xixin* <note>wild ginger/asarum</note>. Grind these into a powder and rub onto the teeth. Rinse the mouth with a decoction of *jingjie* <note>Schizonepeta/Japanese catnip</note>.

**Loose or weak teeth**: Take five pairs of Stone Swallows, calcine them, and grind into powder, quenching in vinegar seven times. 

Another method: Remove the frankincense and wild ginger; add a small amount of musk. Grind evenly with sea salt and musk. Use the finger to rub the powder behind the teeth daily, then rinse with warm wine and swallow.

<gloss>From *Yuan Yishan’s Prescriptions*</gloss>
**Adverse reactions to mineral drugs** <note>referring to "stone-toxicity" caused by the historical practice of consuming alchemical mineral powders</note>: Take seven Stone Swallows, crush them, and boil in water. Use the liquid to wash the affected areas frequently.

<gloss>From *Imperial Encyclopedia of Medicine*</gloss>
**Inverted eyelashes** <note>trichiasis; a condition where eyelashes grow inward and irritate the eye</note>: After plucking out the inverted hairs, take one "male" and one "female" Stone Swallow <note>referring to the convex and concave shapes of the fossil valves</note> and grind them in water. Apply the liquid to the eyelid, then wash with a decoction of *huanglian* <note>coptis/goldthread</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on Stone Swallows to the entry for Stone Crabs (fossilized crustaceans). It details the medicinal indications, the "flavor and nature" (energetic properties), and historical commentary on their origins.</meta>

Volume 10

# Principal Indications

Green blindness <note>referring to a loss of sight where the eye appears externally normal, similar to glaucoma</note>, excessive discharge and films or "veils" over the eye, and nail-shaped opacities <note>small, hard growths on the cornea</note>. It also treats lacquer sores <note>contact dermatitis caused by the lacquer tree</note> <gloss>to neutralize or resolve toxins</gloss>. It is used for all types of medicinal poisoning as well as "Gu" poisoning <note>an ancient category of toxins associated with parasitic infection or folk magic</note>, epidemic heat-related illnesses, inducing labor, expelling a dead fetus, and treating fainting or vertigo due to blood loss. For these conditions, it should be ground into a powder and taken with hot water <gloss>or rubbed with strong vinegar for topical use</gloss>.

<column-break/>

# Properties

Salty and cold; non-toxic. <note>In traditional pharmacology, "salty" substances are thought to soften hardness, while "cold" substances clear inflammation and heat.</note>

<column-break/>

# Collected Explanations

**Ma Zhi** states: Stone Crabs are produced in the South Sea. It is said they are simply ordinary crabs. Over many years, they live in deep water caves; those that remain there for a long time eventually transform. <gloss>They are covered in froth which causes them to fossilize into stone. Whenever the sea tide passes, they are drifted out. There is also another variety.</gloss> They should be ground finely and refined by "water-flying" <note>a process of grinding minerals in water to produce an ultra-fine powder</note> before being added to other medicinal formulas to assist their effects.

**Su Song** states: They are found in all prefectures and counties near the sea. Their physical substance is stone, yet they resemble crabs in every detail, though some have mud or coarse stone adhering to them.

**Li Shizhen** states: According to Gu Jie’s *Notes of the Sea Voyage*, in Yulin Port within Yazhou <note>modern-day Hainan Island</note>, about half a mile inland, the soil is extremely fine and cold. If a crab enters this soil, it becomes unable to move and within a short time turns into stone. People collect these and call them Stone Crabs, placing them on their desks, saying they have the power to brighten the eyes. There are also "Stone Shrimp" which resemble shrimp and come from the seaside, and "Stone Fish" which resemble fish and come from Xiangshan County. <gloss>attached</gloss> These are not used as medicine. <gloss>continuation</gloss> The *Supplementary Records* say that in Mei County, Fengxiang Prefecture, there is a "Mountain Fish" found by digging into the earth and breaking through stone; it is said these can be used to ward off bookworms.

<summary>This page describes the medicinal use of Stone Crabs (fossilized crustaceans), primarily for treating eye disorders, poisoning, and obstetric emergencies. It includes geographic accounts of their formation in fine, "cold" mud in regions like Hainan.</summary>

<keywords>Stone Crab, fossil medicine, ophthalmology, lacquer sores, Gu-poisoning, Li Shizhen, Yulin Port, fossilization</keywords>

Applied topically for carbuncles and swellings <note>Yong-zhong: acute inflammatory skin lesions or abscesses</note>. It may also be ground with boiled water and taken orally. It neutralizes toxins from "gold and stone" <note>Jin-shi: refers to heavy metal poisoning resulting from ancient alchemical elixirs containing minerals like arsenic or mercury</note>.

For throat obstruction with swelling and pain: Grind a Stone Crab in water; drink the liquid and also apply it topically to the throat. <gloss>Source: Imperial Benevolence Record (Shengji Lu)</gloss>

# Stone Snake <gloss>Song Dynasty: Illustrated Classic (Jing Tu)</gloss>

### Collected Explanations

<gloss>Su Song states:</gloss> Stone Snakes are produced among the mountain rocks along the shores of the South Sea. Their shape is coiled and winding like a snake, but they lack a head or tail. They are hollow inside and reddish-purple in color; those that coil to the left are considered of superior quality. They also resemble the "cart-wheel snail" <note>original: "Che Luo" (車螺), a type of spiral shell</note>, and it is unknown what creature they were transformed from. Generally, they are of the same category as Stone Crabs, and their medicinal effects are similar.

<gloss>Kou Zongshi states:</gloss> The Stone Snake's color is like the earth found on ancient walls. It is coiled like a silkworm and is about the size of a pear. It is hollow inside, and the two ends are of unequal thickness. I do not believe it belongs to the same category as the Stone Crab. The crab is a transformation of a real crab, but the "snake" is not a real snake. It is very rarely used by physicians today.

<gloss>Li Shizhen states:</gloss> According to Yao Kuan’s *Collected Words from the Western Creek*, there is a stone mountain on the coast of Enzhou. It is said that whenever a crab passes the mountain, it transforms into stone; likewise, if a <insert>snake</insert> passes by, it also turns to stone. I do not know if this account is actually true. If it were, then the Stone Snake would indeed be a transformation of a real snake.

### Flavor and Nature

Salty, neutral, and non-toxic. <note>In traditional pharmacology, "neutral" (Ping) indicates a substance that does not significantly alter the body's heat or cold balance.</note>

### Principal Indications

Neutralizes toxins from "gold and stone" <note>mineral-based alchemical elixirs</note>.
<gloss>Note from Su Song’s medicinal works</gloss>

# Stone Swallow <gloss>Song Dynasty: Kaibao Materia Medica</gloss>

**Explanation of Names:** Stone Mummified Silkworm <gloss>Compendium of Materia Medica</gloss>

**Collected Explanations** <gloss>Ma Zhi states: Stone Swallows are produced beside rocks along the coast. Their shape resembles a silkworm, but they are actually made of stone.</gloss> <note>Despite the name "Swallow," these are fossilized brachiopods—prehistoric marine shellfish—whose shells often look like small birds in flight or segmented larvae.</note>

**Nature and Flavor:** Bitter, hot, and non-toxic. <gloss>The Secrets of Medicine states: They are bitter and hot, but toxic. Dugu Tao states: They can be used to process cinnabar</gloss> <note>original: "Dan Sha" (丹砂). In ancient alchemy, certain minerals were used to "tame" or chemically alter mercury ores.</note>.

**Main Indications:** For incised wounds <note>Injuries caused by metal weapons like swords or knives</note>, it stops bleeding and promotes the growth of new flesh. It is used to break up urinary stones <term>Shi-lin: "Stone strangury," a condition involving painful urination and the passage of stones</term> and blood blockages. When ground into a powder and taken orally, it will cause the stones to be passed out in fragments. <gloss>Kaibao Materia Medica</gloss>

# Stone Beetle <gloss>Compendium of Materia Medica</gloss>

**Collected Explanations** <gloss>Li Shizhen states: Stone Beetles live by the seashore. Their shape and size are exactly like those of the dung beetle; they are likely transformed from them. The dung beetle is commonly known as the "earth beetle."</gloss> <note>This likely refers to fossilized chitons or trilobites, whose segmented shells bear a resemblance to land-dwelling beetles.</note>

**Nature and Flavor:** Sweet, cooling, and non-toxic.

Mainly used to treat strangury <note>A condition involving painful or difficult urination, often associated with stones or infection</note> and blood diseases. Grind with water to take. —<term>Shizhen</term> <note>Referring to Li Shizhen, the lead author of the Compendium.</note>

# Snake Yellow
->Materia Medica of the Tang Dynasty<-

### Collected Explanations
<term>Gong</term> <note>Su Gong, a prominent 7th-century Tang Dynasty pharmacologist.</note> states: Snake Yellow is produced in the Lingnan region <note>A historical area covering modern-day Guangdong and Guangxi provinces</note> and is found inside the bellies of snakes. It is round and heavy, resembling tin, and is yellow-black in color.
<column-break/>
The *Qing Miscellaneous Records* states: Snake Yellow is mostly reddish. Some are vomited out by snakes, and people in the wilderness occasionally find them.
<column-break/>
<term>Song</term> <note>Su Song, a Song Dynasty scientist and author of the *Illustrated Materia Medica*.</note> states: Nowadays, it is also found in Yuezhou and Xinzhou. Physicians today say that this substance is actually a lump of earth that a snake holds in its mouth while hibernating in winter. When the snake emerges in spring, it spits it out. These objects are as large as pellets <note>Small balls used as ammunition for slings or crossbows</note> and as hard as stone. The exterior is "Snake Yellow," while the interior is black. They are collected in the second lunar month. This account differs from the older descriptions; it is not yet known which is correct.
<column-break/>
<term>Shizhen</term> states: Snake Yellow forms inside the belly, much like how Ox Bezoar <note>Calculus Bovis: gallstones found in cattle, used as a potent medicine</note> is produced. Because it is so rare and difficult to obtain, people nowadays use "Snake-containing Stone" <term>Shehanshi</term> as a substitute, simply because both are associated with snakes. In Pingnan County, Guangxi, there are "snake maps" <note>Possibly referring to local guides or geographical indicators of snake dens</note>. In the ninth lunar month, locals dig down seven or eight feet into the earth to find the Snake Yellow. Large ones are like chicken eggs, and small ones are like pellets. Their color is purple. The *Manual of the Geng and Xin* <term>Gengxin Yuce</term> <note>An important alchemical text</note> says: Snake-containing Stone is a distinct type of stone. It claims that when a snake enters hibernation, it takes a lump of earth in its mouth, which transforms into a yellow stone when it wakes. This is not an accurate statement. People have dug up snake dens to search for them and found no evidence for this theory.
<column-break/>

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal substance "Snake Yellow" (Shehuang), debating whether it is a bezoar formed inside a snake's body or a mineral found in hibernation dens. It is primarily used to treat urinary disorders and blood-related ailments.</summary>
<keywords>Snake Yellow, Shehuang, Su Gong, Su Song, Li Shizhen, Bezoar, Lingnan, Strangury, Snake-containing Stone, Traditional Chinese Medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description and medicinal application of Snake Yellow (Limonite nodules) within the mineral section of the Compendium of Materia Medica. It follows the "Collected Explanations" provided on the previous page regarding its origins in snake dens.</meta>

### Preparation
<gloss>Daming <note>Refers to the Song Dynasty author of the *Materia Medica of Ranhua*</note> says: To use in medicine, heat the stone until it is red-hot, then quench it in vinegar. Repeat this fire-and-vinegar process three to four times. Grind it into a fine powder and use the water-levigation method <note>A process of grinding minerals in water to separate the finest medicinal dust from coarse grit</note> to prepare it for use.</gloss>

<column-break/>

**Properties:** Cold [in nature], non-toxic.

<column-break/>

**Main Indications:** Treats chest and heart pain, sudden possession or stroke <note>Traditional term *zhuwu*, referring to sudden, acute illnesses often attributed to "evil influences"</note>, urinary stones, childhood convulsions and epilepsy, and difficult labor in women. Boil in water and grind for use.

<column-break/>

**Administration:** Drink the liquid. <gloss>Original source: Tang Dynasty Materia Medica</gloss>. Calms the heart. <gloss>Source: Daming</gloss>. Grind into a liquid to apply topically to swollen toxins and abscesses. <gloss>Source: Shizhen <note>Li Shizhen, the compiler of this work</note></gloss>

<column-break/>

### Appended Prescriptions <gloss>New additions</gloss>
**Hidden wind and epileptic disorders:** <gloss>For cases where the patient suddenly collapses to the ground, loses consciousness, and remains so for a long time before waking. Use Snake Yellow: calcine in fire and quench in vinegar seven times. Grind into a fine powder. For each dose, mix with wine and drink.</gloss>

<column-break/>

<gloss>Take two *qian* <note>A historical weight unit, approximately 3.7 grams</note>; the condition is cured after several doses.</gloss> <gloss>Source: Master Wei’s *Effective Formulas*</gloss>. **Convulsions, epilepsy, and childhood wasting diseases:** <gloss>The "Divine Hole Pill" treats acute convulsions, epilepsy, upward-staring eyes, fever from malnutrition, and similar syndromes.</gloss>

<column-break/>

Take four *liang* <note>Approximately 150 grams</note> of purple Snake Yellow and calcine it. You will also need one pig’s heart (weighing about two *liang*). If the heart is small, encase it in a clay vessel. Add one *liang* of lead powder, half a *liang* of cinnabar, and one *qian* of musk. Grind all into a powder and mix with glutinous rice paste.

<column-break/>

Form into pills the size of a gorgon nut. Dry them on a lacquered tray. Upon inspection, each pill will have a tiny hole—hence the name "Divine Hole Pill." For convulsions, dissolve one small pill in mint wine to induce immediate awakening. For fever related to malnutrition, dissolve in cold water. <gloss>Source: *Formulas from the Spiritual Garden*</gloss>

<vocab>蛇黃 (Shehuang / Snake Yellow), 修治 (Xiuzhi / Preparation), 醋淬 (Cu Cui / Vinegar Quenching), 驚癇 (Jingxian / Convulsions and Epilepsy), 神穴丹 (Shenxue Dan / Divine Hole Pill), 附方 (Fufang / Appended Prescriptions), 靈苑方 (Lingyuan Fang / Formulas from the Spiritual Garden), 危氏得效方 (Wei Shi Dexiao Fang / Master Wei’s Effective Formulas)</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the specific medicinal recipes for Snake-Enclosing Stone (Limonite) before transitioning to a new entry regarding "Thunderbolt Anvils," which historical observers often identified as ancient stone tools or meteorites found after storms.</meta>

<margin>From the *Comprehensive Book for the Protection of Children*</margin>
### Weakness of the Neck
For cases caused by "wind-depletion" <note>A traditional diagnosis referring to a state of bodily exhaustion that allows environmental "wind" to cause neurological or muscular symptoms</note>: Take one piece of Snake-Enclosing Stone, heat it until red-hot and quench it in vinegar; repeat this seven times. Grind it into a powder with equal parts Turmeric. Add a small amount of musk and mix with white rice to form pills the size of a longan fruit. For each dose, dissolve one pill in peppermint soup and administer once daily.

<column-break/>

<margin>From *Prescriptions for Extracting the Mysterious*</margin>
### Miasmic and Ghostly Malaria
For malaria triggered by eating: Take one *liang* <note>Approximately 37 grams</note> of Snake-Enclosing Stone powder and grind it evenly. Place it inside a "Water-Fire Tripod" <note>A specialized alchemical furnace or double-vessel setup</note>, cover it with a saucer, and seal the edges with "Six-One Mud" <note>A traditional alchemical sealant made of various earths and minerals</note>. Heat it until the medicine rises to the saucer. Scrape the powder from the saucer and mix with rice paste to form pills the size of mung beans. Coat the pills in Realgar. For each dose, take one pill with water ground with black beans at the fifth watch <note>Around 3:00 AM to 5:00 AM</note>.

<column-break/>

<margin>From *Prescriptions for Universal Relief*</margin>
### Incessant Bloody Dysentery
Take two Snake-Enclosing Stones, fire-calcine them and quench them in vinegar. Grind into a powder. For each dose, take three *qian* <note>Approx. 11 grams</note> with rice water.

<column-break/>

<margin>From *Prescriptions for Universal Relief*</margin>
### Intestinal Wind with Bleeding
For rectal prolapse and blood in the stool: Take two pieces of Snake-Enclosing Stone (also called "Snake Yellow"), fire-calcine and vinegar-quench them seven times. Grind into a fine powder. For each dose, take three *qian* with aged rice water.

<column-break/>

<margin>From the *Supplement*</margin>
# Thunderbolt Anvil
<note>Historically, these objects were often Neolithic stone axes or adzes unearthed by erosion after heavy rain, which people believed were cast down by lightning.</note>

<column-break/>

### Explanation of Names
Thunder Wedge
<margin>Shizhen says: In older texts, this was mistakenly written as "needle" or "shavings."</margin>

<column-break/>

### Collected Explanations
Zangzhen <note>Chen Zangzhen, author of the *Supplement to the Materia Medica* (739 AD)</note> says: This object is found by waiting for a place where lightning has struck and digging three feet into the earth. Their shapes are not uniform: some resemble axes or knives, some look like woodcarving tools, and some have two holes. One tradition says they fall from the heavens during storms.

<summary>This page concludes the medicinal applications of Snake-Enclosing Stone for conditions like pediatric neck weakness and malaria, then introduces the "Thunderbolt Anvil" (likely ancient stone tools), describing their purported celestial origins.</summary>
<keywords>Snake-Enclosing Stone, limonite, pediatric medicine, malaria, dysentery, Thunderbolt Anvil, stone axes, Li Shizhen, Chen Zangzhen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the medicinal properties of "Snake-Enclosing Stones" (Limonite). It marks the beginning of a new entry concerning "Thunder Stones" (meteorites or ancient stone tools discovered after storms), detailing their appearance, mythological origins, and historical accounts of their discovery.</meta>

# Volume Ten
## Section on Metals and Stones

<margin>Zhou Ling of Wujiang says:</margin>

These are found in the mountains and marshes of Bingzhou and Hedong <note>Historical regions in northern China, covering parts of modern Shanxi province</note> after a thunderstorm. Most resemble axes and are bluish-black with mottled patterns; they are as hard as jade. Some suggest these are stones crafted by humans and offered to the Heavenly Court, though the truth of this is unknown.

**Shizhen notes:** According to the *Books of Thunder*, "Thunderbolt Axes" are like axes made of bronze or iron. "Thunderbolt Wedges" resemble wedges but are made of stone and are purplish-black. "Thunderbolt Hammers" weigh several *jin* <note>A traditional unit of weight; at the time, roughly 600 grams</note>, and "Thunderbolt Drills" are over a foot long—both appearing as if made of bronze or iron. These are tools used by the Gods of Thunder to strike and grind objects. "Thunderbolt Rings" resemble jade rings and are said to be ornaments dropped by the Thunder Gods. "Thunderbolt Pearls" are held in the mouths of Divine Dragons and left behind; they are said to fill a room with light at night.

Furthermore, the *Treatise on Curiosities* <note>Original: *Bowu Zhi*</note> states that people often encounter small stone axes shaped like wedges, known as "Thunderbolt Axes" or "Thunderbolt Wedges." The *Records from Within the Mystery* <note>Original: *Xuanzhong Ji*</note> claims that in a country west of the "Royal Gate," there is a temple on a mountain where the people provide drills for the use of the Thunder Gods every year. This, however, is erroneous talk.

While thunder is the result of the friction and collision of the two *qi* <note>The fundamental life force or energy</note> of **Yin** and **Yang**, there are indeed divine beings who govern it. Just as all things are stirred and awakened from hibernation, these axes, drills, wedges, and hammers are physical objects. If one says that "images formed in heaven take shape on the earth"—just as stars fall to become meteorites—then the rains of gold, stones, grain, hair, blood, and other strange phenomena are also physical forms manifested on earth. These are surely caused by divine beings within the Great Void.

During the Chen Dynasty, a "Thunderbolt Hammer" weighing nine *jin* was found in Lingnan. In the Song Dynasty, Shen Kuo <note>1031–1095, a famous polymath and scientist of the Song Dynasty</note> discovered a "Thunderbolt Wedge" beneath a tree that had been struck by lightning; it resembled an axe but lacked a hole for a handle. The ways of ghosts and gods are profound and subtle; they truly cannot be fully investigated.

<summary>This page discusses the nature and origins of "Thunder Stones," interpreting them as divine tools or celestial manifestations found after storms. Li Shizhen provides a rationalized view, comparing them to meteorites while acknowledging the supernatural folklore surrounding them.</summary>
<keywords>Thunder Stones, Thunderbolt Axes, Li Shizhen, Bencao Gangmu, Shen Kuo, meteorites, stone tools, folklore, Yin and Yang</keywords>

**Mainly treats:** It is non-toxic. Primarily used for severe fright, loss of mind, trance-like states where one does not recognize others, and stone strangury <note>A traditional term for painful urination caused by urinary stones</note>. Grind into a liquid for consumption.

<column-break/>

It can also be boiled and consumed, or made into a pillow to eliminate demonic dreams and bad omens. <gloss>Zangqi <note>Referring to the Tang dynasty physician Chen Zangqi</note></gloss> says: Scraping it into a powder and consuming it treats wasting diseases <note>Likely tuberculosis</note> and kills "labor worms" <note>Parasites associated with chronic consumption</note>.

<column-break/>

It expels venomous *gu* poisons <note>Ancient Chinese "hex-toxins" or venom-based magic</note> and stops diarrhea. If placed within trunks or chests, it prevents the growth of moths and boring insects. All "thunder objects," when worn as amulets, bring peace...

<column-break/>

...settle the spirit, calm the will, and treat illnesses caused by frightening evil spirits. <gloss>Shizhen citing the *Books of Thunder*</gloss>

# Thunder Ink <gloss>From the *Compendium*</gloss>

<column-break/>

## Collected Explanations
<gloss>Shizhen notes: According to the *Books of Thunder*, whenever lightning strikes wood or stone, it is said that "thunder writing" penetrates two or three inches into the material. Some say this ink is made of "stone grease" from Mount Penglei. In Leizhou <note>Literally "Thunder Prefecture," a region in modern Guangdong known for frequent storms</note>, whenever a heavy thunderstorm breaks out, these objects fly down like sand or stones. The large ones are like lumps, and the small ones are like grease. They are as hard as stone, black and glossy as a polished mirror, and very heavy. Liu Xun’s *Records of the Lands Beyond the Peaks* says: After sudden rains in Leizhou, people find stones in the fields resembling wrought iron, called "Lord of Thunder's Ink." When struck, they produce a clear, metallic ringing sound; they are lustrous and lovely. Furthermore, Li Zhao’s *Supplement to the National History* says...</gloss>

<meta>This page transitions from the general folklore surrounding "thunder objects" (such as meteorites or fossils) to the specific clinical applications of these substances in traditional medicine. It concludes a series of observations on the physical nature of thunder before formally beginning the tenth volume of the Compendium.</meta>

<header>Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume 10</header>

<meta>A large red imperial seal "Treasure for the Imperial Inspection of the Qianlong Emperor" is stamped across the top of the central columns.</meta>

Leizhou <note>Literally "Thunder Prefecture," a region on the southern coast of China known for frequent and intense storms</note> has much thunder. In autumn, [the thunder] enters a state of dormancy <term>fuzhe</term> <note>A term usually describing hibernating animals or insects</note>, taking a shape like that of a human. People dig these up and eat them. From this, we can see that "thunder" does indeed manifest as a physical object.

For various illnesses, grind [the thunder object] and take it with a decoction of peach-wood charms <note>Historical wooden plaques or talismans used to ward off evil spirits; here the wood is boiled to make a medicinal tea</note>, and the patient will find relief at once. <gloss>Shizhen <note>The author, Li Shizhen</note></gloss>

### Main Indications: Pediatric Convulsions, Epilepsy, and Demonic Possession
